p p -Adic periods and Selmer scheme images Thanks: The second author’s work on this article was supported by ISF grants 726/17 and 621/21.
David Corwin and Ishai Dan-Cohen

Abstract
. The Chabauty–Kim method was developed with the aim of approaching effective Faltings’, the problem of explicitly determining the finite set of rational points on a hyperbolic curve. This method has seen success with the more particular Quadratic Chabauty method, but this method still applies only to certain curves. Previous applications of Chabauty–Kim beyond the quadratic level, as pursued by the authors, by S. Wewers, and by others, use mixed Tate motives and the p p -adic period map of Chatzistamatiou-Ünver to approach the particular hyperbolic curve ℙ 1 ∖ { 0 , 1 , ∞ } \mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\} . The main purpose of this article is to lay foundations for extending the above approach to more general hyperbolic curves, in particular by defining an analogous p p -adic period map for more general categories of motives and their non-conjectural cousins such as systems of realizations and p p -adic Galois representations. We use this to describe a general setup for non-abelian Chabauty for an arbitrary hyperbolic curve. Our period map also connects the study of p p -adic iterated integrals with Goncharov’s theory of motivic iterated integrals, and allows us to investigate Goncharov’s conjectures from a p p -adic point of view. In particular, it suggests the possibility of evaluating syntomic regulators by writing elements of K K -theory in terms of motivic iterated integrals. Lastly, it forms the basis for a certain generalization of the p p -adic period conjecture of Yamashita for mixed Tate motives well-suited to applications in Chabauty–Kim theory.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
p
-adic periods and multiple polylogarithms

The
p
-adic period map of
[
29
]
from the theory of mixed Tate motives (
[
43
]
) plays a central role in drawing a fruitful connection between two areas of investigation. One is the study of the structure of mixed Tate motivic Galois groups as pursued by Goncharov in his “beyond standard” motivic conjectures (
[
68
]
,
[
66
]
,
[
67
]
,
[
65
]
, etc.) as well as by Brown
[
24
,
23
]
and others. A one-sentence summary of this multi-faceted subject might go as follows: one defines certain special functions
f\in\mathcal{O}(G)
on various motivic Galois groups
G
(
motivic multiple polylogarithms
,
unipotent multiple polylogarithms
) and attempts to understand which portions of
\mathcal{O}(G)
are spanned by which families of special functions.

The other is the study of
p
-adic multiple polylogarithms and
p
-adic multiple zeta values (see, for instance,
[
57
,
58
,
14
,
56
,
59
,
111
,
81
,
60
]
), where one of the main interests is in understanding relations between rational values of multiple polylogarithms with a view towards Grothendieck’s philosophy, according to which all relations should
come from geometry
. Indeed, Yamashita has conjectured a
p
-adic mixed-Tate analog of the Grothendieck period conjecture (
[
3
, Conjecture 4.1]
), as follows. There’s a ring homomorphism

\mathrm{per}_{p}:\mathcal{O}(G)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}(U)\to{\mathbb{Q}_{p}},

dual to the period point
\eta
of
[
29
]
defined on the quotient Hopf algebra associated to the prounipotent radical
U
of
G
, which sends motivic multiple polylogarithms to
p
-adic multiple polylogarithms. Yamashita conjectures (
[
115
, Conjecture 4]
1
1

1

Technically, Yamashita uses the period point
\varphi_{\mathfrak{p}}=F_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}\tau(q)^{-1}
due to
[
43
, 5.28]
, which produces Deligne
p
-adic polylogarithms (
[
48
, 19.6]
, c.f.
[
115
, §3.2]
) rather than those of Furusho (
[
57
]
), which are output by
\eta
and therefore also our period point. However,
[
115
, Propositions 3.10, 3.12]
imply that these two versions of the conjecture are equivalent, at least for
N\leq 4
.
) that this is injective for mixed Tate motives over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\mu_{N})
.

1.2.
Mixed Tate Chabauty–Kim

These two areas meet with particular force in the approach to Chabauty–Kim theory in the mixed Tate setting developed by the authors and others. Chabauty–Kim theory (described in detail in
1.4
), also known as non-abelian Chabauty’s Method, is a programme started by Minhyong Kim (
[
83
]
) to
p
-adically bound the number of rational points on an arbitrary hyperbolic curve
X
over a number field.

Kim’s programme has so far found significant applications via the Quadratic Chabauty method of
[
9
,
10
]
, in particular in determining the set of rational points on a previously-inaccessible curve (
[
7
]
). Nonetheless, Quadratic Chabauty, like the original Chabauty–Coleman method that came before it, is limited to hyperbolic curves whose arithmetic and geometric data satisfy certain inequalities. Faltings’ Theorem, on the other hand, applies to all hyperbolic curves. One would therefore like to compute the Chabauty–Kim method for all hyperbolic curves, which conjecturally implies an effective approach to Faltings’ Theorem by
[
8
, Conjecture 3.1]
(c.f. also
[
84
]
).

Work by Dan-Cohen and Wewers in the setting of mixed Tate motives
[
38
,
39
]
, while limited in scope, was particularly successful at going beyond the quadratic level. Subsequent work in this direction includes
[
32
,
35
,
37
,
25
,
19
,
80
]
. The goal of the present article is to lay the foundations for applying this approach to general hyperbolic curves, which necessarily goes beyond mixed Tate motives. We first review previous work in the latter setting.

1.2.1.

Let
X
be the projective line with at least 3 punctures, which are required to be distinct over every closed point of an open subscheme
Z
of
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}
. Such a curve can be viewed as a
non-proper hyperbolic
smooth curve. Siegel’s Theorem says, in a parallel way to Faltings’, that
X(Z)
is finite, and this finiteness was the original testing ground for Kim’s method (
[
82
]
).

The work of Deligne-Goncharov (
[
43
]
) provides a lifting of the rational prounipotent completion of the fundamental group of
X(\mathbb{C})
(at an appropriate base point) to a prounipotent group object
\pi_{1}^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(X)
of the Tannakian category
\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}(Z,{\mathbb{Q}})
of mixed Tate motives over
Z
. We consider finite type quotients
\Pi
of
\pi_{1}^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(X)
. One may regard such a
\Pi
as a unipotent
\mathbb{Q}
-group
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
equipped with an action of the mixed Tate motivic Galois group
G=G^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(Z)
of
Z
at the de Rham fiber functor. Letting
U=U^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(Z)
denote the unipotent radical of
G(Z)
, we have
G/U\simeq{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
. Thus there’s a
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action on the moduli space of cocycles
Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})
, and it is proved in
[
38
, Proposition 5.2]
that
H^{1}(G;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})\simeq Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{{\mathbb{G}_{m}}}
.

By work of
[
29
]
, we have for
p\in Z
a special point
\mathfrak{u}_{p}\in U({\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
corresponding to the homomorphism
\mathrm{per}_{p}
of §
1.1
. The properties of this map show (
[
38
, §4.11]
,
[
32
, §2.4]
) that the map from the global to local Selmer variety in Kim’s theory described in §
1.4
may be identified with evaluation at
\mathfrak{u}_{p}
:

Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{p}}}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}.

In our previous approach to the mixed Tate case, the problem of bounding the image of
X(Z)
in
X({\mathbb{Z}_{p}})
(for some
p\in Z
) is thus reduced to the problem of understanding the (scheme-theoretic) image of
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{p}}
.
Moreover, the abstract structure of
U
as a prounipotent group with
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action is known (it’s free on the rational algebraic
K
-theory of
Z
), and when
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
has trivial
U
-action,
Z^{1}(U;\Pi)^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is readily computable.
2
2

2

Work-in-progress of the two authors and M. Lüdtke computes even when
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
has nontrivial
U
-action.
But
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{p}}
is just the pullback along
\mathfrak{u}_{p}\colon\operatorname{Spec}{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\to U
of the universal cocycle evaluation map (
[
32
, Definition 2.20]
):

Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\times U\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{ev}_{\Pi}}\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\times U.

The period conjecture (
[
115
, Conjecture 4]
) says that
\mathfrak{u}_{p}
is generic, which implies that the image of the former is the pullback of the image of the latter. In particular, letting
I^{\mathrm{mot}}
denote the ideal vanishing on the image of
\mathfrak{ev}_{\Pi}
and
I
the ideal vanishing on the image of
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{p}}
, we have

I^{\mathrm{mot}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(U)}{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\subseteq I;

if the period conjecture holds, then they are equal.

The problem of computing the image of
\mathfrak{ev}_{\Pi}
in terms of free generators for (suitable portions of)
U
is purely geometric and is known as the
geometric step
. The more subtle part is computing coordinates on
U
on which
\operatorname{per}_{p}
may be computed, which ultimately involves some intricate manipulations with motivic iterated integrals, such as in
[
32
, §4.3]
.

Our goal here is to extend the above picture beyond the limited setting of mixed Tate motives, so our initial focus is on aspects that are largely formal.
3
3

3

As the reader will see, non-formal Tannakian considerations do arise.

1.3.
Beyond the mixed Tate setting

For a non-rational hyperbolic curve, one must pass beyond the (Artin-)Tate setting. Here, abelian categories of motives are only conjectural (see §
3.9
and
A
). We are therefore forced to work with systems of realizations if we want our setup to be non-conjectural. Given the central role of
p
-adic Hodge theory in
p
-adic realization, we prefer to use
motivic structures
of Fontaine–Perrin-Riou (
[
53
, §III]
) in §
3.3
-
3.6
, which include the
p
-adic Hodge comparison as part of the data. Nonetheless, our formal results (§
4
-
6
) apply equally to other substitutes for the abelian category of mixed motives, such as appropriate categories of Galois representations.

1.3.1.

For concreteness, let us temporarily focus attention on a special case, namely the category
\mathbf{SM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}},E)
(
3.8.2
,
7.2.2
) of
lisse mixed-
E
-motivic structures over
Z
associated to a non-CM elliptic curve
E/K
with good reduction over an open subscheme
Z\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K}}
. This applies whenever
X
is of
mixed-elliptic type
, for example when
X
is an affine open subscheme of an elliptic curve or a genus
2
curve whose Jacobian is isogeneous to the square of an elliptic curve, such as the curves with LMFDB labels 38416.a.614656.1 and 614656.a.614656.1.

This is, roughly speaking, the Serre subcategory category of
lisse motivic structures over
Z
(
7.2
) generated by
h_{1}(E)
. Its Tannakian Galois group at the fiber functor induced by de Rham realization sits in an exact sequence

1\to U(Z,E)\to G(Z,E)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}\to 1.

The group
U=U(Z,E)
consists of those Tannakian loops which induce the identity on associated graded for the weight filtration. Let
F^{0}U\subset U
(§
4.14
) be the subgroup of automorphisms that preserve the Hodge filtration. For
\mathfrak{p}\in Z
, our main results (§
5.5
) lead to the construction of a special
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-point
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{R}
(resp.
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
) (§
7.3
) of
U
(a “unipotent
p
-adic period loop") which is well-suited to our applications to Chabauty–Kim theory. This point depends on a certain choice, and is well-defined only modulo the left- (resp. right-) action of
F^{0}U
.

Let us focus on
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
. The homomorphism

\mathrm{per}^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}\colon\mathcal{O}\big(U/F^{0}U\big)\to K_{\mathfrak{p}}

associated to
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
should probably not be regarded as
the

p
-adic period map.
4
4

4

Instead, we suggest the terminology
unipotent
p
-adic period map
.
In one of several drawbacks, by its very construction,
\mathrm{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
provides pure motives with no nonzero
p
-adic periods. While in the mixed Tate setting, the only period of a pure motive (
2\pi i
) may reasonably be considered to be “
p
-adically zero”, this appears not to be the case for more general motives. A construction of a candidate for the
p
-adic period map which is better in this regard (although perhaps less suited to Chabauty–Kim) has been carried out concurrently with this work by Ancona–Frăţilă
[
2
]
. See also Dan-Cohen
[
40
]
.

1.3.2.

More generally, in order to apply Chabauty–Kim to an arbitrary curve
X
, we must consider a category

\mathbf{SM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}},J)

of
lisse mixed-
J
-motivic structures over
Z
, where
J
is the Jacobian of
X
, which is the same as above but generated by
h_{1}(J)
. We describe this category in detail in §
3.6
-
3.8
. We have a de Rham fiber functor over
K
giving rise to a motivic Galois group
G(Z,J)
(
7.2.3
) over
K
, sitting in a similar exact sequence

1\to U(Z,J)\to G(Z,J)\to{\mathbb{G}}(J)\to 1,

where
{\mathbb{G}}(J)
is a reductive group closely related to both the Mumford–Tate group and the
p
-adic monodromy group of
J
, and
U=U(Z,J)
is generated (conjecturally freely) by certain
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
groups in
\mathbf{SM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
, which are in turn conjecturally isomorphic to pieces of certain rational algebraic
K
-theory or higher Chow groups of powers of
E
.

Similarly to the case of
E
, for
\mathfrak{p}\in Z
above
p
, we have a homomorphism

\mathrm{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}\colon\mathcal{O}\big(U(Z,J)/F^{0}U(Z,J)\big)\to K_{\mathfrak{p}}.

Remark 1.3.3
.

The reader may be left wondering how to compute the
p
-adic period maps on explicit elements of
\mathcal{O}(U)
. For this, we note that the commutativity of
8.4
(
19
) below allows us to compute the maps on ‘motivic iterated integrals’. Specifically, given
\omega\in\mathcal{O}(\pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})
and
z\in\mathcal{X}(Z)
, we may define a ‘motivic iterated integral’

I^{{\mathrm{mot}}}(\omega;b,z)\coloneqq\kappa_{\Pi}(z)^{\#}(\omega)\in\mathcal{O}(U),

depending on a choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. If
\omega
is left-invariant under
F^{0}
, then

\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}\left(I^{{\mathrm{mot}}}(\omega;b,z)\right)=\int_{b}^{z}\omega

in the sense of Coleman integration.

1.4.
Chabauty–Kim setup

Let
K
be a number field,
Z=\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}
for a finite subset
S\subseteq S_{f}(K)
, and
\mathcal{X}\to Z
a smooth morphism whose generic fiber
X
is a hyperbolic curve (i.e., with non-abelian geometric fundamental group) along with smooth proper compactification
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to Z
such that the complement
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}
is étale over
Z
. We denote by
Y,X^{\prime},X
their generic fibers, respectively.

The theorems of Faltings and Siegel show that
\mathcal{X}(Z)
is always finite. Note that if
X=X^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{X}(Z)=X(K)
, and this is the case of Faltings. The open problem is to compute
\mathcal{X}(Z)
given
\mathcal{X}
.

Fix a place
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)\cap Z
and a finite-dimensional Galois-equivariant quotient
\Pi_{p}
of
\pi_{1}^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\mathrm{un}}(\overline{X})_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
, the
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-prounipotent completion of the profinite group
\pi_{1}^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X})
. Kim’s approach to this problem (
[
83
]
,
[
8
]
) uses a diagram

\textstyle{X(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\Pi_{p}}}
\textstyle{X(Z_{\mathfrak{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\mathfrak{p},\Pi_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{f,S}(K;\Pi_{p})_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi_{p}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\Pi_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\log_{\mathrm{BK}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(F^{0}\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\backslash\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})}

known as Kim’s cutter, where
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\Pi_{p})
.

We refer to the composition

\rm{LocReal}=\rm{LocReal}_{\Pi_{p}}\coloneqq\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi_{p}}

as the
Localization-Realization
map of Chabauty–Kim theory. Computing the
image
of this map is the most important and nontrivial step in using this theory to bound
\mathcal{X}(Z)
.
[
84
, §2]
refers to the computability of this map as “Hypothesis [H]” and shows in the case
K={\mathbb{Q}}
that assuming [H] and the Bloch–Kato conjectures, one may effectively compute a function on
\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})
vanishing on
\mathcal{X}(Z)
.

When
X^{\prime}
has genus
0
, the approach mentioned in §
1.2.1
replaces the
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-scheme
H^{1}_{f,S}(K;\pi_{1}^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\mathrm{un}}(\overline{X})_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}})
by a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-form
H^{1}(\mathbf{MT}(Z,{\mathbb{Q}});\pi_{1}^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(X))
, where
\mathbf{MT}(Z,{\mathbb{Q}})
is the category of mixed Tate motives of
[
43
, 1.6]
, and
\pi_{1}^{\operatorname{\mathbf{MT}}}(X)
is the fundamental group in this category due to
[
43
, 5.2]
. The Localization-Realization map is then quite simply, in the notation of §
1.2.1
, the evaluation map

Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{p}}}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}.

In §
8.2.4
, we define a similar group scheme object
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X)
in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
for an
arbitrary
curve
X
and in
8.3
(
18
) define an alternative version of Kim’s Cutter relative to a finite-dimensional motivic quotient
\Pi
of
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X)
, namely:

(1)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\Pi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\mathfrak{p},\Pi}}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\Pi_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\log_{\mathrm{BK}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}F^{0}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}\backslash(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}.}

In §
6.10
(resp. §
6.13
), we define a more abstract version of
\rm{LocReal}
called ‘
\beta^{R}
’ (resp. ‘
\beta^{L}
’) for any effective group object in a “weight-filtered Tannakian category” (Definition
2.5.9
) with Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor (Definition
2.6.7
). The description of
\log_{\mathrm{BK}}
in
[
8
, 3.4.3]
(c.f.
8.3.4
) implies that this is the same as
\rm{LocReal}
of the previous paragraph.

We thus set out to study the (scheme-theoretic) image
\operatorname{\overline{Im}}\rm{LocReal}
of the associated map of schemes over
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

\mathrm{LocReal}\colon H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)\times_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\operatorname{Spec}{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}F^{0}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}\backslash(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}

(
6.13
).

Our main result is as follows:

Theorem 1.1
.

The (Weil restriction of the) localization-realization map of Chabauty–Kim theory may be identified with the evaluation map:

Z^{1}(U(Z,J);\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{{\mathbb{G}}(J)}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}F^{0}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}\backslash(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}.

See Theorems
6.11
and
6.14
and §
8.4
for precise statements.

1.5.
Remarks on Chabauty–Kim computations

In order to compute the image of the localization-realization map, one needs to know the structure of
U(Z,J)
. This group is generated (conjecturally freely) by the
\operatorname{Ext}
groups
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),V)
, as
V
ranges over irreducible representations of
{\mathbb{G}}(J)
(equivalently, irreducible objects of
\mathbf{SM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
).

Such groups receive maps from certain rationalized algebraic
K
-theory groups (§
3.7
-(
6
),(
9
)), which are generally believed to be isomorphisms. The Beilinson conjectures imply that the dimensions of most of these groups are equal to dimensions of certain absolute Hodge cohomology groups, whose dimensions may be computed in terms of the Hodge structure on
V_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
.

But there is also a natural map

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),V)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\to H^{1}_{f,S}(G_{K};V_{p}),

which
[
53
, III.4.1.5]
conjectures to be an isomorphism (c.f. Conjecture
3.6.9
). Arithmetic duality theorems allow one to compute their dimensions conditionally on the vanishing of Bloch–Kato Selmer groups in non-negative weight, as carried out in
[
33
, §5]
and described in more generality in
[
20
, §2.7]
.

In fact, one may circumvent
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),V)
entirely and work directly with the groups
H^{1}_{f,S}(G_{K};V_{p})
by using
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-Tannakian categories of
p
-adic Galois representations (
3.1
). Our axiomatic setup applies equally in this setting. This has the disadvantage that one loses the potentially significant Zariski closure of the period loop (Conjecture
7.3.2
). On the other hand, it has the advantage that one may sometimes prove the desired vanishing of Bloch–Kato Selmer groups using Iwasawa theory. This direction is pursued in the parallel work
[
34
,
33
]
by the first author.

In particular,
[
33
, §2.4]
, explains how one may use Kato’s Iwasawa Main Conjecture to prove
H^{1}_{f}(G_{{\mathbb{Q}}};V_{p}(E)(1))=0
for a non-CM elliptic curve
E
with ordinary reduction at
p
. This in turn allows one to unconditionally apply the Chabauty–Kim method to the case of
{\mathbb{Z}}[1/\ell]
-points on a punctured elliptic curve of rank
1
, in which Quadratic Chabauty does not apply. M. Lüdtke and the first author have already computed Chabauty–Kim functions in this case. Note that all of that work depends crucially on the main theorem of the present article, which already demonstrates its significance.

1.6.
Proof sketch

While
[
29
]
works only with filtered
\varphi
-modules, we work with global objects (Galois representations, motives, or systems of realizations), for the following reason.
Even if
Y\to\operatorname{Spec}{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
is a smooth and proper morphism, the admissible filtered
\phi
-modules
H^{i}_{\mathrm{rig}}(Y_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
may fail to be semisimple. Thus, beyond the mixed Tate setting, we don’t have a useful weight-filtered category (see the next paragraph) of admissible filtered
\phi
-modules. For this reason, constructions that center on the category of mixed Tate filtered
\phi
-modules don’t generalize readily. Instead, we construct our period loop
\mathfrak{u}_{p}
while working directly with an appropriate global Tannakian category (systems of realizations,
p
-adic Galois representations, or motives, as described in §
3
).

For example, suppose we work with a subcategory
\mathcal{T}
(such as
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
in
3.8.2
) of the category of
motivic structures
(§
3.6
). In our axiomatic setup, an important feature of
\mathcal{T}
is that it is
weight-filtered
(
2.5.9
), which implies that the induced action on associated graded is always semisimple. De Rham realization

\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
provides a
K
-rational fiber functor which is
Hodge filtered
(
2.6.1
); moreover, it is isomorphic over
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
to a fiber functor
\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
over
K_{\mathfrak{p},0}
that is
Frobenius-equivariant
(
2.6.6
), assuming
\mathfrak{p}\in Z
. In this setting, our main theorems are as follows:

Theorem

(
4.18
below)
.

Tannakian paths
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\colon(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W})\to\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
that preserve Hodge filtrations form a trivial (in particular, nonempty!) torsor under
F^{0}U
.

We refer to such paths as
arithmetic Hodge paths
.

Theorem

(
5.1
below)
.

There exists a unique Tannakian path

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}\colon(\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W})\to\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}

which respects Frobenii.

We refer to the base-change
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}
of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
to
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
as the
arithmetic crystalline path
. We define our (left) period loop by (§
5.5
)

\mathfrak{u}_{p}^{L}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}

for any arithmetic Hodge path
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
. Our proof of Theorem
4.18
builds on P. Ziegler’s study of filtered fiber functors in
[
116
]
,
5
5

5

Similar results have also appeared in work of Hain-Matsumoto (
[
72
, Lemma 3.1]
) and Hain (
[
73
,
74
]
). See the paragraph following Theorem
4.18
.

while our proof of Theorem
5.1
is based on Besser’s proof in
[
17
]
of the uniqueness and existence of
geometric
crystalline paths.

In §
6
, we prove the abstract form of Theorem
1.1
. One subtle point in defining the diagram in this abstract setting is to define a Hodge filtration on torsors over an arbitrary
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
-algebra
R
. For this, we prove a form of Tannaka duality for the base-change of a Tannakian category to an arbitrary base ring (Theorem
B.5.8
), which we then use in §
6.5
to base-change an arbitrary filtered fiber functor, generalizing a result of
[
117
]
.

Most of §
7
spells out the concrete consequences of the abstract results of §
6
in arithmetic settings. Then §
8
sets up the non-abstract Chabauty–Kim diagram. A key technical point is the construction in §
8.1
–
8.2
of the motivic fundamental group and path torsors in the category of motivic structures defined in §
3.6
for a curve over a number field.

1.7.
Acknowledgements

We wish to thank Giuseppe Ancona, Richard Hain, Owen Patashnick, Paul Ziegler, Joseph Ayoub, and Clément Dupont for helpful conversations and email exchanges. We thank Amnon Besser, Benjamin Moore, and Martin Lüdtke for their support and encouragement.

The first author thanks Ben Moonen for mentioning Jannsen’s mixed motives for absolute Hodge cycles.

1.8.
Notations and Conventions

When we refer to the
intersection
of two full subcategories of a larger category, we mean the full subcategory on the class of objects that belong to each subcategory. For a union of a filtered collection of subcategories, we similarly mean the full subcategory on all objects contained in at least one of these subcategories. In a given monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathbbm{1}=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{C}}
denotes a unit object. If
\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal category, we let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rig}}
denote the full subcategory of dualizable objects of
\mathcal{C}
. For an object of a category consisting of modules with extra data (filtration, grading, Frobenius, etc.), we let
\mathrm{forg}
denote the functor to the underlying category of modules (usually vector spaces). All functors between additive categories are assumed additive unless otherwise stated. As explained in
B.1.1
, the term ‘tensor category’ is always specified as ‘
X
tensor category’ for
\emptyset\neq X\subseteq\{\mathrm{additive},\mathrm{abelian},k-\mathrm{linear}\}
; a ‘tensor functor’ refers to an additive (and linear if relevant) symmetric monoidal functor.

We use the concept of Tannakian category throughout, which is reviewed in Appendix
B
.

An
algebraic group
refers to a finite-type group scheme over a field, while a
pro-algebraic group
refers to a group scheme that is an inverse limit of algebraic groups.

For a field extension
k^{\prime}/k
, we let
\Ex_{k}^{k^{\prime}}
the extension of scalars functor from
k
to
k^{\prime}
(for vector spaces, algebras, schemes, Tannakian categories, etc.).

If
V
is a vector space, we denote the associated free pronilpotent Lie algebra by
\mathfrak{n}(V)
, the associated prounipotent group by
U(V)
, the completed universal enveloping algebra by
\mathcal{U}(V)
and the coordinate ring by
A(V)
. If
V
is a representation of an algebraic group
\mathbb{G}
, then there’s an induced action on all of the above.

For a field
K
, we let
G_{K}
denote the absolute Galois group of
K
(for some implicit choice of separable closure). If
K
is a number field, we let
S_{\infty}(K)
denote the set of infinite places of
K
,
S_{f}(K)
the set of finite places of
K
, and
S(K)\coloneqq S_{\infty}(K)\cup S_{f}(K)
. For a subset
S\subseteq S(K)
, let
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
denote the ring of
S
-integers in
K
, i.e. the set of elements of the form
\frac{\alpha}{\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
(\beta)
supported in
S
. For a prime number
\ell
, we let
S_{\ell}(K)
denote the subset of
S_{f}(K)
above
\ell
. For
v\in S(K)
, we let
K_{v}
denote the completion of
K
at
v
,
G_{v}
the absolute Galois group of
K_{v}
(note that the embedding
G_{v}\hookrightarrow G_{K}
is defined only up to conjugation by
G_{K}
), and (if
v\in S_{f}(K)
)
\mathcal{O}_{K,v}
the Zariski local ring,
{\mathcal{O}_{v}}
the (completed) integer ring, and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{v}
its maximal ideal. If
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)
, we let
K_{\mathfrak{p},0}
denote the maximal unramified extension of
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
in
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
. For a scheme
X
over
K
, we let
X_{v}\coloneqq\Ex_{K}^{K_{v}}X
and
\overline{X}\coloneqq\Ex_{K}^{\overline{K}}X
.

2.
Weight-Filtered Tannakian Categories

2.1.
Filtered objects in Abelian categories

We recall some basic notions about filtered objects in additive and (pre-)abelian categories. Some of this can be found (in the abelian case) in
[
107
,
Tag 0120
]
.

2.1.1.
Notation

If
\{M_{i}\}_{I}
is a collection of subobjects of an object
M
of a pre-abelian
6
6

6

is additive with all finite (co)limits, hence has (co)kernels, hence (co)images
category
\mathcal{A}
for which
\oplus_{i\in I}M_{i}
is defined (e.g., if
I
is finite or
\mathcal{A}
has all coproducts of size
|I|
), we denote by
\sum_{i\in I}M_{i}
the image of
\oplus_{i\in I}M_{i}\to M
; it is the minimal subobject of
M
containing
M_{i}
for all
i
.

2.1.2.
Definition

For an additive
7
7

7

The only non-abelian additive
\mathcal{A}
we consider are categories of vector bundles (
\FilLF
in
4.3.3
) and categories of filtered objects (
\operatorname{Fil}^{W}_{F}\LF
in
4.11
). The former can be embedded in the abelian category of quasi-coherent sheaves, and the latter is generally pre-abelian.
category
\mathcal{A}
, we let
\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
denote the additive category of tuples
\mathcal{M}
consisting of an object
M
of
\mathcal{A}
and a decreasing
8
8

8

but see
2.1.12
filtration
\{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
M
.

For
M,N\in\mathcal{A}
underlying
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}\in\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
,
\homo_{\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})
is the set of
f\in\homo_{\mathcal{A}}(M,N)
for which
f(\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M})\subseteq\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

2.1.3.

If
\mathcal{A}
is abelian, then
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
is quasi-abelian (
[
103
, Definition 1.1.3]
) by
[
98
, Theorem 3.9]
and is therefore pre-abelian.

2.1.4.
Definition

For
\mathcal{M}
consisting of
M\in\mathcal{A}
and
\{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, we have functors

\mathrm{forg}=\mathrm{forg}_{\mathrm{fil}}\colon\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A},

\mathrm{fil}^{n}\colon\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A},

and if
\mathcal{A}
is pre-abelian,

\operatorname{gr}^{n}\colon\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}

for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
defined by
\mathrm{forg}(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq M
,
\mathrm{fil}^{n}(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}
, and
\operatorname{gr}^{n}(\mathcal{M})=\operatorname{gr}^{n}\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}/\mathrm{fil}^{n+1}\mathcal{M}
.

2.1.5.
Definition

A filtration
\{\mathrm{fil}^{n}M\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
on an object
M\in\mathcal{A}
is said to be
separated
if the only subobject contained in all
\operatorname{fil}^{n}M
is
0
and
exhaustive
if the only subobject containing all
\operatorname{fil}^{n}M
is
M
. It is
finite
if
\exists\,n_{1},n_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
\mathrm{fil}^{n_{1}}M=0
and
\mathrm{fil}^{n_{2}}M=M
.

We denote by
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}\mathcal{A}
) the subcategory of
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
with finite (resp. separated and exhaustive) filtration. If
\mathcal{A}
is Artinian and Noetherian (e.g., the category of finitely-generated modules over an Artinian ring such as a field), then
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}(\mathcal{A})
.

2.1.6.

If
\mathcal{A}
is a (symmetric) pre-abelian tensor category, we define a (symmetric) additive tensor structure on
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})
in general and on
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
if
\mathcal{A}
has countable coproducts, for which

\mathrm{fil}^{n}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N})=\sum_{a+b=n}\mathrm{fil}^{a}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathrm{fil}^{b}\mathcal{N}

2.1.7.

For a field
k
, we let
\Vect_{k}
denote the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over
k
. We let
\FilVect_{k}
denote
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\Vect_{k})=\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}(\Vect_{k})
.

2.1.8.
Convention

Whenever discussing exactness or strictness in
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
or
\Gr(\mathcal{A})
, we assume that
\mathcal{A}
has notions of image and exact sequence.

2.1.9.
Definition

A morphism
f\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
of filtered objects is
strict
or
admissible
if
\operatorname{Im}({\left.\kern-1.2ptf\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}}})=\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}\cap\operatorname{Im}{f}\subseteq N
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. A sequence
\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{N}
is
exact
if it is exact as a sequence in
\mathcal{A}
and
f
is strict. In particular,
\mathrm{forg}
(
2.1.4
) is exact.

2.1.10.
Remark

That
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
is quasi-abelian when
\mathcal{A}
is abelian (
2.1.3
) implies that
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
forms an
exact category
(
[
27
, Definition 2.1]
).

2.1.11.

A sequence
0\to\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{N}\to 0
in
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
is short exact iff it is short exact in
\mathcal{A}
, and
f,g
are both strict.

2.1.12.

Definition
2.1.2
was stated in terms of decreasing filtrations, such as the Hodge filtrations. All of the above results and definitions apply equally to increasing filtrations by the rule that an increasing filtration is a decreasing filtration with negated indices.

2.2.
Categories of graded objects

For an additive category
\mathcal{A}
, we let
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{A})
denote the category of functors
\mathcal{M}
from the discrete category
{\mathbb{Z}}
into
\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{i}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}(i)
.

2.2.1.

If
\mathcal{A}
is abelian, then so is
\Gr(\mathcal{A})
.

2.2.2.

A graded object
\mathcal{M}
is
finite
if
\mathcal{M}_{i}=0
for all but finitely many
i
, and the category of such
\mathcal{M}
is denoted
\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})
.

2.2.3.

If
\mathcal{A}
admits countable coproducts (e.g., quasicoherent sheaves, countably-generated modules), we have a functor

\mathrm{forg}=\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\colon\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}

given by
\mathrm{forg}(\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{i}
. More generally, for arbitrary additive
\mathcal{A}
(e.g., coherent sheaves or vector bundles), we still have

\mathrm{forg}=\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\colon\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A},

defined in the same way. We sometimes think of
\mathcal{M}\in\Gr(\mathcal{A})
as
\mathrm{forg}(\mathcal{M})
along with the collection of subobjects
\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

2.2.4.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an additive tensor category that admits countable coproducts, we have an additive tensor structure on
\mathrm{Gr}(\mathcal{A})
for which

(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N})_{n}=\bigoplus_{a+b=n}\mathcal{M}_{a}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{b}.

Without the assumption of countable coproducts, the same formula defines an additive tensor structure on
\mathrm{Gr}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})
.

In either case,
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}
is monoidal.

2.2.5.

When
\mathcal{A}
is pre-abelian, we have a tensor functor

\operatorname{gr}\colon\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\Gr(\mathcal{A})

defined by
\operatorname{gr}(\mathcal{M})_{i}=\operatorname{gr}^{i}\mathcal{M}
. When
\mathcal{A}
admits countable coproducts (resp. when restricting to finite gradings) we have a tensor functor

\operatorname{fil}\colon\Gr(\mathcal{A})\to\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})

(resp.

\operatorname{fil}\colon\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A})\to\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{A}))

sending
\mathcal{M}\in\Gr(\mathcal{A})
to the filtered object defined by
M\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{M})
and
\operatorname{fil}^{i}M\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j\geq i}\mathcal{M}_{j}
.
9
9

9

Or
j\leq i
when considering increasing filtrations, c.f.
2.1.12
.

2.2.6.

There are canonical isomorphisms of tensor functors

\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{fil}}\circ\operatorname{fil}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}

\operatorname{gr}\circ\operatorname{fil}\xrightarrow{\sim}\id.

2.2.7.

A sequence
\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is exact if
\mathcal{L}_{i}\to\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{N}_{i}
is exact for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The functors
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}
,
\operatorname{fil}
, and
\operatorname{gr}
are all exact, the latter by the following lemma:

Lemma 2.3
.

Consider a sequence
\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{N}
in
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})
for
\mathcal{A}
abelian. Assuming either the underlying sequence
L\to M\to N
is exact in
\mathcal{A}
or that
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
are exhaustive, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{N}
is exact

(2)

\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{fil}^{n}f}\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{fil}^{n}g}\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}
is exact for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}

Either of the above implies

(3)

\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{L}\to\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{M}\to\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{N}
is exact,

and the converse holds if
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
are finite and
g\circ f=0
.

2.3.1.
Proof of
2.3

Let
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then we have a diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fil}^{n}f}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{L,n}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fil}^{n}g}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{M,n}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{N,n}}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{N}

Note that

\operatorname{Ker}(g)\cap\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Ker}(g\circ\iota_{M,n})=\operatorname{Ker}(\iota_{L,n}\circ\mathrm{fil}^{n}g)=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}g)

because
\iota_{N,n}
is injective.

Assume (
2
) and exhaustiveness for
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
. If
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ker}(g)
,
10
10

10

We phrase the proof as if
\mathcal{A}
is a category of modules, noting that this suffices by the Freyd–Mitchell Emebedding Theorem.
then
\alpha\in\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}
for sufficiently small
n
by exhaustiveness, so
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}g)
by (*), and thus
\alpha\in\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}f)
by exactness of the top, hence
\alpha\in\operatorname{Im}(f)
. If
\alpha\in\operatorname{Im}(f)
, say
\alpha=f(\beta)
, then
\beta\in\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}
for sufficiently small
n
, so
\alpha\in\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}f)=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}g)=\operatorname{Ker}(g)\cap\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ker}(g)
. It follows that
L\xrightarrow{f}M\xrightarrow{g}N
is exact.

Assuming
L\xrightarrow{f}M\xrightarrow{g}N
is exact, we have by (*) that
\operatorname{Im}(f)\cap\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Ker}(g)\cap\mathrm{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}g)
. Strictness of
f
(at
n
) is the condition that the left-hand side is
\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}f)
, and exactness of the top is the condition that the right-hand side is
\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{fil}^{n}f)
, so we have shown (
1
)
\iff
(
2
).

For either statement about (
3
), we may assume
g\circ f=0
. In this case,
\operatorname{fil}^{n}g\circ\operatorname{fil}^{n}f=0
for all
n
, so
\operatorname{gr}^{n}g\circ\operatorname{gr}^{n}f=0
.

We thus get a short exact sequence of chain complexes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}f}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}g}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}\mathcal{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n}f}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n}g}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{gr}_{n}\mathcal{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{gr}^{n}f}
\textstyle{\operatorname{gr}_{n}\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{gr}^{n}g}
\textstyle{\operatorname{gr}_{n}\mathcal{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}

and thus a long exact sequence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}f)\to\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}f)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{gr}^{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{gr}^{n}f)\to\Coker(\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}g)

If we assume (
2
), then
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{gr}_{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{gr}_{n}f)=0
for all such
f
and
g
, which implies (
3
).

Conversely, assume (
3
) and that
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
are finite. Choose
m>>0
so that
\operatorname{fil}^{m}\mathcal{L}=\operatorname{fil}^{m}\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{fil}^{m}\mathcal{N}=0
. Then clearly
0\to\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{L}\to\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{M}\to\operatorname{fil}^{n}\mathcal{N}\to 0
is exact for
n\geq m
.

Now we apply downward induction on
n
to show that
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}f)=0
for all
n
. We have shown this for
n+1
. Then
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n+1}f)=\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{gr}^{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{gr}^{n}f)=0
by induction, so
\operatorname{Ker}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}g)/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{fil}^{n}f)=0
.

2.3.2.

As in
2.1.12
, Lemma
2.3
applies equally to exact sequences of objects with increasing instead of decreasing filtration.

2.3.3.
Remark

Lemma
2.3
implies that
\mathrm{fil}^{n}\colon\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{A}
is exact in the sense that it sends exact sequences (in the sense of
2.1.9
) to exact sequences.

2.4.
Functors into categories of filtered objects

2.4.1.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be additive categories. A functor
\phi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{B})
is equivalent to giving a functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
and a decreasing sequence of subfunctors
\{F^{n}\omega\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
\omega
. Indeed, given
\phi
, we set
\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}\circ\phi
and
F^{n}\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{fil}^{n}\circ\phi
. Given
\omega
,
\{F^{n}\omega\}
, and
M\in\mathcal{A}
we set
\phi(M)\in\mathrm{Fil}(\mathcal{B})
to be
\omega(M)\in\mathcal{B}
with filtration given by
\mathrm{fil}^{n}\phi(M)\coloneqq F^{n}\omega(M)
.

2.4.2.

If
\phi
is exact and
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M})
, then
\phi(\mathcal{L}\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{M}\to\Coker(f))
is exact in
\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{B})
, so
\phi(f)
is strict. It follows easily that
\phi
is exact iff
\omega
is exact and
\phi(f)
is strict for all
f\in\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{A})
.

By Lemma
2.3
(for
\mathcal{A}
abelian),
\phi
is exact (
2.3.3
) iff
\omega
and each
F^{n}\omega
is exact. If
\phi
lands in
\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{B})
, then it is exact iff
\operatorname{gr}\circ\phi
is exact.

2.4.3.

A symmetric monoidal structure on
\phi
is equivalent to a symmetric monoidal structure on
\omega
for which

F^{n}\omega(M\otimes N)=\sum_{a+b=n}F^{a}\omega(M)\otimes F^{b}\omega(N)

for all
n
under the isomorphism
\omega(M\otimes N)\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega(M)\otimes\omega(N)
.

2.4.4.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian category (
B.1.3
) over
k
, a
filtered fiber functor
(
[
116
, Definition 4.4(i)]
; functor with exact
\otimes
-filtration in
[
97
, 2.1.1]
) over
k^{\prime}
is an exact tensor functor
\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilVect_{k^{\prime}}
. We will return to this in §
4.4
.

2.4.5.

If
\omega\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is an additive functor between abelian (resp. additive) categories, then we have induced functors
\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)\colon\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{A})\to\operatorname{Fil}(\mathcal{B})
(resp.
\Gr(\omega)\colon\Gr(\mathcal{A})\to\Gr(\mathcal{B})
), the former by setting
\operatorname{fil}^{n}(\omega(A))\coloneqq\operatorname{Im}(\omega(\operatorname{fil}^{n}(A))\to\omega(A))
. In this way,
\operatorname{Fil}
and
\Gr
can be viewed as functors from the category of abelian (resp. additive) categories to the category of quasi-abelian (resp. additive) categories. The same is true for
\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}
and
\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}
, as well as
\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}
if
\omega
respects countable filtered limits and colimits.

We always have

\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{fil}}\circ\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)=\omega\circ\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{fil}}

\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}(\omega)=\omega\circ\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}

\operatorname{fil}\circ\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}(\omega)=\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\omega)\circ\operatorname{fil}.

If countable coproducts exist and are respected by
\omega
, then

\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\Gr(\omega)=\omega\circ\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}

\operatorname{fil}\circ\Gr(\omega)=\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)\circ\operatorname{fil}.

If
\omega
is left exact, then

\operatorname{fil}^{n}\circ\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)=\omega\circ\operatorname{fil}^{n},

and if
\omega
is exact, then

\operatorname{gr}^{n}\circ\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)=\omega\circ\operatorname{gr}^{n}

\operatorname{gr}\circ\operatorname{Fil}(\omega)=\Gr(\omega)\circ\operatorname{gr}.

2.5.
Filtrations on Tannakian categories

For the rest of §
2
, we fix a field
k
of characteristic
0
and work exclusively with a Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
over
k
.

2.5.1.
Definition

A
filtration

W
on
\mathcal{T}
is a tensor functor
\xi_{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{fil}}\circ\xi_{W}=\id_{\mathcal{T}}
.

We set
W_{n}\coloneqq\mathrm{fil}^{-n}\circ\phi
, so that
\{W_{n}M\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
is an increasing filtration of any
M\in\mathcal{T}
(c.f.
2.1.12
).

A Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
with a choice of filtration is called a
filtered Tannakian category
.

2.5.2.

For a filtered Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
,
M\in\Ob(\mathcal{T})
, and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set

\operatorname{gr}_{n}M\coloneqq W_{n}M/W_{n-1}M.

Note that
\operatorname{gr}_{n}
is a functor from
\mathcal{T}
to itself. In the notation of
2.1.4
, we have
\operatorname{gr}_{n}=\operatorname{gr}^{-n}\circ\xi_{W}
.

We also set

(2)

\operatorname{gr}^{W}(M)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{gr}_{n}M.

In the notation of
2.2.5
,
\operatorname{gr}^{W}=\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\operatorname{gr}\circ\xi_{W}
.

2.5.3.
Definition/Theorem

A filtration
\{W_{n}\}
on a Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
is
strict
if any of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(1)

The functor
\xi_{W}
is exact.

(2)

Every morphism in
\mathcal{T}
is strict for the filtration

(3)

Each functor
W_{n}
is exact

(4)

The associated graded functor
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
is exact

A Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
equipped with a strict filtration is called a
strictly filtered Tannakian category
.

2.5.4.
Proof of
2.5.3

Since
\mathrm{forg}\circ\xi_{W}=\id_{\mathcal{T}}
is exact,
2.4.2
says (
1
) is equivalent to
\xi_{W}(f)
being strict for all
f\in\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{T})
, which is (
2
).

The equivalence between (
1
) and (
3
) (resp.
4
) is immediate from the first (resp. second) part of Lemma
2.3
, noting again that
\mathrm{forg}\circ\xi_{W}=\id_{\mathcal{T}}
is exact, and
\xi_{W}
lands in
\mathrm{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}\mathcal{T}
.

2.5.5.
Remark

If
\xi_{W}
is a strict filtration on
\mathcal{T}
and
\omega
a choice of fiber functor, then
W_{n}
turns
\omega
into a filtered fiber functor (Definition
4.10
). Conversely, if
\omega
is a filtered fiber functor over
k
whose Tannakian Galois group preserves the filtration on every object, then this filtration comes from a strict filtration on
\mathcal{T}
(
4.10.1
).

2.5.6.
Definition

We say that an object
M
in a filtered Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
is
concentrated in degree
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
if
0=W_{n-1}M\subseteq W_{n}M=M
.

Lemma 2.5.7
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
has a filtration. Then the filtration is strict iff for any
M,N\in\Ob(\mathcal{T})
and
m\neq n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, no nonzero subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{m}M
is isomorphic to a nonzero subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{n}N
.

Proof.

Suppose the filtration is strict. Then every morphism in
\mathcal{T}
is strict for the filtration. In particular, if
0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{f}C\to 0
is an exact sequence, then
W_{i}A=W_{i}B\cap A
, and
W_{i}C=f(W_{i}B)
for all
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. From this, it is easy to see that
\operatorname{gr}_{m}M
(resp.
\operatorname{gr}_{n}N
) and any subquotient thereof is concentrated in degree
m
(resp.
n
). Thus if
L
is isomorphic to a subquotient of both
\operatorname{gr}_{m}M
and
\operatorname{gr}_{n}N
for
m<n
, then
L=W_{m}L\subseteq W_{n-1}L=0
, so
L=0
.

Conversely, fix a morphism
f\colon A\to B
in
\mathcal{T}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
C
denote the preimage in
A
of
W_{n}B
, so that
C/\operatorname{Ker}{f}\simeq f(C)=W_{n}B\cap f(A)
. Modding out on both sides by
W_{n}A
, we get
C/(W_{n}A+\operatorname{Ker}{f})\simeq(W_{n}B\cap f(A))/f(W_{n}A)
. It suffices to show that this is always zero.

Suppose
D\coloneqq C/(W_{n}A+\operatorname{Ker}{f})
is nonzero. Then for
i\geq n
,
V_{i}\coloneqq(C\cap W_{i}A)/(W_{n}A+(\operatorname{Ker}{f}\cap W_{i}A))
is an increasing sequence of subobjects of
D
with
V_{n}=0
and
V_{i}=D
for
i
sufficiently large (e.g., such that
W_{i}A=A
). Let
m>n
such that
V_{m}\neq 0
but
V_{m-1}=0
. Then
V_{m}=V_{m}/V_{m-1}=(C\cap W_{m}A)/((\operatorname{Ker}{f}\cap W_{m}A)+C\cap W_{m-1}A)
is a quotient of
(C\cap W_{m}A)/(C\cap W_{m-1}A)
, which is the image of
C
in
\operatorname{gr}_{m}A
. In particular, it is a subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{m}A
.

On the other hand, for
i\leq n
, let
U_{i}\coloneqq(W_{i}B\cap f(A))/(W_{i}B\cap f(W_{n}A))
. We clearly have
U_{n}=D
, while
U_{i}=0
for
i
sufficiently small. Let
j\leq n
so that
U_{j}\cap V_{m}\neq 0
while
U_{j-1}\cap V_{m}=0
. Then
U_{j}\cap V_{m}
is a nonzero subobject of
V_{m}
and hence a subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{m}A
.

Now
U_{j}/U_{j-1}
is a quotient of
(W_{j}B\cap f(A))/(W_{j-1}B\cap f(A))
, which is the image of
W_{j}B\cap f(A)
in
\operatorname{gr}_{j}B
, hence a subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{j}B
. On the other hand,
U_{j}\cap V_{m}
maps isomorphically onto its image in
U_{j}/U_{j-1}
, so
U_{j}\cap V_{m}
is a subquotient of
\operatorname{gr}_{j}B
. Since
j\leq n<m
, we find that
U_{j}\cap V_{m}=0
, a contradiction. Therefore,
D
is zero, and the filtration is strict.

∎

2.5.8.

If the filtration is strict, note
\operatorname{gr}_{n}M
is concentrated in degree
n
. It follows that

(3)

W_{n}\operatorname{gr}^{W}M=\sum_{m\leq n}\operatorname{gr}_{m}M

and hence that

\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}\operatorname{gr}^{W}M=\sum_{m\leq n}\operatorname{gr}_{m}M\big/\sum_{m\leq n-1}\operatorname{gr}_{m}M\simeq\operatorname{gr}_{n}M,

and thus

(4)

\operatorname{gr}^{W}\operatorname{gr}^{W}M\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}M\simeq\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}M=\operatorname{gr}^{W}M.

Furthermore, any
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}M,\operatorname{gr}^{W}N)
respects the decomposition (
2
) by Lemma
2.5.7
and thus equals its own associated graded. Thus the isomorphism (
4
) upgrades to an isomorphism of functors

(5)

\operatorname{gr}^{W}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}\simeq\operatorname{gr}^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}.

2.5.9.
Definition

We say that a strict filtration
W_{n}
on a Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
is a
weight filtration
if for any
M\in\Ob(\mathcal{T})
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M

is semisimple in
\mathcal{T}
. In this case,
\operatorname{gr}^{W}M
is semisimple.

A Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
with a choice of weight filtration is called a
weight-filtered Tannakian category
.

2.5.10.
Remark

Goncharov lists some of the defining properties of a weight-filtered Tannakian category when describing categories of the form
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
in
[
69
, §3]
, especially at the bottom of p.15 of loc.cit. We mention these briefly in Remark
A.4.1
.

2.5.11.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a filtered Tannakian category, and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian subcategory, then
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
acquires a natural filtration. If the filtration on
\mathcal{T}
is strict (resp. a weight filtration), then so is the filtration on
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. For the weight filtration, note that if
M\in\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is semisimple in
\mathcal{T}
, then it is semisimple in
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, so
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is weight-filtered if
\mathcal{T}
is.

2.6.
Hodge and Frobenius fiber functors

2.6.1.
Definition

If
\mathcal{T}
11
11

11

Technically a strictly filtered Tannakian category is a pair
(\mathcal{T},\xi_{W})
, but we suppress this in the notation.
is a strictly filtered Tannakian category over a field
k
and
k^{\prime}/k
a field extension, a
Hodge-filtered fiber functor over
k^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{T}
is a filtered fiber functor (
2.4.4
)

\omega_{H}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathrm{FilVect}_{k^{\prime}}.

By abuse of language, we sometimes refer to
\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}\circ\omega_{H}
as a Hodge-filtered fiber functor, viewing the filtration as a sequence of subfunctors
F^{p}\omega
as in
2.4.1
.

2.6.2.
Definition

A
theory of weights
on a field
k^{\prime}_{0}
is a collection of disjoint subsets
w_{n}=w_{n}(k^{\prime}_{0})
of
\overline{k^{\prime}_{0}}
indexed by
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
1_{k^{\prime}_{0}}\in w_{0}
, if
\alpha\in w_{n}
and
\beta\in w_{m}
, then
\alpha\beta\in w_{n+m}
, and if
\alpha^{m}\in w_{nm}
, then
\alpha\in w_{n}
.

2.6.3.
Example

If
k
is of characteristic
0
and
q
is a positive real number, we define the
q
-Weil weight structure
by setting
W_{n}
to be the set of
\alpha\in\overline{k}
such that for any embedding
\iota\colon{\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha)\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, we have
|\iota(\alpha)|=q^{\frac{n}{2}}
.

2.6.4.
Definition

Let
R
a
k
-algebra equipped with a finite-order
k
-algebra automorphism
\phi
. A
\varphi
-module over
R
is a an
R
-module equipped with a
\phi
-linear automorphism
\varphi
.
\varphi
-Modules over
R
form an abelian tensor category with

\varphi_{X\otimes Y}=\varphi_{X}\otimes\varphi_{Y}.

We denote the tensor category of filtered
\varphi
-modules over
R
by
\varphi\mathrm{Mod}(R)
.

2.6.5.

Let
d
be the order of
\phi
acting on
R
. Then
\varphi^{d}
is an
R
-linear automorphism of
M
.

Definition 2.6.6
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a strictly filtered Tannakian category over a field
k
,
k_{0}^{\prime}/k
a field extension,
\{w_{n}\}
a theory of weights on
k^{\prime}_{0}
, and
\phi
an automorphism of
k^{\prime}_{0}
of order
d
fixing
k
. A
Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
(k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
is an exact tensor functor

\omega_{\phi}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\varphi\mathrm{Mod}(k^{\prime}_{0})

such that for
M=\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}(M)
, the eigenvalues of
\varphi^{d}
acting on
\omega_{\phi}(M)
are in
w_{dn}
. We set
\omega_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}_{\phi}\circ\omega_{\phi}
.

Definition 2.6.7
.

If
k^{\prime}/k^{\prime}_{0}/k
are field extensions where
k^{\prime}_{0}
has a theory of weights and a finite-order automorphism
\phi
, a
Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor

\omega_{H,\phi}=(\omega_{H},\omega_{\phi},\eta)
over
(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
is a triple of a Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega_{H}
valued in
\FilVect_{k^{\prime}}
, a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
\omega_{\phi}
valued in
\varphi\mathrm{Mod}(k^{\prime}_{0})
, and a natural
\otimes
-isomorphism

\omega\xrightarrow[\raisebox{1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}]{\eta}\Ex_{k^{\prime}_{0}}^{k^{\prime}}\circ\omega_{0}.

We use
\eta
to implicitly identify the two functors over
k^{\prime}
.

2.6.8.
Remark

A Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
naturally outputs filtered
\varphi
-modules over
k^{\prime}
. One may define such a functor to be
admissible
if its image lands in admissible filtered
\varphi
-modules, where the Newton polygon is defined in an obvious way using the theory of weights on
k^{\prime}_{0}
.

2.6.9.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a strictly filtered Tannakian category with a Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
\omega_{H,\phi}
, and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian subcategory, then
\omega_{H,\phi}
restricts to a Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor on
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.

3.
Examples of Weight-Filtered Tannakian Categories

3.1.
Strictly geometric Galois representations

We recall some constructions from
[
34
, §3]
and explain how they fit under the general definitions above.

3.1.1.
Definition

Let
K
be a number field and
p
a prime number, and let

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{g}}(G_{K})

denote the category of
p
-adic representations of
G_{K}
that are unramified almost everywhere and de Rham at every place of
K
above
p
. We let

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})

denote the subcategory of representations
V
that are
strongly geometric
, meaning
V
has a finite increasing filtration
W^{\bullet}V
, known as the
motivic weight filtration
, for which

\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}V

is pure of weight
n
at almost all (unramified) places in the sense of
[
47
]
. This category is strictly filtered by Lemma
2.5.7
.

3.1.2.

We let
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm sg}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
be the subcategory of
V\in\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm sg}
for which
\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}V
is semisimple for each
n
. Then
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
is a neutral weight-filtered Tannakian category over
k={\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
in the sense of Definition
2.5.9
.

3.1.3.

Fixing a place
\mathfrak{p}
of
K
above
p
, the functor

\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H}\colon V\mapsto\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(V)\coloneqq V^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(V)\in\mathrm{FilVect}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}

is a Hodge-filtered fiber functor on
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})
over
k^{\prime}=K_{\mathfrak{p}}
by
[
52
, Theorem 5.29]
, and
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}\circ\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H}
.

3.2.
S
-integral variants

For a subset
S\subseteq S_{f}(K)
, let
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm f,S}(G_{K})
denote the full subcategory of
V\in\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm g}(G_{K})
with good reduction
12
12

12

A
p
-adic representation of
G_{K}
has
good reduction
at
v
if it is unramified (resp. crystalline) for
v\notin S_{p}(K)
(resp.
v\in S_{p}(K)
).
at all
v\notin S
. Let
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})
denote the full subcategory of
V\in\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{f,S}}(G_{K})\cap\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})
such that:

•

For
v\notin S
, the Frobenius at
v
acts by weight
n
on
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}V
(resp.
\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}V)
) for
v\notin S_{p}(K)
(resp.
v\in S_{p}(K)
).

We similarly define
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}\coloneqq\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})\cap\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}

3.2.1.
Definition

The category
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm sf,S}(G_{K})
has a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor as long as there is
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)\setminus S
. If we fix
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)
, we may simply work with

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf},\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}(G_{K})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{p}\notin S}\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\rm sf,S}(G_{K}).

Then the functor

V\mapsto\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}(V)\coloneqq V_{\mathrm{cr}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(V)

is a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor on
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf},\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}(G_{K})
over
(k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)=(K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})
with respect to the standard Frobenius and the
q
-Weil weight structure, where
q
is the size of the residue field of
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

3.2.2.

The inclusion
B_{\mathrm{cris}}\subseteq B_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
induces a functorial tensor isomorphism

V^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\xrightarrow[\raisebox{1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}]{\eta^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}}V^{\mathrm{cris}}\otimes_{K_{\mathfrak{p},0}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}

making
\boxed{(\omega_{H},\omega_{\phi},\eta)=(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H},\omega^{\mathrm{cr}},\eta^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}})}
into a Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
\boxed{(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)=(K_{\mathfrak{p}},K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})}
on the strictly filtered Tannakian category
\boxed{\mathcal{T}=\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf},\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}(G_{K})}
over
\boxed{k={\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}
and thus also on its weight-filtered subcategory
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf},\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
. Note that it is admissible in the sense of Remark
2.6.8
.

3.3.
Systems of realizations and pre-motivic structures

While categories of Galois representations suffice for some purposes, it is sometimes better to have
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear Tannakian categories of motives. Such a category is known to exist with all the desired properties only in the category of mixed (Artin-)Tate motives, which apply only when
X
is rational. Therefore, in general, we must consider the unconditional categories of systems of realizations. For a treatment of the conjectural abelian category of mixed motives, see Appendix
A
.

There are numerous versions of the category of systems of realizations in the literature, for example due to Deligne, Huber, and Jannsen. We focus on Fontaine–Perrin-Riou’s category
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
of pre-motivic structures over
K
with
{\mathbb{Q}}
-coefficients, as it has a built-in
p
-adic Hodge comparison isomorphism, which is particularly relevant to
p
-adic periods and the Chabauty–Kim method. We show how to define the subcategory
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
of realizations of motives (which
[
53
, III.4.1.2]
assumes but does not define). We make remarks about comparisons between the different categories (
3.4.8
) and note in particular that
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
should be equivalent to the category of mixed motives for absolute Hodge cycles (Remark
3.6.4
).

3.3.1.
Brief Overview of §
3.3
-
3.8

For the convenience of the reader who wishes to take the rest of §
3
as a black box, we give a quick guide to the definition of our replacement
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(Definition
3.6.3
) for the category of mixed motives over
\mathbb{Q}
.

In
3.3.2
, we define the category
\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
abstract systems of realizations, and in Definition
3.3.4
, we define the category
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
of systems with a weight filtration.

In Theorem
3.6.2
, we define a realization functor from Voevodsky’s triangulated category of geometric motives to the bounded derived category of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Finally, in Definition
3.6.3
, we define

\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})

as the Tannakian subcategory of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
generated by all cohomology objects of complexes in the image of the realization functor.

In §
3.4
-
3.5
, we discuss various subcategories of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. The most important one is
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
, as it contains
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
; it consists of those that have good reduction and appropriately-weighted Frobenii outside
S
for some finite set
S
of places of
K
and have a polarization. The latter property ensures that the category is weight-filtered. These sections are not strictly needed for the definition of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
but imply useful properties of it.

In Definition
3.6.5
and §
3.8
, we define some important subcategories of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
, including those with good reduction outside a fixed set
S
of places and those with socle generated by the cohomology of a single fixed variety. In §
3.7
, we discuss the relation between
\operatorname{Ext}
in this category and motivic cohomology and
K
-theory.

3.3.2.

We recall, for a number field
K
, Fontaine–Perrin-Riou’s category
\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(
[
53
, III.2.1.1]
). An object
M
of
\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
consists of the modules:

•

A vector space
M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
over
K
with a finite decreasing Hodge filtration
F^{i}M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}

•

For each
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
, a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector space
M_{B,v}
with an action of
G_{v}

•

For each prime number
\ell
, an object
M_{\ell}\in\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{g}}(G_{K})

and the comparison isomorphisms:

•

For each pair of a prime number
\ell
and
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
, an isomorphism

\iota_{\ell,v}\colon M_{B,v}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}M_{\ell}

compatible with the action of
G_{v}

•

For each
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
, a
G_{v}
-equivariant isomorphism

\iota_{v}\colon M_{B,v}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\overline{K_{v}}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}\overline{K_{v}},

where
G_{v}
acts on
M_{B,v}
and both copies of
\overline{K_{v}}

•

For each prime number
p
and
v\in S_{p}(K)
, an isomorphism

\iota_{v}\colon M_{p}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}B_{{\mathrm{dR}},v}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-7.97224pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}}M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}B_{{\mathrm{dR}},v}

of
B_{{\mathrm{dR}},v}
-vector spaces compatible with action of
G_{v}
and the Hodge filtration.

A morphism
f\colon M\to N
is a collection of morphisms of all associated modules (Betti, de Rham,
\ell
-adic), that preserve the associated structures (Hodge filtration, conjugation action,
G_{K}
-action) and are compatible with all comparison isomorphisms. The tensor product is defined in an obvious way, making this category Tannakian.

3.3.3.
Fiber Functors

For each
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
, we have a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear fiber functor
\omega^{v}=\omega^{B,v}
given by
M\mapsto M_{B,v}
. We also have a
K
-linear fiber functor
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
sending
M
to
M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, and for each prime number
\ell
, a
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-linear fiber functor
\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}=\omega^{\ell}
sending
M
to
M_{\ell}
. The comparison isomorphisms give natural isomorphisms between all these fiber functors over the appropriate base extensions.

When we remember the Galois representation on
M_{\ell}
, we get a functor
\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}\colon\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})\to\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{g}}(G_{K})
.

3.3.4.
Definition

Following
[
53
, III.2.1.4]
, we define a
pre-motivic structure

M
to be an object
M\in\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
along with an increasing finite filtration
\{W_{n}M\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
in
\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
such that

•

For each
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
, the vector space
M_{B,v}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{R}}
is a mixed Hodge structure over
K_{v}
when equipped with the weight filtration coming from
\{W_{n}M\}
, the Hodge filtration coming from
\iota_{v}
, and if
F_{v}\simeq{\mathbb{R}}
, the Frobenius action from
G_{v}
.

As in
[
53
, III.2.1.4]
, we denote the category of such objects and morphisms in
\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
that respect the filtration by
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. As mentioned in loc.cit., it is a neutral
{\mathbb{Q}}
-linear Tannakian category, with unit object the cohomology of
\operatorname{Spec}{K}
.

By III.2.1.2 of loc.cit., all morphisms are strictly compatible with the weight filtration, so
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
is a strictly filtered Tannakian category over
k={\mathbb{Q}}
with Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H}
.

3.3.5.

When we remember the filtration on
M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, we get a filtered
13
13

13

The subfunctors
F^{p}\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
are exact by
[
46
, Théorème 2.3.5(iii)]
.
fiber functor (
2.4.4
)
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H}\colon\mathbf{PSPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})\to\FilVect_{K}
.

3.4.
Variants of pre-motivic structures

We describe various Tannakian subcategories of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
, most of which are known to (and all of which are expected to) contain realizations of motives.

3.4.1.

We let
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Hub}}
denote the subcategory of
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
such that

•

For each
\ell
, the
G_{K}
-representation
\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M_{\ell}
is pure of weight
n
at almost all unramified
v\in S_{f}(K)
in the sense of
[
47
]
.

3.4.2.

Convention: Any appearance of ‘
K,S
’ as a subscript after
\mathbf{M}
is interchangeable with ‘
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
’ or ‘
Z
’ where
Z=\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}
.

For a subset
S\subseteq S_{f}(k)
, we let
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})=\mathbf{SPM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}})
denote the subcategory of
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
for which
M_{\ell}\in\Rep^{\rm{sf,S}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}(G_{K})
for all
\ell
, and

\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}\coloneqq\bigcup_{\underset{\mathrm{finite}}{S\subseteq S_{f}(K)}}\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}}).

Note that
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}\subseteq\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Hub}}
.

3.4.3.

For any
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)\setminus S
, we have a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
(k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)=(K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\mathrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{p}})
on the category
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
. In fact, the union

\mathbf{SPM}_{K,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}({\mathbb{Q}})\coloneqq\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S_{f}(K)\setminus\{\mathfrak{p}\}}({\mathbb{Q}})=\mathbf{SPM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})

over all such
S
has a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}
over
(K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\mathrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{p}})
given by

M\mapsto\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}(M)\coloneqq M_{\mathrm{cr}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(M_{p}).

3.4.4.

We let
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
denote the full subcategory of
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
for which each
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M
is
polarizable of weight
n
in the following sense:

•

There exists an isomorphism
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M\to(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M)^{\vee}(-n)
in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
whose restriction to the Betti and de Rham realizations is a polarization of Hodge structures of weight
n
.

3.4.5.

We claim the categories
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}\subseteq\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}\subseteq\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Hub}}
and
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
are Tannakian subcategories of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

For the first claim, note that the property of being unramified/crystalline and of weight
n
at a particular place
v
is stable under taking subobjects, quotients, tensor products, and duals, so this follows.

For the second, see
3.5.2
for kernels and quotients.
Finally, noting that the weight-graded pieces of a tensor product (resp. dual) are the tensor products (resp. duals) of the weight-graded pieces and that one can take the tensor product (resp. dual) of polarization(s),
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
is stable under tensor product and dual.

3.4.6.
Remark

One might wish to define the full subcategory
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
of
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
for which
\{M_{\ell}\}_{\ell}
form a (weakly) compatible system of
\ell
-adic representation in the sense of
[
108
, p.81]
. Similarly, we could define
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{scomp}}
denote those for which
\{M_{\ell}\}_{\ell}
is strongly compatible in the sense of loc.cit. Note that
[
108
, Conjecture 1.3(2)]
says that
\mathrm{comp}=\mathrm{scomp}
.

Unfortunately, these subcategories are not abelian, since the kernel and cokernel of a map between compatible systems need not be compatible.
14
14

14

For example, if we take
M
to be the motive of a degree
2
extension of
K
but then twist the Betti-étale comparison isomorphisms for a single
\ell
by the involution that switches the trivial and nontrivial characters, then there is a map from
M
to itself whose kernel is not in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
.
In particular,
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
is not a Tannakian category, so we cannot apply the Nori formalism to it as in §
3.6.6
. Nonetheless, the realization of the motive of a single smooth proper variety is contained in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
(c.f. Remark
3.6.10
).

If we restrict to objects in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
, all of whose subobjects in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
are also in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{comp}}
, then we get an abelian category. However, we don’t know that the cohomology of a variety satisfies this additional property. Such a property would follow from the absolute Hodge conjecture and an argument as in
[
1
]
.

3.4.7.

For
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m}\in\{\mathrm{Hub},\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{comp},\mathrm{scomp},\mathrm{pol}\}
and

\beta\in\{\{K\},\{K,S\},\{K,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})\}\}
, we let

\mathbf{SPM}_{\beta}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m}}\coloneqq\mathbf{SPM}_{\beta}({\mathbb{Q}})\cap\bigcap_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\alpha_{j}}.

For example, we will show that motives have realizations in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
.

3.4.8.
Comparison with the literature

There is an obvious functor from
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
to Jannsen’s category
\underline{\mathrm{MR}}_{k}
(
[
79
, Definition 2.1]
).

There is also a functor from
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Hub}}
to Huber’s
\mathcal{MR}
(
[
77
, 11.1]
), where
A_{\sigma,\ell}\coloneqq A_{\sigma}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
and
A_{\sigma,{\mathbb{C}}}\coloneqq A_{\sigma}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{C}}
.

Finally, there is are obvious functors from
\mathbf{SPM}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}
to Deligne’s
systèmes de réalisations
(
[
48
, 1.9]
) and from
\mathbf{SPM}_{{\mathbb{Q}},S}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}
to those that are lisse over
\operatorname{Spec}{{\mathbb{Z}}}\setminus S
(
[
48
, 1.15]
) by taking
M_{\mathrm{cris},p}=\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(M_{p})
for
p
such that
M_{p}
is crystalline (resp.
p\notin S
).

None of these functors is expected to be an equivalence, as none of the target categories require the Galois representations to be de Rham.

3.5.
Polarization and weight-filtered structures

Proposition 3.5.1
.

For
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
,
\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M
is semisimple in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. In particular,
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
is a weight-filtered Tannakian category over
k={\mathbb{Q}}
.

By
2.5.11
and
3.4.5
, so are
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol},\mathrm{Hub}}
,
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol},\mathrm{Del}}
, and
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
.

3.5.2.
Proof

The proof is a modification of that of
[
79
, Lemma 1.1]
. Given
M\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
and
A\subseteq\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M
in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
, we define
B\subseteq\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M
to be the orthogonal complement of
A
under the polarization. Note that
B
can also be defined by taking the cokernel
B^{\prime}
of

A\to\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M\to(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M)^{\vee}(-n)

and then dualizing and twisting the map
(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M)^{\vee}(-n)\to B^{\prime}
to get an inclusion
B\to\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M
, which shows that
B
is an object of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. The polarization property implies by a standard argument that
A
and
B
are complementary, so that
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M\simeq A\oplus B
in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

Note that the polarization restricts to a pairing on any subobject in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
, and this restriction is in fact non-degenerate (and also positive-definite) by the positive-definiteness of a polarization. In particular,
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M\simeq A\oplus B
is in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
as well. This implies
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M
is also semisimple in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
, and therefore
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}
is a weight-filtered Tannakian category.

3.5.3.
