Now that we have a weight-filtered Tannakian category, let us recall the extra structure on its fiber functors.

The category
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(and therefore any Tannakian subcategory) has a
K
-linear Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}_{H}
(
3.3.5
). Let
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}_{H}
denote its base-change along
K\hookrightarrow K_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

As noted in
3.4.3
,
\mathbf{SPM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})
(and therefore all its Tannakian subcategories) has a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
\omega^{\mathrm{cr}}
over
(K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

We set
\eta^{{\mathrm{mot}}}\coloneqq\eta^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}\circ(\iota_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1})^{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, where

(\iota_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1})^{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}\colon M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}=(M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}B_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}})^{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}\to(M_{p}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}B_{{\mathrm{dR}},v})^{G_{\mathfrak{p}}}=V^{{\mathrm{dR}}}.

Then
\boxed{(\omega_{H},\omega_{\phi},\eta)=(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}_{H},\omega^{\mathrm{cr}},\eta^{{\mathrm{mot}}})}
is a
Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
over
\boxed{(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)=(K_{\mathfrak{p}},K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})}
on the
strictly filtered Tannakian category

\boxed{\mathcal{T}=\mathbf{SPM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})}
over
\boxed{k={\mathbb{Q}}}
and thus also on its
weight-filtered Tannakian
subcategory
\boxed{\mathbf{SPM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{pol}}}
.

3.5.4.
Remark

The Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor of
3.2.2
pulls back to that of
3.5.3
via the functor
\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}\colon\mathbf{SPM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})\to\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}^{\mathrm{sf,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}}(G_{K})
. Note in particular that
\iota_{\mathfrak{p}}
provides the isomorphism between
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\circ\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}
(
3.1.3
,
3.3.3
) and
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}
(
3.5.3
).

3.6.
Motivic structures

While
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
might seem like a good category to work with, the
\operatorname{Ext}
groups are generally not finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces. This is in particular because the compatibility between
M_{\ell}
for different
\ell
sees only the characteristic polynomial, which does not see extensions. One could therefore cook up independent extension classes at many different
\ell
, creating an extension space that is at best finite rank over the adeles. This issue is discussed briefly in
[
77
, p.194]
.

To regulate this chaos, it seems necessary to restrict to systems of realizations that come from geometry (equivalently, from motives). In terms of
\operatorname{Ext}
groups, this corresponds roughly to looking at the image

K_{m}(Y)^{(n)}\to\prod_{v\in S_{\infty}(K)}H^{2n-m}_{\mathcal{D}}(Y_{v};K_{v}(n))\times\prod_{\ell}H^{1}_{\rm{g}}(G_{K};H^{2n-m-1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{Y};{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}(m)))

for a variety
Y/K
and
m>0
, which is a (conjecturally) finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector space inside a space most naturally considered as a module over the adeles.

This is what
[
53
]
refers to as “motivic systems” and denotes
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. However,
[
53
, III.4.1.2]
does not specify what this means precisely, and in particular, what a “mixed motive” is. They conjecture furthermore (
[
53
, III.4.1.5]
) that such a category should induce isomorphisms (
3.6.9
) of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector spaces

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}},M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}_{\rm{g}}(G_{K};M).

This property is especially important for us because the dimensions of the latter (and thus by the conjecture, of the former) follow from the Bloch–Kato conjectures, as mentioned in §
1.5
.

We claim that “mixed motive” should be taken in the sense of Voevodsky’s triangulated category
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
(
[
112
, §2.1]
,
[
31
, 11.1.10]
) of
geometric motives
and construct a realization functor from this category to the bounded derived category
D^{b}(\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}})
and define
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
as the subcategory generated by the image of
H^{0}
.

3.6.1.
Motivic Structures Associated to Pairs of Varieties

As a preliminary step, we describe the object
h^{i}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)
of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
associated to the
i
th cohomology of a pair
X,Y
of smooth varieties over
K
.

We let
M(X,Y)
denote the geometric motive given by the complex
0\to Y\hookrightarrow X\to 0
with
X
in degree
0
. We get all the objects and most of the compatibilities and conditions defining
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Hub}}
by applying the contravariant functor
R_{\mathcal{MR}}
of
[
77
, Theorem 2.3.3]
to the Voevodsky motive
M(X,Y)
and taking
H^{i}
.
The one missing piece is the
p
-adic Hodge comparison and its compatibility with maps; but this follows by applying
[
92
, Theorem 1.2]
to
M(X,Y)
.

Note that any pair
(X,Y)
has a nice model over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
for sufficiently large
S
, which shows that
A^{*}(X,Y)
in fact lies in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del}}
.

For the polarization, note that most of the data of the polarization comes from
[
77
, Proposition 21.2.3]
. The only missing part is the compatibility of the polarizations with
p
-adic comparison isomorphisms. The polarization on a smooth projective variety is constructed using the Lefschetz class of a hyperplane section and Poincaré duality. The former is compatible with the
p
-adic comparison isomorphism by
[
109
, (A1.3)]
and the latter by
[
109
, (A1.2),(A1.5)]
.

We remark that for
M=h^{i}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)
,
M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}=H^{i}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X,Y)
,
M_{B,v}=H^{i}_{B,v}(X,Y;{\mathbb{Q}})\coloneqq H^{i}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X(K_{v}),Y(K_{v});{\mathbb{Q}})
, and
M_{\ell}=H^{i}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X_{\overline{K}},Y_{\overline{K}};{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
.

Theorem 3.6.2
.

There is a realization functor

\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}\colon\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})\to D^{b}(\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}})

such that

H^{-i}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(M(X,Y)))=h_{i}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)\coloneqq h^{i}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)^{\vee}.

Definition 3.6.3
.

We define the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
of
motivic structures
as the Tannakian subcategory of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
generated by the essential image of

H^{0}\circ\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}\colon\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}.

By
2.5.11
and Proposition
3.5.1
, it is a weight-filtered Tannakian category.

We believe this is the
meaning
of
[
53
, III.4.1.2]
and for
K={\mathbb{Q}}
of
[
48
, Definition 1.11]
, updated to reflect the availability of
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
. (See also the
hope
expressed in the paragraph immediately preceding Definition 1.11 of loc.cit.)

3.6.4.
Remark

The category
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
is very close to various categories of mixed motives for absolute Hodge cycles: the category
\mathcal{MM}
of
[
77
, 22.1.3]
, the category
\underline{MM}_{K}
of
[
79
, Definition 4.1]
, and
\mathcal{MM}_{\mathrm{AH}}
of
[
76
, Definition 6.3.11]
. The main difference for us is that our category of mixed realizations includes
p
-adic Hodge comparison; we felt this was important both because our application (Chabauty–Kim) heavily uses
p
-adic Hodge theory and because it allows us to invoke Fontaine–Perrin-Riou’s conjectures (
3.6.9
) comparing with Bloch–Kato Selmer groups.

It’s not clear that Jannsen’s
\underline{MM}_{K}
includes realizations of all complexes of varieties; see the discussion in the middle of p.194 of
[
77
]
. We want all realizations of complexes of varieties so as to be able to realize path torsors as in
8.1.1
.

3.6.5.
Definition

For
\beta\in\{\{K\},\{K,S\},\{K,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})\}\}
, we let (c.f. §
1.8
):

\mathbf{SM}_{\beta}({\mathbb{Q}})\coloneqq\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})\cap\mathbf{SPM}_{\beta}({\mathbb{Q}}).

Note then that by
3.5.3
and
2.6.9
,

\mathbf{SM}_{K,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}({\mathbb{Q}})=\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})

and its subcategories
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
for
\mathfrak{p}\notin S
are weight-filtered Tannakian categories with Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}_{H},\omega^{\mathrm{cr}},\eta^{{\mathrm{mot}}})
over
(K_{\mathfrak{p}},K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

3.6.6.
Proof of Theorem
3.6.2

We use
[
42
, Theorem 4.10]
(described also in
[
30
, §3.2]
), noting that the arguments work the same if we take
F
to be any field of characteristic
0
. The theorem associates a canonical monoidal realization functor
R_{A}\colon\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})\to D^{b}(\mathscr{A})
to:

•

An
F
-linear Tannakian category
\mathscr{A}
and fiber functor
\omega\colon\mathscr{A}\to\Vect_{F}

•

A representation
A^{*}\colon\Delta_{g}\to\mathscr{A}
for which
\omega\circ A^{*}
is the singular representation (see below).

Here,
\Delta_{g}
is Nori’s directed graph of good pairs, which is a localization of
\Delta_{g}^{{\rm{eff}}}
at multiplication by
({\mathbb{G}_{m}},\{1\},1)
. The directed graph
\Delta_{g}^{{\rm{eff}}}
has vertices
3
-tuples
(X,Y,i)
with
X,Y
quasi-projective (reduced but not necessarily integral) varieties over
K
such that
H^{j}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X},\overline{Y};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})=0
for
j\neq i
. The edges are everything of the form either
(X,Y,i)\to(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},i)
for a morphism
f\colon(X,Y)\to(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
of pairs or a boundary map
(Y,Z,i)\to(X,Y,i+1)
for a chain
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X
. It has an obvious product structure given by Cartesian product of pairs of varieties.

We take
F={\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mathscr{A}=\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol}}
and wish to define
A^{*}
. As in
[
76
, Lemma 8.2.4]
, it suffices to define
A^{*}
on
\Delta_{g}^{{\rm{eff}}}
in such a way that it is monoidal and such that
({\mathbb{G}_{m}},\{1\},1)
maps to an invertible object.

We can define
A^{*}(X,Y,i)\coloneqq h^{i}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)
as in
3.6.1
. Monoidality follows from that of
[
77
, Theorem 2.3.3]
and by functoriality and compatible with cup products of
p
-adic period isomorphisms
[
109
, A.1.1-2]
. Finally,
h^{1}_{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}},\{1\})
is invertible because
1=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}}H^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}},\{1\})=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}}H^{1}_{B,\sigma}({\mathbb{G}_{m}},\{1\};{\mathbb{Q}})=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}H^{1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(({\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{\overline{K}},\{1\};{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
, so we are done.

3.6.7.
Remark

Note that for any
(X,Y,i)

h^{i}_{\mathbf{SM}}(X,Y)\coloneqq h^{i}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,Y)=H^{-i}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(M(X,Y)))^{\vee}\in\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}).

The proof of Theorem
3.6.2
applied to
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
in place of
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
shows that
\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}
factors through a functor

\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}\colon\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})\to D^{b}(\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})).

3.6.8.
Notation

We set

{\mathbb{Q}}(1)={\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{\mathbf{SM}}\coloneqq h^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}}).

It satisfies
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}=\operatorname{gr}_{F}^{1}{\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, its
\ell
-adic realization is the
\ell
-adic cyclotomic character for each
\ell
, and
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}=2\pi iK\iota_{v}({\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{B,v})\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}}(1)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{F}\overline{F_{v}}
for each
v\in S_{\infty}(K)
.

We set
{\mathbb{Q}}(n)\coloneqq{\mathbb{Q}}(1)^{\otimes n}
, so that in particular
{\mathbb{Q}}(0)=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}(-1)={\mathbb{Q}}(1)^{\vee}
.

For
M\in\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
, we set

M(n)\coloneqq M\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}(n).

3.6.9.
Conjecture

In
[
53
, III.4.1.5]
, Fontaine–Perrin-Riou conjecture that
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
is
S
-admissible. As described in
[
53
, III.3.3.1]
, this says in particular that for
M\in\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
and any prime number
\ell
, the natural map

{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),M)\to H^{1}_{f,S}(G_{K};M_{\ell})

is an isomorphism.

This would imply that
\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),M)=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}}H^{1}_{f,S}(G_{K};M_{\ell})
. As mentioned in §
1.5
and described in more detail in
[
20
, §2.7]
, it is possible to give conjectural formulas for these dimensions.

3.6.10.
Remark

[
53
, III.4.1.5]
posits also that every object
M
of
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
is “L-admissible”. This means that in fact
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol},\mathrm{scomp}}
.

What’s known is that for a smooth proper variety
X/K
, we have
H^{i}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}(M(X)))\in\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})^{\mathrm{Del},\mathrm{pol},\mathrm{comp}}
. Replacing
\mathrm{comp}
with
\mathrm{scomp}
is Conjecture
C_{WD}(X,m)
of
[
54
, 2.4.3]
.

3.7.
Maps from motivic cohomology and
K
-theory

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety over
K
. By
[
85
, Theorem 3.4]
and
[
88
, Theorem 19.1]
, we have isomorphisms

(6)

K_{2n-k}(X)^{(n)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow[\raisebox{1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}]{\che}\CH^{n}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow[\raisebox{1.99306pt}{\smash{$\sim$}}]{\cyc_{{\mathrm{mot}}}}H^{k}_{{\mathrm{mot}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}(n)).

By
[
112
, p.197]
, we have an isomorphism

(7)

H^{k}_{{\mathrm{mot}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}}(n))\simeq\homo_{\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})}(M(X),{\mathbb{Q}}(n)[k]),

hence an isomorphism with all of (
6
).

Via the functor
\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}
of Remark
3.6.7
, we have a map

(8)

\homo_{\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})}(M(X),{\mathbb{Q}}(n)[k])\to\homo_{D^{b}(\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}(M(X)),{\mathbb{Q}}(n)[k]).

Taking
H^{-k}
gives us a map

\beta_{k}\colon\homo_{D^{b}(\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}(M(X)),{\mathbb{Q}}(n)[k])\to\homo_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}(H^{-k}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(M(X,Y))),{\mathbb{Q}}(n)).

Since
H^{-k}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}(M(X,Y)))\simeq h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k}(X)^{\vee}
, this latter group is

\homo_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}(h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k}(X)^{\vee},{\mathbb{Q}}(n))\simeq\homo_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k}(X)(n)).

The latter is trivial unless
k=2n
, in which case it is the space of absolute Hodge cycles in degree
k
. Either way, it is zero on
\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, the (higher
15
15

15

Of course
\CH^{n}(X,m)=\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,m)
if
m>0
.
) Chow group of cycles homologically equivalent to zero. The fact that
\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}(M(X))
is supported in negative degrees means the spectral sequence computing
\homo_{D^{b}(\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}
gives us a map

\operatorname{Ker}{\beta_{k}}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}(h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)^{\vee},{\mathbb{Q}}(n))\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))

and thus a motivic Abel–Jacobi map

(9)

\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}\colon\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n)).

The composition

\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}}H^{1}(G_{K};H_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}^{k-1}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))

is the étale Abel–Jacobi map
\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}
induced from the étale cycle class map
\cyc_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}\colon\CH^{n}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))
and the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence.

3.8.
Subcategories generated by a single variety

The categories
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
and
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
, although they have (conjecturally in the first case) finite-dimensional Ext-groups for
S
finite, are nonetheless too big to describe explicitly. We need to restrict to objects whose weight-graded pieces are in the subcategory generated by the cohomology of a fixed variety.

3.8.1.

Given a smooth projective variety
A/K
, let
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
) denote the Tannakian subcategory of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})
) generated by
\{h^{n}_{\mathbf{SM}}(A)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
(resp.
\{H^{n}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{A};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
). Our main focus is the case where
A
is an abelian variety, in which case
h^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}}(A)
(resp.
H^{1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{A};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
) generates
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
). We denote by

\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)

(resp.

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A))

the subcategory of
M
in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})
) whose weight-graded pieces lie in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
).

3.8.2.

If
A
has good reduction outside
S
, then
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}\coloneqq\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}\coloneqq\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
) is contained in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})
), and we let

\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},A)

(resp.

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K},A))

denote the full subcategory on the intersection of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)
and
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)
and
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})
).

3.8.3.

In general, every object of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
is semisimple by Proposition
3.5.1
. The latter is true for
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
if
A
satisfies the Grothendieck–Serre Semisimplicity Conjecture (GSSC), as discussed in
[
34
, §4.1]
. This is true if
A
is an abelian variety, by Faltings’ work. Only under this assumption do we have
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}\subseteq\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
and thus
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)\subseteq\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
.

When working with motivic structures or if
A
satisfies the GSSC,
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},A)
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K},A)
) is the thick subcategory of
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
) generated by
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K},A)^{\mathrm{ss}}
).

The categories
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)
and
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)
have the important property that their semisimple subcategory has a tensor generator (
B.1.6
), which implies that the maximal reductive quotient of their fundamental groups are algebraic groups (i.e., finite-type as schemes over
{\mathbb{Q}}
or
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, respectively).

3.8.4.

If
A
has good reduction at
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)
, we let

\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}},A)

(resp.

\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}}(G_{K},A))

denote the full subcategory on the intersection of
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A)
and
\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K},A)
and
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,\mathrm{cr}(\mathfrak{p})}}(G_{K})
). As in
3.6.5
,
3.5.3
and
2.6.9
imply these categories have Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}_{H},\omega^{\mathrm{cr}},\mathrm{id})
over
(K_{\mathfrak{p}},K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

3.9.
Mixed motives

Another example of a weight-filtered Tannakian category is expected to be provided by the conjectural abelian category of mixed motives. We discuss this in more detail in Appendix
A
and explain how conjectured properties of this category imply that it is a weight-filtered Tannakian category in our sense.

4.
Construction of Arithmetic Hodge Path

4.1.
Introduction

The goal of this section is to show (Theorem
4.18
) that a weight-filtered Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
over
k
with Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega_{H}
over an extension
k^{\prime}/k
has a functorial splitting of the weight filtration over
k^{\prime}
that respects the Hodge filtration. Such a splitting is given by a
\otimes
-isomorphism

\Psi\colon\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq\omega\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega

which we call an
arithmetic Hodge path
. The condition of respecting the Hodge filtration says that for
M\in\mathcal{T}
,
\Psi_{M}^{-1}
sends

F^{i}\omega(M)

to

\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\operatorname{Im}(F^{i}\omega(M)\cap\omega(W_{n}M)\to\omega(\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M))=F^{i}\omega(\operatorname{gr}_{n}^{W}M),

the latter equality following from
4.12.1
.

4.1.1.
Overview of §
4

Most of §
4
is devoted to explaining what is meant by such a splitting; this is ultimately defined as a
Hodge-filtered weight-splitting
in Definition
4.13
.

More precisely, in §
4.2
have a mostly informal discussion of the concept of splitting of a filtration in the setting of vector spaces. In §
4.3
-
4.9
, we discuss technicalities on graded and filtered fiber functors. In §
4.10
-
4.15
, we spell out that formalism more explicitly in the still-abstract setup from §
2
of a strictly-filtered Tannakian category with a Hodge-filtered fiber functor.

Finally, in §
4.16
-
4.18
, we prove the main theorem, which is a multi-step reduction to a form of Levi’s Theorem.

4.2.
Some linear algebra

We fix a field
k
of characteristic
0
. We begin with a discussion of some elementary linear algebra. Then, starting in
4.3
, we will upgrade this discussion in two ways: (1) Instead of working with vector spaces (and their subspaces) over
k
we will work with finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules (and locally split submodules) for
S
an arbitrary
k
-scheme. (2) Instead of working with individual
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules we will work with a neutral Tannakian category over
k
and fiber functors with various extra structures (filtrations, gradings) over
S
.

4.2.1.

By a
filtered vector space
we mean an object of
\FilVect_{k}
as in
2.1.7
, i.e., a finite-dimensional vector space with a finite (equivalently, separated and exhaustive) filtration. We may use an increasing filtration (usually denoted
W
) or a decreasing filtration (usually denoted
F
). As in
2.1.12
, we may convert an increasing filtration
W
to a decreasing filtration
F
by setting
F^{i}=W_{-i}
. This operation defines an isomorphism of symmetric monoidal categories. In
4.2.2
-
4.10
, we will for the most part work agnostically with
filtered vector spaces
without specifying if our filtrations are increasing or decreasing.

4.2.2.

We distinguish two notions of
splitting
of a filtration. If
V
is a filtered vector space, a
parabolic splitting
is an isomorphism of filtered vector spaces

\operatorname{fil}\operatorname{gr}V\overset{\Psi}{\simeq}V.

A
unipotent splitting
is a linearly independent collection
\{V_{i}\}_{i}
of subspaces
V_{i}\subset V
(i.e. for vectors
v_{i}\in V_{i}
,
\sum_{i}v_{i}=0
implies
v_{i}=0
for all
i
) such that the filtration is equal to the filtration induced by
\{V_{i}\}_{i}
.

Every unipotent splitting
\{V_{i}\}_{i}
gives rise to a parabolic splitting given by

V=\sum_{i}V_{i}\simeq\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}.

This map from unipotent splittings to parabolic splittings is evidently injective with image given by those filtered isomorphisms
\Psi
as in (P) for which the composition

\operatorname{gr}V\xrightarrow[\ref{defn:functors_fil_gr}(*)]{\sim}\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{fil}\operatorname{gr}V\xrightarrow[\operatorname{gr}\Psi]{\sim}\operatorname{gr}V

is
\id_{\operatorname{gr}V}
.

Conversely, a parabolic splitting
\Psi
gives rise to a unipotent splitting given by setting

V_{i}\coloneqq\Psi(\operatorname{gr}_{i}V).

For example, if
V
is concentrated in filtered degree
0
, then every automorphism of
V
is a parabolic splitting, but there’s only one unipotent splitting, given by
V_{0}\coloneqq V
.

4.2.3.

We let
\GrVect_{k}\coloneqq\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}(\Vect_{k})
in the sense of
2.2.2
. When the field
k
stays fixed, we allow ourselves to drop the subscript ‘
k
’.

By a
graded and filtered vector space
we mean a vector space equipped with a grading and a filtration subject to
no
compatibility conditions. A graded and filtered vector space is a filtered object of the category of graded vector spaces if and only if it is a graded object of the category of filtered vector spaces, as we now explain.

Suppose
V=\bigcup_{i}F^{i}V
is a filtered object of
\GrVect
. This means we’re given gradings

F^{i}V=\bigoplus_{j}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}F^{i}V

preserved by the inclusions

F^{i}V\subset F^{i+1}V.

Setting
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V\coloneqq\bigcup_{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}F^{i}V
gives a graded and filtered vector space structure on
V
.

We may retrieve the filtered object of
\GrVect
from the underlying graded and filtered vector space via

\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}F^{i}V=\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V\cap F^{i}V.

Furthermore, a graded and filtered vector space comes from a filtered object of
\GrVect
if and only if for each
i
, the subspaces

\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V\cap F^{i}V\subset F^{i}V

span
F^{i}V
.

Suppose now that

V=\bigoplus_{j}\operatorname{gr}_{j}^{W}V=\bigoplus_{j}V_{j}

is a graded object of
\FilVect
. This means we’re given (separated, exhaustive) filtrations

V_{j}=\bigcup_{i}F^{i}(V_{j})

which are subject to
no
conditions. We may form the
underlying
vector space with filtration and grading by endowing
V
with the direct sum filtration

F^{i}V=\bigoplus_{j}F^{i}(V_{j}).

We then have

F^{i}V\cap V_{j}=\left(\bigoplus_{j}F^{i}V_{j}\right)\cap V_{j}=F^{i}(V_{j})

which shows that the graded object (*) is uniquely determined by the underlying filtered and graded vector space. Furthermore, a graded and filtered vector space

\bigcup_{i}F^{i}V=V=\bigoplus_{j}V_{j}

comes from a graded object of
\FilVect
if and only if the filtration
is
the direct sum filtration associated to the induced filtrations

V_{j}=\bigcup_{i}(F^{i}V\cap V_{j})

of the graded pieces. To conclude:

Lemma 4.2.4
.

A graded and filtered vector space
determines a graded object of
\FilVect_{k}
if and only it it determines a filtered object of
\GrVect_{k}
.

4.2.5.
Proof of Lemma
4.2.4

The conditions described in
4.2.3
are tautologically the same.

4.2.6.
Definition

We will say the filtration and the grading are
compatible
if they satisfy these conditions.

4.2.7.
Corollary

Let
\bigcup F^{i}V=V=\bigoplus V_{j}
be a graded and filtered vector space. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The filtration and grading are compatible.

(2)

For each
i
, the intersections
V_{j}\cap F^{i}V
span
F^{i}V
.

(3)

The filtration
F
of
V
is the direct sum of the filtrations

V_{j}=\bigcup_{i}(V_{j}\cap F^{i}V)

of the subspaces
V_{j}
.

(4)

The
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action associated to the grading preserves the filtration.

Indeed, as noted above, (2) and (3) are tautologically the same. On the other hand, (4) is clearly equivalent to the condition that
V
determine a filtered object of
\Rep{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\simeq\GrVect
which, as noted above, is equivalent to the other conditions.

4.2.8.

Let
V
be a Hodge and weight filtered vector space (i.e. equipped with an increasing filtration
W
and a decreasing filtration
F
both assumed finite). The Hodge filtration on
V
endows
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(V)
with a Hodge filtration given by defining
F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V
to be the image of

F^{i}V\cap W_{j}V\subset W_{j}V\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V

and then taking the direct-sum filtration. By construction, the weight grading and Hodge filtration on
\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
are compatible.

Lemma 4.2.9
.

A unipotent splitting
\Psi\colon\operatorname{gr}^{W}V\simeq V
of the weight filtration preserves Hodge filtrations if and only if the associated representation

\chi\colon{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\to\operatorname{GL}V

lands in
P(F)\coloneqq\{g\in\operatorname{GL}V\,\mid\,g(F^{i}V)=F^{i}V\,\forall\,i\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

4.2.10.
Proof of Lemma
4.2.9

By
4.2.7
,
\chi
lands in
P(F)
iff the grading on
V
determined by
\Psi
is compatible with the Hodge filtration on
V
.

Since the Hodge filtration on
\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
is compatible with the grading, this compatibility holds for
V
if
\Psi
preserves the Hodge filtration.

Conversely, suppose the grading associated to
\Psi
and the Hodge filtration on
V
are compatible. Let
V_{j}\coloneqq\Psi(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V)
, and for
\alpha\in V
, let
\alpha_{j}
denote the projection of
\alpha
onto
V_{j}
, so that
\alpha_{j}\in V_{j}
for all
j
, and
\alpha=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}
.

Suppose
\alpha\in F^{i}V
. The compatibility implies that
\alpha_{j}\in F^{i}V
for all
j
. Thus
\alpha_{j}\in F^{i}V\cap V_{j}\subseteq F^{i}V\cap W_{j}V
.

The subspace
F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V
is defined as the image of
F^{i}V\cap W_{j}V
under the projection
W_{j}V\twoheadrightarrow W_{j}V/W_{j-1}V=\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V
, so the image of
\alpha_{j}\in W_{j}V
is in
F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V
.

But the composition
V_{j}\subseteq W_{j}V\twoheadrightarrow W_{j}V/W_{j-1}V=\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V
is the same as
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Psi^{-1}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{V_{j}}}
. Thus
\Psi^{-1}(\alpha_{j})\in F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V\subseteq F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
.

It follows that

\Psi^{-1}(\alpha)=\Psi^{-1}\left(\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}\right)=\sum_{j}\Psi^{-1}(\alpha_{j})\in F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V.

In other words,
\Psi^{-1}
sends
F^{i}V
into
F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
. Since

\dim F^{i}V=\sum_{j}\dim(F^{i}V\cap W_{j}V)/(F^{i}V\cap W_{j-1}V)=\sum_{j}\dim F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{j}V=\dim F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V,

\Psi^{-1}
induces an isomorphism
F^{i}V\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
, so that
\Psi(F^{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}V)=F^{i}V
, and we are done.

4.3.
Gradings and filtrations in families

We recall some notation from
[
116
, §3]
. If
S
is a scheme, we define
\LF(S)
to be the category of finite locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules (
[
107
,
Tag 01C6
(2)]
), also known as ‘vector bundles’.

4.3.1.
Notation

We let
\GrQCoh(S)
and
\FilQCoh(S)
denote
\Gr(\QCoh(S))
and
\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}(\QCoh(S))
, respectively.

4.3.2.
Definition

We define
\GrLF(S)
to be the subcategory of
\mathcal{M}\in\Gr(\LF(S))
for which
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{M}_{i}\in\LF(S)
.
16
16

16

This can be larger than
\Gr^{\mathrm{fin}}(\LF(S))
, for example if
S
has infinitely many connected components.
By
[
116
, Remark 3.2]
,
\GrLF(S)=\GrQCoh(S)_{\mathrm{rig}}
(c.f. §
1.8
).

4.3.3.
Definition

As in
[
116
, §4.1]
, we define
\FilLF(S)
to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{s-ex}}(\LF(S))
for which the inclusion
\mathrm{fil}^{n}(\mathcal{M})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}
is locally split
17
17

17

For
S
affine, it is in fact split, so
\operatorname{fil}(\operatorname{gr}(M))\simeq M
for all
M\in\FilLF(S)
.
for all
n
. By
[
116
, Lemma 4.2]
,
\FilLF(S)=\FilQCoh(S)_{\mathrm{rig}}
. We set
\FilLF^{\mathrm{fin}}(S)\coloneqq\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\LF(S))\cap\FilLF(S)
.

4.3.4.

We have the exact tensor functors
\operatorname{fil}
and
\operatorname{gr}
18
18

18

Since
\LF(S)
is not even pre-abelian,
\operatorname{gr}
lands a priori in
\GrQCoh(S)
; however, the condition of being locally split ensures the graded piece are in
\LF(S)
.
as in
2.2.5
between the categories
\GrLF(S)
and
\FilLF(S)
. We also have the usual functors
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{fil}},\operatorname{fil}^{n},\operatorname{gr}^{n}
(
2.1.4
) and
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}
(
2.2.3
) landing in
\LF(S)
.

4.3.5.

Note that for a field
F
,
\LF(F)\coloneqq\LF(\operatorname{Spec}{F})=\Vect_{F}
. Similarly,
\FilLF(F)=\FilVect_{F}
, and
\GrLF(F)=\GrVect_{F}
.

Definition 4.4
.

For the rest of §
4
, let
\mathcal{T}
be a Tannakian category over
k
.

A
graded (resp. filtered) fiber functor over
S
is an exact
k
-linear tensor functor

\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\GrQCoh(S)\hskip 14.22636pt(\mathrm{resp.}\,\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilQCoh(S))

4.4.1.

As in
[
116
, Remarks 3.2, 4.5]
, any graded (resp. filtered) fiber functor takes values in
\GrLF(S)
(resp.
\FilLF(S)
) by
[
44
, 2.7]
. C.f. the footnote in
B.1.2
.

4.4.2.

If
\phi
is a graded (resp. filtered) fiber functor, then
\operatorname{fil}\circ\phi
(resp.
\operatorname{gr}\circ\phi
) is filtered (resp. graded).

If
\phi
is graded, then by
2.2.6
,

\operatorname{gr}\circ\operatorname{fil}\circ\phi\simeq\phi

canonically.

4.5.
Subgroups associated to a filtration

In §
4.5
-
4.17
, we discuss some general results and definitions regarding filtered fiber functors. While our notation mostly reflects decreasing filtrations, we recall as in
2.1.12
that results and definitions apply equally in either case, and we will apply this material both to (decreasing) Hodge-filtered fiber functors
\omega_{H}
(Definition
2.6.1
) and (increasing) weight-filtered fiber functors
\omega^{W}
(Definition
4.10
).

4.5.1.

Given
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
over
k
, a graded (resp. filtered) fiber functor
\phi
over
S
may be base-extended to a graded (resp. filtered) fiber functor over
S^{\prime}
in an evident way:

(\phi)_{S^{\prime}}(E)=f^{*}\phi(E)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all }E\in\mathcal{T}.

4.5.2.

For any two graded (resp. filtered) fiber functors
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
on
\mathcal{T}
over
S
, we let
\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
be the presheaf

\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}\to\mathrm{Sets}

which sends
S^{\prime}\to S
to the set of tensor isomorphisms

(\phi_{1})_{S^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\phi_{2})_{S^{\prime}}.

By
[
116
, Theorems 3.11, 4.7]
,
\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
is representable by an affine
S
-scheme, which is flat in the graded case.

4.5.3.

Let
\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilVect_{S}
be a filtered fiber functor, and let
\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq\operatorname{gr}\circ\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\GrVect_{S}
. Following Ziegler
[
116
, Definition 4.7]
, we define the associated
parabolic Galois group

P(\phi)
(in terms of points with values in an arbitrary
S
-scheme
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
) by

P(\phi)(S^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathrm{Aut}\,^{\otimes}(f^{*}\circ\phi),

and the
Levi Galois group

L(\phi)
by

L(\phi)(S^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathrm{Aut}\,^{\otimes}(f^{*}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}})=\mathrm{Aut}\,^{\otimes}(\operatorname{gr}\circ f^{*}\circ\phi).

4.5.4.

Taking the associated graded of an isomorphism of filtered objects provides a morphism

\mathrm{ss}\colon P(\phi)\to L(\phi),

and we define the
unipotent Galois group
as

U(\phi)=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathrm{ss});

By
4.5.2
, these groups are all representable by affine
S
-groups.

4.6.
Splittings of filtered fiber functors

Let

\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilLF(S)

be a filtered fiber functor over a
k
-scheme
S
. We use the
\otimes
-functors
\mathrm{fil}
and
\operatorname{gr}
to define two notions of splitting.

A
parabolic splitting
of
\phi
is a
\otimes
-isomorphism of filtered fiber functors

\mathrm{fil}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}\overset{\Psi}{\simeq}\phi

A
unipotent splitting
(also simply a
splitting
as in
[
116
, Definition 4.9(i)]
) is a graded fiber functor

\gamma\colon\mathcal{T}\to\GrLF(S)

such that

\phi=\mathrm{fil}\circ\gamma.

4.6.1.

Equivalently, a unipotent splitting is an equivalence class of parabolic splittings modulo the left action of
L(\phi)
acting by automorphisms of filtered fiber functors on
\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
, as we now explain.

A parabolic splitting
\Psi
gives rise to a unipotent splitting
\gamma
given by
\gamma(E)\coloneqq\phi(E)
with grading given by

\operatorname{gr}_{i}\gamma(E)\coloneqq\Psi_{E}(\operatorname{gr}_{i}\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)).

If we precompose
\Psi
with an automorphism of
\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
, the resulting graded fiber functor
\gamma
is the same. Thus we get a map from parabolic splittings modulo
L(\phi)
to unipotent splittings.

If parabolic splittings
\Psi,\Psi^{\prime}
give rise to the same
\gamma
, then
\Psi^{-1}\circ\Psi^{\prime}
, a priori only an automorphism of
\mathrm{fil}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
, in fact preserves the grading and is thus an automorphsim of
\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
; i.e., an element of
L(\phi)
. Thus this map is injective.

To see that the map is surjective, note that a unipotent splitting gives rise to a parabolic splitting given by the isomorphism of filtered fiber functors

\mathrm{fil}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}=\mathrm{fil}\circ\operatorname{gr}\circ\phi=\mathrm{fil}\circ\operatorname{gr}\circ\mathrm{fil}\circ\gamma\overset{\ref{sssec:fil_gr_of_fiber_functors}}{\simeq}\mathrm{fil}\circ\gamma=\phi.

Thus the concept of a
splittable
filtered fiber functor (
[
116
, Definition 4.9(ii)]
) is independent of which notion of splitting we choose.

4.6.2.

The proof of surjectivity in
4.6.1
in fact provides a canonical section of the projection from parabolic splittings to unipotent splittings. The image is given by those
\otimes
-isomorphisms of filtered fiber functors whose composition with
\operatorname{gr}
is the identity automorphism of
\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
.

4.6.3.

Parabolic splittings forms a pseudo-torsor
19
19

19

torsor or empty set with trivial action
under the parabolic Galois group
P(\phi)
; unipotent splittings form a pseudo-torsor under the unipotent Galois group
U(\phi)
.

4.7.
Graded Galois groups

Let

\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilLF(S)

be a filtered fiber functor. In addition to the parabolic and Levi Galois groups, as well as the usual Galois (or
fundamental
) group

G(\phi)=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{forg}_{\mathrm{fil}}\circ\phi),

we define the
(usual and parabolic) graded Galois groups of
\mathcal{T}
at
\phi
by

G^{\mathrm{gr}}(\phi)=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{forg}_{\mathrm{gr}}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}})

P^{\mathrm{gr}}(\phi)=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\operatorname{fil}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}})

(functorial in
S^{\prime}\to S
in the usual way). Note that
L(\phi)\subseteq G^{\operatorname{gr}}(\phi)
.

4.8.
Associated cocharacter

Let

\phi\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilVect_{S}

be a filtered fiber functor. We define the
associated cocharacter

\chi(\phi)\colon{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\to L(\phi)

as follows. We work with points with values in an arbitrary affine
S
-scheme
\operatorname{Spec}{R}
. Fix
z\in R^{\times}={\mathbb{G}_{m}}(R)
and
E\in\mathcal{T}
. Then

\phi_{R}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)=\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\otimes_{S}R=\bigoplus_{i}\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)_{i}\otimes_{S}R

is a graded
R
-module, and we have
z
act on it via scalar multiplication by
z^{i}
on the
i
th summand. Evidently,
\chi(\phi)(z)
preserves gradings, and hence belongs to
L(\phi)(R)
.

Lemma 4.8.1
.

The cocharacter
\chi(\phi)
associated to a filtered fiber functor
\phi
is
central
in the Levi Galois group
L(\phi)
; moreover,
L(\phi)
is its centralizer in
\Gr^{\operatorname{gr}}(\phi)
.

Proof.

The structure of a grading is the same as a
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action. Thus an automorphism preserves the grading iff it commutes with the
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action, so
L(\phi)
is the centralizer of
\chi({\mathbb{G}_{m}})
.

∎

4.9.
Diagram of groups associated to a filtered fiber functor

In addition to the map
\mathrm{ss}
defined in
4.5.4
, there is a map
\mathrm{ss}^{\operatorname{gr}}\colon P^{\operatorname{gr}}(\phi)\to L(\phi)=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\phi^{\operatorname{gr}})
defined by applying the functor
\operatorname{gr}
to an automorphism of
\operatorname{fil}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
and using the isomorphism
\operatorname{gr}\circ\mathrm{fil}\simeq\mathrm{id}_{\GrLF(S)}
. The functor
\operatorname{fil}
similarly defines a section
r\colon L(\phi)\to P^{\operatorname{gr}}(\phi)
, fitting into a diagram of groups over
S
:

with
\chi(\phi)
central in
L(\phi)
.

Definition 4.10
.

Any fiber functor
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\to\QCoh(S)
on a strictly filtered Tannakian category
(\mathcal{T},\xi_{W})
over
k
upgrades canonically to a(n increasing) filtered fiber functor
\omega^{W}
defined by

\omega^{W}\coloneqq\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\omega)\circ\xi_{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}(\mathcal{T})\to\operatorname{Fil}^{\mathrm{fin}}\QCoh(S)\subseteq\FilQCoh(S)

(c.f.
2.4.5
), or on objects by
W_{i}\omega^{W}(E)\coloneqq\omega(W_{i}E)
; we define a
weight-filtered fiber functor
to be a filtered fiber functor of this sort.

We also set

\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq\operatorname{gr}\circ\omega^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\GrLF(S)

\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}_{\mathrm{gr}}\circ\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\LF(S).

If
\phi=\omega^{W}
, then
\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}=\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
, and
\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}=\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\phi^{\operatorname{gr}}
in the notation of
4.5.3
.

4.10.1.

For such
\omega^{W}
,
P(\omega^{W})=G(\omega^{W})=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)
, and
P^{\operatorname{gr}}(\omega^{W})=G^{\operatorname{gr}}(\omega^{W})=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega^{\operatorname{gr}})
.

4.10.2.
Remark

Conversely, suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian category over
k
, and
\omega^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilVect_{k}
is any (increasing) filtered fiber functor for which
P(\omega^{W})=G(\omega^{W})
. Then there is a unique strict filtration on
\mathcal{T}
for which
\omega^{W}
is the associated weight-filtered fiber functor.

4.10.3.

Let

\omega^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilLF(S)

be a weight-filtered fiber functor. Since
\omega^{W}
is exact, for each
E\in\mathcal{T}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we have an exact sequence

0\to\omega^{W}(W_{i-1}E)\to\omega^{W}(W_{i}E)\to\omega^{W}(\operatorname{gr}_{i}^{W}E)\to 0

in
\FilLF(S)
, from which a canonical isomorphism

\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}\omega^{W}E\simeq\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}E).

and thus

\displaystyle\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\operatorname{gr}\circ\omega^{W}(E)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{gr}_{i}\omega^{W}(E)

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{\simeq}

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}E)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\omega\left(\bigoplus_{i}\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{i}E\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E),

which are in
E
and thus provide a canonical isomorphism of tensor functors

(10)

\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\simeq\omega\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}

In fact, we can replace the RHS by
\omega^{W}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}
to get a functor valued in
\FilLF(S)
and then note that this upgrades to an isomorphism

(11)

\operatorname{fil}\circ\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}\simeq\omega^{W}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}

of filtered fiber functors since
W_{n}
of either side is
\bigoplus_{i\leq n}\operatorname{gr}_{i}\omega^{W}(E)\simeq\bigoplus_{i\leq n}\omega(\operatorname{gr}_{i}^{W}E)
by
2.5.8
(
3
).

We will often use (
10
) and (
11
) to commute
\omega^{W}
and
\operatorname{gr}^{W}
without further mention.

4.10.4.
Remark

For
E\in\mathcal{T}
, there are two natural Galois actions on
\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\simeq\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
. The first is via the natural action of
G^{\operatorname{gr}}
on
\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)
, while the second is via the natural action of
G
on
\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
.

The actions are related in the following way. The action of
G
on
\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
is trivial when restricted to
U^{W}
and thus factors through an action of
L^{W}
on
\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
. There is also an action of
L^{W}
on
\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)
via
L^{W}\hookrightarrow G^{\operatorname{gr}}
. Then the isomorphism (
11
) intertwines these
L^{W}
-actions.

In this way, when
k^{\prime}=k
, one may actually view
\operatorname{gr}^{W}
as the operation of taking the fiber at
\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}
, letting
G
act via
G=P^{W}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ss}}L^{W}\hookrightarrow G^{\operatorname{gr}}
, then viewing this vector space with
G
-action as an object of
\mathcal{T}
via
\omega
. C.f.
5.2.1
-
5.2.2
.

4.10.5.
Remark

If
\mathcal{T}
is weight-filtered, then
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
lands in the full subcategory
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
of semisimple objects. Conversely, if
M\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
, then
M\simeq\operatorname{gr}^{W}M
, so
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
is the essential image of
\operatorname{gr}^{W}
. The isomorphism
2.5.8
(
5
) thus implies that

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\operatorname{gr}^{W}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}}}\simeq\id_{\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}}

4.10.6.
Remark

Every weight-filtered fiber functor over
k^{\prime}
on a strictly filtered Tannakian category over
k
admits a (
k^{\prime}
-rational) splitting. Indeed, this is a special case of Ziegler
[
116
, Theorem 4.15]
. However, under our extra assumption that the filtration exists on the level of the category itself, this is much easier to prove, as we now explain. Classical Tannaka duality guarantees the existence of an isomorphism of fiber functors

\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}\circ\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}=\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\overset{\Psi^{\circ}}{\simeq}\omega=\mathrm{forg}_{\mathrm{fil}}\circ\omega^{W}

fpqc locally. Since the filtration exists on the level of the category itself,
\Psi^{\circ}
respects the filtration and hence upgrades canonically to an isomorphism
\Psi
of filtered fiber functors, i.e. to a parabolic splitting. As we’ve seen, this gives us a unipotent splitting fpqc locally. Since unipotent splittings form a torsor under a prounipotent group, this guarantees the existence of a
k^{\prime}
-rational unipotent splitting.

4.11.
Notation

It will be convenient for us to decorate ‘
\FilVect
’ and ’
\FilLF
’ so as to specify a symbol to be used to denote the associated filtrations and also to indicate whether filtrations are increasing or decreasing: a superscript
X
as in ‘
\mathrm{Fil}^{X}
’ or ‘
\FilLF^{X}
’ indicates increasing filtrations denoted by
X
; a subscript
X
as in ‘
\mathrm{Fil}_{X}
’ or ‘
\FilLF_{X}
’ indicates decreasing filtrations denoted by
X
. If
\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian category, by a
Hodge-filtered fiber functor
we mean a filtered fiber functor which takes values in
\FilLF_{F}
. If
\mathcal{T}
is a strictly filtered Tannakian category, by a
weight-filtered fiber functor
we mean the filtered fiber functor with values in
\FilLF^{W}
induced by an ordinary fiber functor
\mathcal{T}\to\LF
. We decorate symbols such as
\operatorname{fil}
,
\operatorname{gr}
,
\mathrm{forg}
(
2.1.4
,
2.2.3
,
2.2.5
) and others with
W
and
F
.

We set
\FilLF^{W}_{F}(S)
to be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fil}^{W}(\FilLF_{F}(S))
for which forgetting the Hodge filtration lands in
\FilLF^{W}(S)
and for which the inclusions
W_{i}\subseteq W_{i+1}
are strict for the Hodge filtration. We let
\operatorname{GrFilLF}_{F}^{W}(S)
be the full subcategory of
\Gr^{W}(\FilLF_{F}(S))
for which
\mathrm{forg}_{\operatorname{gr}}^{W}
is also in
\FilLF_{F}(S)
. Note that both are additive tensor categories but not abelian.

4.12.
Weight and hodge together

Given a Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega_{H}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilLF_{F}(S)
, we may set
W_{i}\omega^{W}_{H}(E)\coloneqq\omega_{H}(W_{i}E)
as in Definition
4.10
to get an object of
\FilLF^{W}_{F}(S)
.

We can then apply
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\colon\FilLF^{W}_{H}(S)\to\operatorname{GrFilLF}_{F}^{W}(S)
, with Hodge filtration determined by
4.2.8
(in other words, we form the associated graded by taking quotients in the category
\mathrm{Fil}_{F}\LF(S)
), to
\omega_{H}^{W}
to get a functor
\omega_{H}^{W,\operatorname{gr}}
. We set
\omega_{H}^{\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq\mathrm{forg}^{W}\circ\omega_{H}^{W,\operatorname{gr}}
.

Lemma 4.12.1
.

The isomorphism
\operatorname{fil}^{W}(\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}(E))\simeq\omega^{W}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
of
4.10.3
(
11
) preserves the Hodge filtration, i.e., upgrades to an isomorphism
\operatorname{fil}^{W}\omega_{H}^{W,\operatorname{gr}}(E)\simeq\omega_{H}^{W}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
in
\FilLF^{W}_{F}(S)
.

4.12.2.
Proof

Let
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. By exactness of
\omega_{H}
, the map
\omega_{H}(W_{j}E)\to\omega_{H}(E)
is strict, so
F^{i}\omega(W_{j}E)=\omega(W_{j})\cap F^{i}\omega(W)
. Since
0\to W_{j-1}E\to W_{j}E\to\operatorname{gr}_{j}^{W}E\to 0
is exact in
\mathcal{T}
, the sequence

0\to\omega_{H}(W_{j-1}E)\to\omega_{H}(W_{j}E)\to\omega_{H}(\operatorname{gr}_{j}^{W}E)\to 0

is exact in
\FilLF_{F}(S)
. Thus
F^{i}\omega(\operatorname{gr}_{j}^{W}E)
is the image of
F^{i}\omega(W_{j}E)=\omega(W_{j})\cap F^{i}\omega(W)
, which agrees with
4.2.8
and thus
4.12
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a strictly filtered Tannakian category over a field
k
and let

\omega_{H}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\FilLF_{F}(S)

be a Hodge-filtered fiber functor over a
k
-scheme
S
.

Recall we have
\omega
valued in
\LF(S)
as in
2.6.1
,
\omega^{W},\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}},\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}
valued in
\FilLF^{W}(S)
,
\GrLF^{W}(S)
,
\LF(S)
as in
4.10
, and
\omega_{H}^{W}
,
\omega_{H}^{W,\operatorname{gr}}
and
\omega_{H}^{\operatorname{gr}}
valued in
\operatorname{Fil}^{W}_{F}\LF(S)
,
\Gr^{W}\operatorname{Fil}_{F}\LF(S)
,
\operatorname{Fil}_{F}\LF(S)
as in
4.12
.

Any
\otimes
-isomorphism as in the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Fil}_{F}\LF(S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{H}^{\operatorname{gr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

upgrades canonically to a
\otimes
-isomorphism as in the following diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\mathrm{forg}^{W}\circ\Psi^{W}=\Psi
.

Now
\mathrm{forg}_{F}\circ\Psi^{W}
is an isomorphism from
\operatorname{fil}^{W}\circ\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}
to
\omega^{W}
. By a
Hodge-filtered weight-splitting
, we mean a
\otimes
-isomorphism
\Psi
as above, for which
\mathrm{forg}_{F}\circ\Psi^{W}
is a unipotent splitting of
\omega^{W}
; recall this means that its further composition with
\operatorname{gr}^{W}
is equal to the identity natural automorphism of
\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}
.

For the remainder of §
4
, we fix a weight-filtered Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
and a Hodge-filtered fiber functor
\omega_{H}
over
k^{\prime}
.

4.14.
Diagram of groups associated to a Hodge-filtered fiber functor

Associated to the filtered fiber functors
\omega_{H}
and
\omega^{W}
we have
Levi
,
Parabolic
,
unipotent
, and
graded
Galois groups; we decorate accordingly. Specifically, we set

•

G=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega)

•

G^{\operatorname{gr}}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W})=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{\operatorname{gr}})=P^{W,\operatorname{gr}}

•

P^{W}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{W})=G

;

P_{H}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega_{H})=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega_{H}^{W})

•

L^{W}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}})=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega^{W}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}}})

•

U^{W}=\operatorname{Ker}(P^{W}\to L^{W})

as in
4.5.3
. We also have the central cocharacter
\chi^{W}\colon{\mathbb{G}}_{m}\to L^{W}
associated to
\omega^{W}
(
4.8
). We let
U^{W}_{H}
be the kernel of the induced map

P_{H}\hookrightarrow G=P^{W}\twoheadrightarrow L^{W}.

These fit into a commutative diagram of groups over
k^{\prime}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U^{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U_{H}^{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P^{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{G}_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L^{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G^{\operatorname{gr}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ss}^{\operatorname{gr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi^{W}}

4.14.1.
Remark

The condition of
\mathcal{T}
being
weight-filtered
(
2.5.9
) rather than just
strictly filtered
(
2.5.3
) ensures that
L^{W}
is reductive, although we will not need this until §
5
.

4.15.
Example: non-CM elliptic curve

Consider
\mathbf{SM}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathbb{Q}},E)
(
3.8
) for
E
an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
without complex multiplication, and let

\omega_{H}^{\mathrm{dR}}\colon\mathbf{SM}_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathbb{Q}},E)\to\FilVect_{\mathbb{Q}}

and
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
as in
3.3.3
.

We have

L^{W}=\operatorname{GL}(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(h^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}}(E)))=\operatorname{GL}(H^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(E))\simeq\operatorname{GL}_{2},

which follows either by the fact that the Mumford–Tate group of
h_{1}(E)
is
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
(
[
89
, 5.4]
) or by Serre’s Open Image Theorem (
[
105
]
).

Then the image of
P_{H}
in
L^{W}
, denoted
F^{0}\operatorname{GL}_{2}
is the Borel (hence parabolic) subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}(H^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(E))
fixing the subspace generated by a nonzero invariant differential form on
E
.

Proposition 4.16
.

The affine
k^{\prime}
-group
U^{W}_{H}
is prounipotent.

Proof.

Evidently, it’s enough to show that
U^{W}
is prounipotent. This has been noted before;
see, for instance,
[
69
, §3]
. One way to see this is to note that the left-regular representation of
G
provides a faithful Ind-unipotent representation of
U^{W}
.
∎

Lemma 4.16.1
.

For a
k^{\prime}
-scheme
S
, let
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(S)
denote the set of Hodge-filtered weight-splittings of
(\omega_{H})_{S}
. Then
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})
forms an effective
21
21

21

representable by a scheme
pseudo-torsor under
U^{W}_{H}
over
\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
for the fpqc topology.

Proof of Lemma
4.16.1
.

A priori this is a presheaf on
k^{\prime}
-schemes, but we claim it is an affine
k^{\prime}
-scheme. Indeed,
\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\omega_{H}^{\operatorname{gr}},\omega_{H})
is an affine
k^{\prime}
-scheme by
4.5.2
, and
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})
is the fiber of
\id_{\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}}
under the map
\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\omega_{H}^{\operatorname{gr}},\omega_{H})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{forg}_{F}}\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\omega^{\operatorname{gr}},\omega)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{gr}^{W}}\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{\operatorname{gr}})
, thus a closed subscheme.

Next, note that
U^{W}_{H}=U^{W}_{H}(\omega)
is the kernel of the map

P_{H}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{W}_{H})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{gr}^{W}\circ\mathrm{forg}_{F}}\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}})=L^{W}.

From this, it is clear that if
a\in\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(S)
and
b\in U^{W}_{H}(S)
, then
b\circ a\in\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(S)
. Similarly, if
a,a^{\prime}\in\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(S)
, then
a^{\prime}\circ a^{-1}\in U^{W}_{H}(S)
. Thus
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})
is a pseudo-torsor (as a sheaf) under
U^{W}_{H}
.

∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is algebraic (
B.1.6
). Then the ranks of
P_{H}
,
G
,
P^{W}
,
L^{W}
, and
G^{\operatorname{gr}}
are all equal.

Proof.

The assumption on
\mathcal{T}
implies that all the groups in
4.14
(*) are algebraic (i.e., of finite type), so they have a well-defined notion of rank.

Let
M
be a generator of
\mathcal{T}
. Then
P_{H}
is the subgroup of
G
fixing the Hodge filtration on
\omega_{H}(M)
, so
P_{H}
is parabolic, and therefore
r\coloneqq\rk{P_{H}}=\rk{G}
. Since
G=P^{W}
,
\rk{P^{W}}=\rk{G}=r
. Since
G\simeq G^{\operatorname{gr}}
, we have
\rk{G^{\operatorname{gr}}}=\rk{G}=r
.

Since the kernel of
\mathrm{ss}
is unipotent,
\rk{\mathrm{ss}(P^{W})}=\rk{P^{W}}=r
. Since a subgroup has smaller or equal rank, we have
r=\rk{\mathrm{ss}(P^{W})}\leq\rk{L^{W}}\leq\rk{G^{\operatorname{gr}}}=r
, so in fact
\rk{L^{W}}=r
.
∎

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero,
\mathcal{T}
a strictly filtered Tannakian category over
k
,
k^{\prime}
an overfield of
k
, and

\omega_{H}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathrm{Fil}_{F}\Vect_{k^{\prime}}

a Hodge-filtered fiber functor. Then
\omega_{H}
admits a
k^{\prime}
-rational Hodge-filtered weight-splitting; in other words,
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(k^{\prime})
is nonempty.

The proof spans
4.18.1
–
4.18.2
. In the case
\mathcal{T}
is algebraic, the result reduces to a form of Levi’s Theorem (
[
116
, Theorem 5.5]
), as described in
4.18.1
. A similar idea appears in
[
71
, Lemma 3.1]
and
[
74
, §10.2]
, although without the condition of respecting the Hodge filtration; c.f. also
[
73
, Proposition 3.1]
. We reduce to this case in
4.18.2
.

4.18.1.

Assume that
\mathcal{T}
and hence
G
is algebraic. Let
T
be a maximal torus of
P_{H}
. Since
\rk{P_{H}}=\rk{G}
,
T
is also a maximal torus of
G=P^{W}
. Furthermore,
T\cap U^{W}
is trivial, so
\mathrm{ss}(T)\simeq T
is a subtorus of
L^{W}
of rank
\dim T=\rk{P_{H}}\overset{\ref{prop:ranks_equal}}{=}\rk{L^{W}}
. Therefore
\mathrm{ss}(T)
is a maximal torus of
L^{W}
. Since
\chi^{W}
is central (Lemma
4.8.1
), its image lands in
\mathrm{ss}(T)
.

We may therefore lift
\chi^{W}
to a cocharacter of
T
and therefore of
P_{H}
:

\tilde{\chi}^{W}\colon{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\to P_{H}

Since
\tilde{\chi}^{W}
, regarded as a cocharacter of
P^{W}
, lifts
\chi^{W}
, it splits the weight filtration by
[
116
, Lemma 4.11]
. Since it lands in
P_{H}
, the splitting preserves Hodge filtrations by Lemma
4.2.9
.

4.18.2.

Next, following
[
116
, §5.5]
we argue indirectly that the same holds for general
\mathcal{T}
. (Meanwhile, we avoid the main issues dealt with there by restricting attention to
k
of characteristic zero.) The collection
I
of algebraic full Tannakian subcategories is a
set
since
\mathcal{T}
, by definition, is essentially small. As a matter of notation, we treat
I
as an index set, and we denote the Tannakian category associated to
i\in I
by
\mathcal{T}_{i}
. Let
G_{i}=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega{\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}})
be the Tannakian Galois group of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
. Then
G_{i}
is a finite type quotient of
G
. In particular, the case considered above applies to show that
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega_{H}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}})
possesses a
k^{\prime}
-point.

Whenever
\mathcal{T}_{i}\subset\mathcal{T}_{j}
, there’s an associated map

\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega_{H}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}})\leftarrow\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega_{H}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{j}}}).

These assemble to a diagram of affine
k^{\prime}
-schemes, and a straightforward verification shows that

\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})=\varprojlim_{i\in I}\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega_{H}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}}).

As explained in
[
116
, Lemma 2.9]
,
I
is a lattice and is in particular filtered. Additionally, the base
\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
occurs within the diagram as

\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}=\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega_{H}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\langle\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}\rangle}}).

Hence, Proposition 8.3.8 of EGA IV
[
70
]
applies to show that
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})
is faithfully flat over
k^{\prime}
(equivalently, nonempty). Consequently,
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})
is an fpqc torsor under the prounipotent group
U^{W}_{H}
.

Let
U\coloneqq U^{W}
and
U_{i}\coloneqq U(\omega^{W}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{T}_{i}}})
. In
[
116
, Theorem 4.17]
, it is shown that every fpqc torsor under
U
is trivial (apply the theorem to
\varphi=\omega^{W}
) using an intricate argument involving
\varprojlim^{1}
. This is done by putting compatible decreasing filtrations
\{U^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}},\{U^{\alpha}_{i}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}}
on
U
and
U_{i}
and showing (in Lemma 4.29 of loc.cit.) that

{\varprojlim_{i}}^{1}U^{\alpha}_{i}/U^{\alpha+1}_{i}

is trivial for each
\alpha\geq 1
. The proof uses Theorem 2.17 of loc.cit., for which there are four conditions.

We may put the same filtration on the subgroup
U^{W}_{H}
of
U=U^{W}

, where
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}
is the subgroup acting trivially on
W_{j+\alpha}\omega(M)/W_{j}\omega(M)
for all
M\in\mathcal{T}
and
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We let
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}_{i}
denote the image of
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}
in
G_{i}
; note that it respects the Hodge filtration and acts trivially on the associated graded for the weight filtration. We wish to show that

{\varprojlim_{i}}^{1}(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}_{i}/(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha+1}_{i}

is trivial for each
\alpha\geq 1
. In fact, all the conditions in Theorem 2.17 follow from the corresponding conditions in the proof of Lemma 4.29 of loc.cit.: (i) because it is the same lattice, (ii) because in characteristic
0
, an algebraic subgroup of an abelian unipotent group is again abelian unipotent, and (iii),(iv) because we have defined
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}_{i}
as the image of
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}
.

Then the proof of Lemma 4.30 and thus of Theorem 4.17 of loc.cit. works in the same way (in particular, for fixed
i
, we also have
(U^{W}_{H})^{\alpha}_{i}
trivial for sufficiently large
\alpha
) to show that every fpqc torsor under
U^{W}_{H}
is trivial. Therefore,
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{H})(k^{\prime})
is nonempty, and we are done.

∎

5.
Construction of Unipotent
p
-adic Period Map

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a weight-filtered Tannakian category over
k
,
k^{\prime}_{0}/k
an extension with a theory of weights and finite order automorphism
\phi
fixing
k
, and

\omega_{\phi}\colon\mathcal{T}\to\phi\mathrm{Mod}(k^{\prime}_{0})

a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
(k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
(Definition
2.6.6
).

Then there exists a unique
\otimes
-isomorphism of Frobenius-equivariant fiber functors

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{\phi}\colon\omega_{\phi}^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\omega_{\phi},

where
\omega_{\phi}^{\operatorname{gr}}
has Frobenius action via
\omega_{\phi}^{\operatorname{gr}}\simeq\omega_{\phi}\circ\operatorname{gr}^{W}
(
4.10.3
). In other words, in the notation of
2.6.6
, there exists a unique Tannakian path

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}\colon\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\omega_{0}

such that for each
E\in\mathcal{T}
, the square

\textstyle{\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)}
\textstyle{\omega_{0}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\omega_{0}(E)}

commutes.

The proof spans §
5.2
-
5.4
.

5.2.
Bitorsor of splittings

Since
\phi
does not in general commute with arbitrary
k^{\prime}_{0}
-linear maps, we cannot upgrade
\omega_{\phi}
to a functor from
\mathcal{T}_{k^{\prime}_{0}}
(
B.5.1
) to
\phi
-modules. Since our proof uses Frobenius actions on groups such as

and on the torsor of splittings, we must realize these as objects of
\mathcal{T}
itself. Since our only given fiber functor

is over

rather than

, we will need to use the theory described in §

, beginning in

.

Suppose

is strictly filtered. Let
\omega\coloneqq\omega_{0}=\mathrm{forg}_{\phi}\circ\omega_{\phi}
, and in the notation of §

over the diagonal

.

5.2.1.

If

, we can upgrade this to a bitorsor in

as follows:

In the notation of §
B.3
, we define a left action
\star
of

on

We also denote the conjugation action of

on

and via

on

. This gives rise to group schemes

and

.
22
22

22

We remark that

With these definitions, the composition action

-bitorsor

We now wish to apply

to do this even when

.
