Lemma 5.3
.

In the situation of §
5.2
, suppose furthermore that
\mathcal{T}
is weight-filtered, so that
\mathbb{G}\coloneqq L(\omega_{0})
is reductive and
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}})=0
. Then there’s an isomorphism in
\Pro-\mathcal{T}

a\colon\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\prod_{V\,\mathrm{simple}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V)^{\vee}\otimes V.

Proof.

This is well known when
S_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}{k}
; see for example
[
26
, Proposition 6.1]
or
[
51
, Proposition 3.1.1]
for the dual statement, or
[
73
, (3.4)]
for a related non-dual statement. Let us define the map and check equivariance in general; the map will be an isomorphism since it is after base-change to
S_{0}
.

Given
V
, we define the
V
-component

a_{V}\colon\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}}\to M_{V}\coloneqq\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V)^{\vee}\otimes_{k}V

of
a
as follows. If
V
is the trivial representation, then the Ext group is trivial so the target is terminal. Suppose
V
is not trivial, and let

0\to M_{V}\to E_{V}\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}\to 0

be the universal extension.

As usual, we work with values in an arbitrary affine
S_{0}
-scheme
\operatorname{Spec}{R}
. Let
1_{E}
be an element of
\omega(E_{V})(R)
mapping to
1\in R
. Then for
u\in U(\omega)(R)
,

a_{V}(u)\coloneqq-1_{E}+u(1_{E})\in\omega(M_{V})(R).

We must verify that it is well-defined (independent of
1_{E}
). For this, note that since
V
is simple, we have
V=\operatorname{gr}^{W}V
. Therefore,
U(\omega)
acts trivially on
\omega(V)
and hence on
\omega(M_{V})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V)^{\vee}\otimes_{k}\omega(V)
. It follows that if
1_{E}^{\prime}
is another lift of
1
to
\omega(E_{V})(R)
, we have

\displaystyle\left[-1_{E}+u(1_{E})\right]-\left[-1_{E}^{\prime}+u(1_{E}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[-1_{E}+1_{E}^{\prime}\right]-\left[-u(1_{E})+u(1_{E}^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[-1_{E}+1_{E}^{\prime}\right]-u\left[-1_{E}+1_{E}^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

because
-1_{E}+1_{E}^{\prime}\in\omega(M_{V})(R)
.

We can use well-definedness to show that the map is a group homomorphism. We have

\displaystyle a_{V}(u_{1}u_{2})=-1_{E}+u_{1}(u_{2}(1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-1_{E}+u_{1}(1_{E})+u_{1}(-1_{E}+u_{2}(1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{V}(u_{1})+u_{1}(a_{V}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{V}(u_{1})+a_{V}(u_{2}),

the last by the fact that
U(\omega)
acts trivially on
\omega(M_{V})
. Since the target is abelian, the map factors through
U(\omega)^{\mathrm{ab}}=\omega(\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}})
.

We have thus defined a homomorphism of group schemes over
S_{0}
from
\omega(\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}})
to
\omega(M_{V})
. We now must show that it is equivariant for the action of
\mathcal{G}(\omega)
to get a map
\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}}\to M_{V}
.

Let
T
be a
k
-scheme and
g\in\mathcal{G}(T)
. We denote (by abuse of notation)
s(g),b(g)
by
s,b
so that we have morphisms of
k
-schemes
s,b\colon T\to S_{0}
. We write
T_{s}
and
T_{b}
for these two different ways of viewing
T
as an
S_{0}
-scheme. We have a map
g\colon s^{*}\omega\to b^{*}\omega
. We must show that the following diagram is commutative:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.01682pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-30.55917pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{s^{*}\omega(U^{\mathrm{ab}})(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.23184pt\raise 5.75972pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.25418pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g_{U^{\mathrm{ab}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.47446pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.32042pt\raise-16.2475pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.16931pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s^{*}a_{V}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.47446pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b^{*}\omega(U^{\mathrm{ab}})(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.83571pt\raise-16.2475pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.16931pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{b^{*}a_{V}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.83571pt\raise-24.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-31.01682pt\raise-32.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{s^{*}\omega(M_{V})(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.194pt\raise-26.63806pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.15695pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g_{M_{V}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.01682pt\raise-32.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.01682pt\raise-32.49501pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{b^{*}\omega(M_{V})(T)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Fix
1_{E}\in\omega(E_{V})(S_{0})
. Note that we have an isomorphism

g_{E_{v}}\colon s^{*}\omega(E_{V})\to b^{*}\omega(E_{V})

restricting to
g_{M_{V}}
on
s^{*}\omega(M_{V})
.
Since
g
is a tensor-isomorphism, it induces the trivial action on
\omega(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}})
. In particular, it sends
s^{*}1_{E}\in\omega(E_{V})(T_{s})
to an element of
\omega(E_{V})(T_{b})
projecting to
1\in\omega(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}})(T_{b})=\mathcal{O}(T_{b})
. In particular, we find that for
u_{b}\in b^{*}\omega(\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}})(T)
,

b^{*}a_{V}(u_{b})=-g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E})+u_{b}(g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E}))=-b^{*}1_{E}+u_{b}(b^{*}1_{E}).

Letting
u_{s}\in s^{*}\omega(\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}})(T)
, we find that

\displaystyle b^{*}a_{V}(g_{U^{\mathrm{ab}}}(u_{s}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b^{*}a_{V}(g\star u_{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E})+(g\star u_{s})(g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E})+g_{E_{V}}(u_{s}(s^{*}1_{E})),

where the last step follows by commutativity of
5.2.2
(*). But

\displaystyle-g_{E_{V}}(s^{*}1_{E})+g_{E_{V}}(u_{s}(s^{*}1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{E_{V}}(-s^{*}1_{E}+u_{s}(s^{*}1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{M_{V}}(-s^{*}1_{E}+u_{s}(s^{*}1_{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{M_{V}}(s^{*}a_{V}(u_{s})),

so we are done.

∎

5.4.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We apply Lemma
5.3
to the
\mathcal{T}
of Theorem
5.1
and
\omega=\omega_{0}
. We get an object
\underline{U}
of
\mathcal{T}
and an isomorphism
a\colon\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\prod_{V\,\mathrm{simple}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V)^{\vee}\otimes V.
Note that for
V
simple, there is a unique
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for which
V=\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}V
, which we call its
weight
. The weight-filtered property of
\mathcal{T}
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V)=0
unless
n<0
. This implies that
\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}}
is concentrated in negative weights. Applying
\omega_{\phi}
, we find that the action of
\phi
on

\omega_{0}(\underline{U}^{\mathrm{ab}})

has eigenvalues
24
24

24

We mean eigenvalues of any
k^{\prime}_{0}
-linear power of
\phi
.
in
w_{<0}(k^{\prime}_{0})\coloneqq\bigcup_{m<0}w_{m}(k^{\prime}_{0})
.

Let
\underline{\mathcal{U}}
denote the completed universal enveloping algebra of
\underline{U}
(
B.2.5
), and let
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}
denote its augmentation ideal. Then for each
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n}/\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n+1}
is a quotient object of a tensor power of
U^{\mathrm{ab}}=\underline{\mathfrak{a}}/\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{2}
in
\mathcal{T}
. In particular, since
w_{<0}(k^{\prime}_{0})
is closed under multiplication, all Frobenius eigenvalues acting on
\omega_{0}(\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n}/\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n+1})
and hence on
\omega_{0}(\underline{\mathfrak{a}})
are in
w_{<0}(k^{\prime}_{0})

Consequently, the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
shows that the map
U(\omega_{0})\to U(\omega_{0})
given on points by

g\mapsto g^{-1}\phi(g)

is an isomorphism. Specifically, the operator
S
on
\omega_{0}(\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n}/\underline{\mathfrak{a}}^{n+1})
given by

S(y)=\phi(y)-y

considered in loc. cit. has eigenvalues of the form
\alpha-1
for
\alpha\in w_{<0}(k^{\prime}_{0})
; since
1\in w_{0}(k^{\prime}_{0})
, and the different
w_{m}(k^{\prime}_{0})
are disjoint (Definition
2.6.2
), the operator
S
is invertible. In view of this, the proof goes through with no change.

5.4.1.

We recall the
\underline{U}-\underline{U^{\operatorname{gr}}}
-bitorsor
\underline{\Spl^{W}}
(
5.2.2
). Applying
\omega_{\phi}
, we get a
\phi
-equivariant composition action

\circ\colon U(\omega_{0})\times\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0})\times U^{\operatorname{gr}}(\omega_{0})\to\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0}).

Consequently, the proof of
[
17
, Corollary 3.2]
applies without change to show that
\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0})
possesses a unique
k^{\prime}_{0}
-point
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
such that
\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0})=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
.

5.4.2.

We must analyze the
\phi
-action on
\underline{\Spl^{W}_{H}}(\omega_{0})
to show that
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
satisfies the conditions of the theorem. Let
E\in\mathcal{T}
. Recall there is an isomorphism
4.10.3
(*)

\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\simeq\omega_{0}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E).

For
g\in\mathcal{G}(T)
, note that
g^{\mathrm{ss}}_{E}=g_{\operatorname{gr}^{W}E}
under this isomorphism. By the action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0})
described in
5.2.2
, the map

\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0})\times\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\to\omega_{0}(E)

is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant. Since the action on
\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)
is the usual
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\omega_{0}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
, this upgrades to a diagram

\underline{\Spl^{W}}\times\operatorname{gr}^{W}E\to E

in
\mathcal{T}
.

Applying
\omega_{\phi}
, we get an action

\omega_{\phi}(\underline{\Spl^{W}})\times\omega_{\phi}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)\to\omega_{\phi}(E)

in the Tannakian category
\varphi\mathrm{Mod}(R)
. In other words, this says that (**) is
\phi
-equivariant. The action of
\phi
on
\underline{\Spl^{W}}(\omega_{0})
is the one that fixes
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
, the action on
E
is the
\phi
from
5.1
(*), and the action on
\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)\simeq\omega_{0}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
is the
\phi
-module structure on
\omega_{\phi}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}E)
, which is none other than
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)
. Thus for
v\in\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(E)
,

\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}(\alpha))=(\phi(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}))(\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)(\alpha))=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)(\alpha)),

as desired. ∎

5.5.
Hodge and Frobenius together

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a weight-filtered Tannakian category over
k
with an admissible Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor (
2.6.7
,
2.6.8
)
\omega_{H,\phi}=(\omega_{H},\omega_{\phi},\eta)
over
(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
on
\mathcal{T}
. Let
U\coloneqq U^{W}
,
L^{W}
,
P_{F}
, and
F^{0}U\coloneqq U^{W}_{H}
be as in
§
4.14
.

By Theorem
4.18
, there exists a Hodge-filtered weight-splitting

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\colon\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\omega

over
k^{\prime}
.

We refer to
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
as an
arithmetic Hodge path
. Arithmetic Hodge paths form a trivial torsor under
F^{0}U
. By Theorem
5.1
there’s a unique Frobenius-equivariant weight-splitting

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}\colon\omega_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\omega_{0}

which we refer to as the
arithmetic crystalline path
. We let

\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\colon\omega^{\operatorname{gr}}\to\omega

denote the path obtained from
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
by base-changing from
k^{\prime}_{0}
to
k^{\prime}
and transporting along
\eta
.

Definition 5.6
.

In the situation above, we define the
right-hand unipotent period loop of
\mathcal{T}
by

\mathfrak{u}^{R}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}\in U,

We define the
left-hand unipotent
\mathfrak{p}
-adic period loop of
\mathcal{T}
by
25
25

25

Evidently these are inverse to one another; our terminology allows us to avoid picking a favorite.

\mathfrak{u}^{L}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}}\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}=(\mathfrak{u}^{R})^{-1}\in U.

When we wish to get rid of the dependence on choice of arithmetic Hodge path, we may regard
\mathfrak{u}^{R}
as a
k^{\prime}
-point of the homogeneous space
F^{0}U\backslash U
and
\mathfrak{u}^{L}
as a
k^{\prime}
-point of the homogeneous space

U/F^{0}U.

5.6.1.
Remark

While the terms ‘left’ and ‘right’ might seem reversed, we will see in Theorems
6.11
and
6.14
that they correspond to Bloch–Kato logarithms with values in
F^{0}\backslash\omega(\pi)
and
\omega(\pi)/F^{0}
, respectively.

6.
Geometry of Localization-Realization Maps for Selmer Schemes, in an axiomatic setting

In this section, while staying in an abstract setting, we prove that the period loops of Definition
5.6
have the desired relationship to the non-abelian Bloch–Kato Selmer maps of Chabauty–Kim.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a reductive group over a field
k
, let
\pi
be a unipotent
k
-group equipped with a
\mathbb{G}
-action, and let
R
be an arbitrary
k
-algebra. Then
H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};\pi_{R})=\{*\}
.

Proof.

The category of quasi-coherent representations of
\mathbb{G}_{R}
is canonically equivalent to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the algebraic stack
B\mathbb{G}_{R}
over
\operatorname{Spec}R
; the equivalence interchanges group cohomology with cohomology of quasi-coherent sheaves. This helps to put the problem in a heavily studied context.
26
26

26

Our use of algebraic stacks here is frivolous: we could equally argue directly with injective resolutions in the category of quasi-coherent representations à la
[
61
]
.

In particular, we have compatibility of cohomology with flat base-change by
[
107
,
Tag 0765
]
: if
V
is a representation of
\mathbb{G}
over
k
then

H^{i}(\mathbb{G}_{R};V_{R})=H^{i}(\mathbb{G};V)\otimes R=0

for
i>0
. Assuming for an induction on the level of unipotence that
H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};\pi_{R})=0
and that

0\to V\to\pi^{\prime}\to\pi\to 0

is an extension of unipotent
k
-groups, we have a transitive action of
\{*\}=H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};V_{R})
of the fibers of
H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};\pi^{\prime}_{R})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};\pi_{R})=\{*\}
and the proposition follows.
∎

Lemma 6.1.1
.

If
\pi
is a unipotent group in a weight-filtered Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
and
P
a right
\pi
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}
, then
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P
is a trivial
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
-torsor.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{gr}^{W}
is an exact tensor functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
, we may apply it to
P
to get a
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
. Since
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
is semisimple, we may show by induction on the unipotence level of
\pi
as in the proof of Proposition
6.1
that
H^{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}};\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi)=0
. Thus
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P
is a trivial
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
and hence also in
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

6.2.
Prounipotent groups in a filtered Tannakian category

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a strictly filtered Tannakian category over a field
k
. We refer to §
B.2
for basics on pro- and ind- categories and algebraic geometry in a Tannakian category.

6.2.1.

We say that an object
\phi\colon I\to\mathcal{T}
of
\Pro\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\Ind\mathcal{T}
) for
I
a cofiltered (resp. filtered) indexing category is
graded-finite
if the the composed functor
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}\circ\phi
has a limit (resp. colimit) in
\mathcal{T}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
M\in\Pro{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\in\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
), we can define the tensor algebra
T(M)\in\Pro{\mathcal{T}}
(resp.
\in\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
) as the projective (resp. inductive) limit of the truncated tensor algebras
T^{\leq n}(M)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}M^{\otimes i}
under the obvious projection (resp. inclusion) maps.

6.2.2.

For a graded-finite object
M
,
\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}(M)
is naturally in
\Gr(\LF(S))
.

6.2.3.

There is a ‘duality’ contravariant auto-equivalence on
\Gr(\LF(S))
sending
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in\Gr(\LF(S))
to
\{M_{-i}^{\vee}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, and
\omega^{W,\operatorname{gr}}(M)
commutes with duality on graded-finite objects.

6.2.4.

If
\pi
is a prounipotent group in
\mathcal{T}
(
B.2.5
), we say that
\pi
is
graded-finite
if
\operatorname{Lie}\pi
is. We say that
\pi
is a
unipotent group
if
\operatorname{Lie}\pi\in\mathcal{T}
.

6.2.5.

If
\omega_{H}
is a Hodge-filtered fiber functor over
k^{\prime}
and
\pi
a prounipotent group in
\mathcal{T}
, then we get a filtration
F^{i}\omega(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
. We denote by
F^{i}\omega(\pi)
the image of
F^{i}\omega(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
under the exponential map
\exp\colon\omega(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)\to\omega(\pi)
(which is an isomorphism of schemes over
k^{\prime}
).

6.2.6.

In the notation of
6.2.5
, suppose
i\geq 0
. Then
F^{i}\omega(\pi)
is a subgroup scheme; in this case, we let
\omega(\pi)/F^{i}
(resp.
F^{i}\backslash\omega(\pi)
) denote the homogeneous space
\omega(\pi)/F^{i}\omega(\pi)
(resp.
F^{i}\omega(\pi)\backslash\omega(\pi)
).

Let
I_{\pi}
denote the kernel of the counit
\mathcal{O}(\pi)\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}
. By
[
48
, Lemme 7.6]
,
F^{i}\omega(\pi)
is defined by the ideal generated by
F^{1-i}\omega(I(\pi))
. If
i=0
, note that this is the ideal generated by
F^{1}\omega(\mathcal{O}(\pi))=F^{1}\mathcal{O}(\omega(\pi))
.

Definition 6.3
.

For
\mathcal{T}
as above, an
effective
27
27

27

Here, ‘effective’ accords with Voevodsky’s conventions, by which the
homological
motives functor lands in the triangulated category of
effecti/ve motives
.
prounipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group
is a graded-finite prounipotent group
\pi
in
\mathcal{T}
such that
W_{-1}\operatorname{Lie}\pi=\operatorname{Lie}\pi
(equivalently,
W_{0}\operatorname{CoLie}\pi=0
).

6.3.1.

If
\pi
is effective, then
W_{-i}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)^{\otimes i}=(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)^{\otimes i}
for
i\geq 0
, so

T^{\leq n+1}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)\to T^{\leq n}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)

induces an isomorphism on
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{-i}
for
n\geq i
. Therefore,
T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
and hence
\mathcal{U}\pi
and
\mathcal{O}(\pi)
are graded-finite.

More precisely,
W_{-1}T^{\leq n}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)^{\otimes i}
and
W_{0}T^{\leq n}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)=T^{\leq n}(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
, so

T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)=W_{0}T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{0}T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)=\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}

is the counit. Since the kernel of
T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)\to\mathcal{U}\pi
is in
W_{-1}T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
, the same is true for
\mathcal{U}\pi
. By duality, we have
W_{-1}\mathcal{O}(\pi)=0
and
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{0}\mathcal{O}(\pi)=W_{0}\mathcal{O}(\pi)
is the image of the unit
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}\xrightarrow{\eta}\mathcal{O}(\pi)
.

Proposition 6.3.2
.

Let
\pi
be an effective unipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group,
\omega
a fiber functor over
k^{\prime}
, and
R
an arbitrary
k^{\prime}
-algebra. Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is weight-filtered, so that
{\mathbb{G}}\coloneqq L^{W}(\omega)
is reductive. Let
P\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
. Then
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)
possesses a unique
\mathbb{G}_{R}
-fixed
R
-point
\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.1
,
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}(P)
is trivial when regarded as a
\mathbb{G}_{R}
-equivariant
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}(\pi)
-torsor, so we may assume that
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)=\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}(\pi)=\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
(
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-equivariant
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
-torsors). By
6.3.1
,
W_{0}\mathcal{O}(\pi)=\mathrm{Im}(\eta)
, so
W_{0}\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})=\mathrm{Im}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\eta)_{R})=R1_{\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})}
.

A
\mathbb{G}_{R}
-fixed point corresponds to an
R
-algebra homomorphism from
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})
to
R=R_{0}
respecting the
\mathbb{G}_{R}
-action and thus the grading; in particular, it must kill
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})_{>0}
. But
R1_{\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})}
surjects onto
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})/\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})_{>0}=\operatorname{gr}_{0}^{W}\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})
, so such a homomorphism is determined by
R
-linearity and thus unique. (More precisely, it is the counit
\epsilon
of
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R})
, corresponding to the identity of the group
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}(R)
.)
∎

6.3.3.

Proposition
6.3.2
implies there is a unique
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-equivariant isomorphism
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)\simeq\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
of
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
-torsors. By applying this to
R=k^{\prime}
, we find that the trivialization in Lemma
6.1.1
is unique when
\pi
is effective.

Corollary 6.3.4
.

If
\pi
is an effective prounipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group, then the conclusion of Lemma
6.1.1
(resp. Proposition
6.3.2
) holds (in particular, any torsor becomes trivial in
H^{1}(\mathbb{G}_{R};-)
).

Proof.

Note that since
W_{0}\operatorname{Lie}\pi=\operatorname{Lie}\pi
, the subobjects
W_{-n}\operatorname{Lie}\pi
are Lie ideals in
\mathcal{T}
for
n\geq 0
. Setting
\pi_{n}
to be the unipotent group in
\mathcal{T}
associated to
\operatorname{Lie}\pi/W_{-n-1}\operatorname{Lie}\pi
, we have
\pi=\varprojlim_{n}\pi_{n}
.

Let
P
be a right
\pi
-torsor (resp.
\pi_{R}
-torsor) in
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{R}
), and let
P_{n}
be the pushout of
P
along
\pi\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{n}
(resp.
\pi_{R}\twoheadrightarrow(\pi_{n})_{R}
). Then
P=\varprojlim_{n}P_{n}
as a scheme in
\mathcal{T}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{R}
).

By graded-finiteness, each
\pi_{n}
is unipotent, so we may apply
6.3.3
(resp. Proposition
6.3.2
) to find that
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P_{n}
has a unique trivialization (resp.
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P_{n})
has a unique
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-fixed
R
-point). The uniqueness implies that these piece together to give a trivialization of
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P
(resp.
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-fixed
R
-point of
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)
). Then we can assume
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P=\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
(resp.
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)=\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
) as
\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
-torsors in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
(resp.
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-equivariant
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}
-torsors), and the rest of the proof proceeds as in Proposition
6.3.2
and
6.3.3
.
∎

6.4.
Representability of cohomology schemes

Let us consider the association
R\mapsto H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
defined on
k
-schemes
R
in
B.5.12
. Given a
k
-map
R\to R^{\prime}
, we get a map
(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{R^{\prime}}\to(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{R}
of
k
-stacks, thus an associated pullback functor
\mathcal{T}_{R}\to\mathcal{T}_{R^{\prime}}
, and thus a map
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)\to H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R^{\prime})
. In particular, this association is a functor from
k
-schemes to (pointed) sets. We wish to discuss when this is representable by a
k
-scheme.

For an object
M\in\mathcal{T}
and
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we have the functor
R\mapsto H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)
.

Lemma 6.4.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is neutral,
28
28

28

This assumption should be unnecessary; for example, it would follow by compatibility of cohomology with flat base-change for fpqc-algebraic stacks. However,
[
107
,
Tag 0765
]
proves it only for (fppf-)algebraic stacks.
and suppose
M
is an object
\mathcal{T}
. Then
H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)=H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)\otimes_{k}R
. In particular, if
\dim_{k}H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)<\infty
, then
R\mapsto H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)
is representable by the vector group associated to the vector space
H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)
.

Proof.

Let
\omega
be a neutral fiber functor,
\omega_{R}
its base-change to
R
, and recall that

H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)=H^{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M)).

The right-hand-side is computed as the cohomology of the complex
C^{\bullet}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))
, where

C^{k}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))

is the set of homomorphisms of
R
-schemes from
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R})^{k}
to
\omega_{R}(M)
. Thus

\displaystyle C^{k}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{R}(M)\otimes_{R}\mathcal{O}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R})^{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\omega(M)\otimes_{k}R)\otimes_{R}\mathcal{O}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)^{k}_{R})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(M)\otimes_{k}\mathcal{O}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)^{k})\otimes_{k}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C^{k}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega);\omega(M))\otimes_{k}R.

Since
R
is flat over
k
, tensoring with
R
commutes with taking cohomology, so we find that
H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)=H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)\otimes_{k}R
, and we are done.
∎

6.4.2.

We make a remark about functoriality in
R
of
C^{\bullet}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))
and thus
H^{k}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))
. If
R^{\prime}
is an
R
-algebra (i.e., we have a homomorphism
R\to R^{\prime}
of
k
-algebras), then
C^{k}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R^{\prime}});\omega_{R^{\prime}}(M))
, a priori the set of maps of
R^{\prime}
-schemes from
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R^{\prime}})
to
\omega_{R^{\prime}}(M)
, may be identified with the set of maps of maps of
R
-schemes from
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R})
to
\omega_{R^{\prime}}(M)
. As a result, the map of
R
-schemes with
G_{R}
-action
\omega_{R}(M)\to\omega_{R^{\prime}}(M)
defines a map of complexes
C^{\bullet}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R});\omega_{R}(M))\to C^{\bullet}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{R^{\prime}});\omega_{R^{\prime}}(M))
and thus a map on
H^{k}
.

The same is true for
\pi
in place of
M
, at least for
k\leq 1
.

Theorem 6.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a neutral weight-filtered Tannakian category and
\pi
an effective pro-unipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group. Suppose that
\dim_{k}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},\Gr^{W}_{-n}\operatorname{Lie}\pi)<\infty
for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Then the functor on
k
-algebras
R\mapsto H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
is representable by an affine
k
-scheme.

Proof.

The proof follows that of
[
21
, Theorem 2.2.1]
, which itself follows that of
[
82
, Proposition 2]
.

We let
\omega
be a neutral fiber functor and set
G\coloneqq\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T};\omega)
. We set
U\coloneqq\omega(\pi)
,
U_{n}\coloneqq\omega(\pi_{n})
, and
V_{n}\coloneqq\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{-n}\pi)=\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{-n}\operatorname{Lie}{\pi})
, all with
G
-action. The compatibility of the Lie bracket on
\operatorname{Lie}\pi
with the weight filtration implies that
V_{n}
is central in
U_{n}
(in particular, abelian).

The proof proceeds by induction on
n
, showing that

R\mapsto H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi_{n})(R)=H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})

is representable by a finite-type affine scheme and that
H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{R};(U_{n})_{R})=0
for all
n
. For
n=0
,
U_{n}
is trivial, so the result is immediate (it is represented by
\operatorname{Spec}{k}
).

Suppose
n\geq 1
, and
R\mapsto H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})
is representable by a finite-type affine
k
-scheme. We apply
[
104
, Proposition 43]
to the short exact sequence
0\to(V_{n})_{R}\to(U_{n})_{R}\to(U_{n-1})_{R}\to 0
, noting as in
[
21
, Footnote 7]
that the result still works because
U_{n}\to U_{n-1}
and hence
(U_{n})_{R}\to(U_{n-1})_{R}
is algebraically split (and hence the map on chains
C^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})\to C^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})
is surjective). We get a long exact sequence

(12)

\begin{split}0\to H^{0}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\to H^{0}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})\to H^{0}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})\to\\
H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})\xrightarrow{\partial_{R}}H^{2}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R}),\end{split}

with the usual interpretation given that
H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})
and
H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})
are pointed sets rather than groups.

For a map
R\to R^{\prime}
of
k
-algebras, we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(V_{n})_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U_{n})_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U_{n-1})_{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(V_{n})_{R^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U_{n})_{R^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(U_{n-1})_{R^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

that is
G_{R}
-equivariant, hence by
6.4.2
induces a map of the corresponding exact sequences (
12
).

We have
H^{0}(G;V_{n})=\operatorname{Ext}^{0}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},V_{n})=0
by Lemma
2.5.7
because
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}}
and
V_{n}
are concentrated in degrees
0
and
-n
, respectively, and
n\geq 1
. Thus
V_{n}^{G}=0
in a scheme-theoretic sense, hence
(V_{n})_{R}^{G_{R}}=H^{0}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})=0
. By the induction hypothesis,
H^{0}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})=0
, so by the long exact sequence,
H^{0}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})=0
.

Since
H^{0}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})=0
,

0\to H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})\xrightarrow{\beta^{\prime}}H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})\xrightarrow{\partial_{R}}H^{2}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})

is exact. Thus

0\to H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})\xrightarrow{\beta}\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)\to 0

is exact for every
R
, in the sense that the abelian group
H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})
acts simply transitively on the fibers of the surjective map
\beta
.

We note, as in
[
21
, Corollary 2.2.5]
, the functor
R\mapsto H^{2}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})
is, by Lemma
6.4.1
, a subfunctor of a representable functor. More precisely, choosing a basis
\{b_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of the
k
-vector space
H^{2}(G;V_{n})
, we have
H^{2}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\cong\bigoplus_{i\in I}R
functorially in
R
, which is a subfunctor of the representable functor
{\mathbb{A}}^{H^{2}(G;V_{n})}\colon R\mapsto\prod_{i\in I}R
(which, as noted in loc.cit., is represented by
\operatorname{Spec}\operatorname{Sym}H^{2}(G;V_{n})^{\vee}
). Thus
\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)
is the kernel of the composite
H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})\xrightarrow{\partial_{R}}H^{2}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{A}}^{H^{2}(G;V_{n})}
, which is a morphism of representable presheaves and thus a morphism of schemes. Furthermore,
R\mapsto\{0\}\subseteq\prod_{i\in I}R
is a closed subscheme (defined by the ideal generated by
H^{2}(G;V_{n})^{\vee}
in
\operatorname{Sym}H^{2}(G;V_{n})^{\vee}
), so
R\mapsto\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)
is a closed subscheme of
R\mapsto H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n-1})_{R})
, hence representable by a finite-type affine scheme.

Let
A
represent
R\mapsto\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)
. Then we have an element
\id_{A}\in\partial_{A}^{-1}(0)=\homo_{k}(A,A)
. Let
\alpha\in H^{1}(G_{A};(U_{n})_{A})
map onto
\id_{A}
. Then
\alpha
defines by Yoneda’s Lemma a map of functors
\alpha\colon\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})
that is a section of
\beta
. Using the action
\star
of
H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})
on
H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})
, we define a map

H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\times\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)\to H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})

given by
(x,y)\mapsto x\star\alpha(y)
. Since
\alpha
is a section of
\beta
, and
H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})
acts transitively, this defines an isomorphism
H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})\times\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)\simeq H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})
of functors from
k
-algebras
R
to sets. Since
R\mapsto H^{1}(G_{R};(V_{n})_{R})
is representable, finite-type, and affine by Lemma
6.4.1
and the finite-dimensionality of
H^{1}(G;V_{n})\cong\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},\Gr^{W}_{-n}\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
, and we showed
R\mapsto\partial_{R}^{-1}(0)
is representable, finite-type, and affine, the same is true of
R\mapsto H^{1}(G_{R};(U_{n})_{R})
.

Finally, as in the proof of Corollary
6.3.4
, a right
\pi_{R}
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}_{R}
is an inverse limit of
\pi_{n}
-torsors
P_{n}
, so it follows that
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi_{n})
. An inverse limit (as presheaves) of affine schemes is again an affine scheme, so we are done.
∎

6.4.4.
Remark

The proof shows more precisely that
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)
is finite-type when
\pi
is finite-type and in general an inverse limit of finite-type affine schemes.

6.4.5.
Remark

Although the proof of Theorem
6.4.3
used a neutral fiber functor
\omega
, the result is independent of
\omega
. In particular, if we had two fiber functors
\{\omega_{j}\}_{j=1,2}
over
k
that were not isomorphic, the resulting
k
-scheme structure on
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)
would be independent of
j
. Once again, we suspect Theorem
6.4.3
to be true without the assumption that
\mathcal{T}
is neutral.

6.4.6.
Remark

Without the assumption
\dim_{k}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{T}},\Gr^{W}_{-n}\operatorname{Lie}\pi)<\infty
, the functor
R\mapsto H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
should still be
ind-representable
, as is the case for infinite-dimensional vector spaces.

6.5.
Base-change of filtered fiber functors

In order to define the non-abelian Bloch–Kato logarithm as a map of schemes, we must define it for torsors with coefficients in an arbitrary
k^{\prime}
-scheme
S
. It follows that we must define the Hodge filtration on an arbitrary such torsor, and for this we must be able to base-change a filtered fiber functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}_{S}
for an arbitrary
k
-scheme
S
. This has been done in
[
116
, Lemma 5.3]
when
S
is a finite field extension of
k^{\prime}
and in
[
117
, Proposition 4.7(i)]
when the Tannakian category in question is neutral.

If
\phi
is a graded or filtered fiber functor over a field
k^{\prime}
with underlying fiber functor
\omega
, and
S
is a
k
-scheme, we recall from
B.5.3
that we have a base-changed fiber functor
\omega_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\LF(S_{k^{\prime}})
, and given a map
S\to\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
, we get
\omega_{S/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\LF(S)
. We would like to do the same for
\phi
.

6.5.1.

If
\phi
is graded and we are given
S\to\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
, we can base-change the natural map
({\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{k^{\prime}}\to\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)
of
k^{\prime}
-schemes to a map
({\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{S}\to\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)_{S}
. Using the equivalence
\mathcal{T}_{S}\simeq\Rep(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)_{S})
, we get a grading on
\omega_{S/S}(M)
for every
M\in\mathcal{T}_{S}
, which upgrades
\omega_{S/S}
to a graded fiber functor
\phi_{S/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\GrLF(S)
.

We describe an alternative, stack-theoretic version of this construction that will be necessary in
6.5.2
. Note that
\GrLF(k^{\prime})=\LF((B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{k^{\prime}})
, so that
\phi
is the same as a fiber functor valued in
\LF((B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{k^{\prime}})
, hence by Theorem
B.5.8
is isomorphic to the pullback functor associated to a map
(B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{F_{\LF}^{-1}(\phi)}\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
of
k
-stacks.

As in
B.5.3
but with
X=(B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{k^{\prime}}
, we can base-change along
S\to\operatorname{Spec}{k}
to get a map
(B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{S_{k^{\prime}}}\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
of
S
-stacks and thus a graded fiber functor

\phi_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\GrLF(S_{k^{\prime}}).

Given
S\to\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
, we can base-change along
S\to S_{k^{\prime}}
to get a map
(B{\mathbb{G}_{m}})_{S}\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
of
S
-stacks and thus a graded fiber functor

\phi_{S/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\GrLF(S).

6.5.2.

Now suppose
\phi
is filtered. By
[
113
, Example 2.13]
, for a ring
A
, the category
\LF([{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{1}]_{A})
is the same as
\FilLF^{\mathrm{fin}}(A)
(Definition
4.3.3
). In fact, it holds for a general scheme
S
in place of
A
with ‘finite projective’ replaced by ‘finite locally free’ in (a) and (b) of loc.cit. In particular,
\phi
defines an exact tensor functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\LF([{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{1}]_{k^{\prime}})
, whence by Theorem
B.5.8
a map
[{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{1}]_{k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{F_{\LF}^{-1}(\phi)}\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
of
k
-stacks.

As in
6.5.1
, we get a map
[{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{1}]_{S_{k^{\prime}}}\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
of
S
-stacks and thus a filtered fiber functor

\phi_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\FilLF(S_{k^{\prime}}).

Given
S\to\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
, can base-change along
S\to S_{k^{\prime}}
to get a map
[{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}^{1}]_{S}\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
of
S
-stacks and thus a filtered fiber functor

\phi_{S/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\FilLF(S).

Note that
\phi_{S/S}
is compatible with base-change from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}_{S}
, so it is the unique extension described in
[
117
, Proposition 4.7(i)]
when
S=\operatorname{Spec}{R^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Spec}{R^{\prime\prime}}
is affine.

6.6.
Setup for Abstract Chabauty–Kim diagram

For the rest of §
6
, we let
\mathcal{T}
denote a weight-filtered Tannakian category over
k
,
\omega_{H,\phi}=(\omega_{H},\omega_{\phi},\eta)
over
(k^{\prime},k^{\prime}_{0},\phi)
a Hodge-filtered Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor,
G
,
G^{\operatorname{gr}}
,
U\coloneqq U^{W}
,
L^{W}
,
P_{F}
,
F^{0}U\coloneqq U^{W}_{H}
as in §
4.14
,
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}
as in §
5.5
. We set
\mathbb{G}\coloneqq L^{W}=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}},\omega)=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}},\omega^{\operatorname{gr}})
, which is reductive.
We have the usual diagram of pro-algebraic
k^{\prime}
-groups (
4.9
):

\textstyle{U^{\operatorname{gr}}\subseteq G^{\operatorname{gr}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ss}^{\mathrm{gr}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{G\supseteq U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ss}}

We have

\mathfrak{u}^{R}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\circ{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}}^{-1}\in U(k^{\prime})

\mathfrak{u}^{L}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\circ{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}}^{-1}\in U(k^{\prime})

as in Definition
5.6
, which is uniquely determined modulo left (resp. right) multiplication by
F^{0}U
.

6.7.
Frobenius-invariant paths of torsors

Let
\pi
be an effective prounipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group, and let
P
be a right
\pi
-torsor. The proof of
[
17
, Corollary 3.2]
applies without change to show that
\omega_{0}(P)
possesses a unique
\phi
-fixed
k^{\prime}_{0}
-point
\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0}
. The next lemma concerns the interaction between
\gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{cr}}\in\omega_{0}(P)(k^{\prime}_{0})
and the
\mathbb{G}
-fixed point
\gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{gr}}\coloneqq\gamma^{\operatorname{gr}}_{k^{\prime}_{0}}
of Corollary
6.3.4
through the Frobenius equivariant Tannakian path

\mathfrak{p}_{0}^{\mathrm{cr}}\colon\omega_{0}^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\omega_{0}

of Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma 6.7.1
.

In the situation and the notation of §
6.7

\gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{gr}}=(\mathfrak{p}_{0}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}(\gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{cr}})\in\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(P)(k^{\prime}_{0}).

Proof.

The associated graded
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\pi)
is an effective prounipotent group object of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
, and
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(P)
is a
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\pi)
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{ss}}
.
The Frobenius automorphism on
\omega_{0}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}(P))\simeq\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(P)
is the associated graded
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)
of the Frobenius automorphism
\phi
of
\omega_{0}(P)
.

It follows, on the one hand, that
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(P)
contains a unique
k^{\prime}_{0}
-point fixed by
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)
; by the commutativity of Diagram
5.1
(*),
(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0})^{-1}(\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0})
is fixed by
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(\phi)
, hence it
is
that point. On the other hand, by Corollary
6.3.4
, it follows that
\operatorname{gr}^{W}(P)
is trivial; i.e., there’s an isomorphism of affine
\mathcal{T}
-schemes
\operatorname{gr}^{W}P\simeq\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi
. Under such an isomorphism, the image of the unique Frobenius-fixed point of
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(P)
must again be the unique Frobenius-fixed point of
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}_{0}(\pi)
, namely the group identity
1
. Since
1
is
\mathbb{G}
-fixed, so is
(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0})^{-1}(\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{0})
, and we are done.
∎

6.7.2.

Thus, if
\pi
is an effective prounipotent

\mathcal{T}
-group and
P
is a
\pi
-torsor, then
\mathfrak{p}_{0}^{\mathrm{cr}}(\gamma_{0}^{\mathrm{gr}})
is the unique
\phi
-fixed
k^{\prime}_{0}
-point of
\omega_{0}(P)
. For
R
an arbitrary
k^{\prime}_{0}
-algebra and
P
a
\pi_{R}
-torsor in
\mathcal{T}_{R}
, we
define

\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R})\in(\omega_{0})_{R/R}(P)(R).

6.8.
Hodge paths of torsors

Let
\pi
be an effective prounipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group,
R
a
k^{\prime}
-algebra,
P\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
, and
\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}
as in Proposition
6.3.2
and Corollary
6.3.4
. For a choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
, we define

\gamma_{R}^{\mathrm{H}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}(\gamma_{R}^{\operatorname{gr}})\in\omega(P)(R).

We spend the rest of §
6.8
proving:

Proposition 6.8.1
.

Any two choices of
\gamma_{R}^{\mathrm{H}}
differ by an element of
F^{0}\omega(\pi)(R)
.

In doing so, we will in fact define a subscheme
F^{0}\omega(P)\subseteq\omega(P)
and show that
\gamma_{k^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{H}}\in F^{0}\omega(P)
.

6.8.2.

Let
R
be a ring,
A
a filtered
R
-algebra,
and
Y\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}{A}
. In such a case, we define an
R
-subscheme

F^{0}Y\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}{A/(A\cdot F^{1}A)}\subseteq Y.

Note that if
Y=\omega(\pi)
for
\pi
a prounipotent group in
\mathcal{T}
, this notation is consistent with
6.2.6
.

Lemma 6.8.3
.

Suppose
\{A_{i}\}_{i=1,2,3}
are filtered
R
-algebras,
Y_{i}\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}{A_{i}}
, and

f\colon Y_{1}\times_{R}Y_{2}\to Y_{3}

is a map for which
f^{*}\colon A_{3}\to A_{1}\otimes_{R}A_{2}
is a filtered map. Then
f
restricts to a map
F^{0}f\colon F^{0}Y_{1}\times_{R}F^{0}Y_{2}\to F^{0}Y_{3}
.

Proof.

Indeed, the image of
F^{1}A_{3}
lies in
\sum_{p+q=1}F^{p}A_{1}\otimes_{R}F^{q}A_{2}
, which is contained in
F^{1}A_{1}\otimes_{R}A_{2}+A_{1}\otimes_{R}F^{1}A_{2}
. The latter generates the ideal of the subscheme
F^{0}Y_{1}\times_{R}F^{0}Y_{2}
in
Y_{1}\otimes_{R}Y_{2}
, so we are done.
∎

6.8.4.

If
S
is an arbitrary
k
-scheme, then by
6.5.2
, we can base-change
\omega_{H}
to a filtered fiber functor

(\omega_{H})_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\FilLF(S_{k^{\prime}}).

From now through
6.8.14
, if
M
is an object of or affine scheme in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
, we let
\omega(M)
denote
\omega_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}(M)
; by abuse of notation, we refer to
\operatorname{fil}^{n}(\omega_{H})_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}(M)
as
F^{i}\omega(M)
.

By
6.8.3
, if
S
is affine,
\{Y_{i}\}
are affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
, and we have a map
f\colon Y_{1}\times Y_{2}\to Y_{3}
of affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
, then we get an associated map of
S_{k^{\prime}}
-schemes:

F^{0}\omega(f)\colon F^{0}\omega(Y_{1})\times_{S_{k^{\prime}}}F^{0}\omega(Y_{2})\to F^{0}\omega(Y_{3}).

6.8.5.

In particular, given a torsor action
a_{P}\colon P\times\pi_{S}\to P
in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
,
\omega(a_{P})
restricts to a map
F^{0}\omega(P)\times_{S_{k^{\prime}}}F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})\xrightarrow{F^{0}\omega(a_{P})}F^{0}\omega(P)
. Since
F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})=(F^{0}\omega(\pi))_{S}
is a subgroup scheme, we thus have an action of
F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})
on
F^{0}\omega(P)
over
S_{k^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 6.8.6
.

F^{0}\omega(P)
is an
F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})
-torsor over
S_{k^{\prime}}
.

The proof spans
6.8.8
-
6.8.14
. First, we show that this lemma implies Proposition
6.8.1
.

6.8.7.
Proof that Lemma
6.8.6

\implies
Proposition
6.8.1

We return to notation as in Proposition
6.8.1
, in which
R
is a
k^{\prime}
-algebra,
P
a torsor under
\pi_{R}
in
\mathcal{T}_{R}
, and
\omega(P)
(resp.
\omega(\pi_{R})
) refers to
\omega_{R/R}(P)
(resp.
\omega_{R/R}(\pi_{R})
).

Corollary
6.3.4
provides a unique
{\mathbb{G}}_{R}
-trivialization

\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)\simeq\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi_{R}).

Under this isomorphism,
\gamma^{\operatorname{gr}}
corresponds to the identity of
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi_{R})\simeq\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}\simeq\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi)_{R}
, which is necessarily in
F^{0}\omega(\operatorname{gr}^{W}\pi)
because it is
\exp(0)
.

Then
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\colon\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)\to\omega(P)
respects the Hodge filtration, so in particular, it sends
F^{0}\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(P)
to
F^{0}\omega(P)
. Thus
\gamma_{R}^{\mathrm{H}}\in F^{0}\omega(P)
for any choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
.

By
6.8.5
,
F^{0}\omega_{R_{k^{\prime}}/R}(P)
is a torsor under
F^{0}\omega_{R_{k^{\prime}}/R}(\pi_{R})
. We can base-change along
R_{k^{\prime}}\to R
to see that
F^{0}\omega(P)=F^{0}\omega_{R/R}(P)
is a torsor under
F^{0}\omega(\pi_{R})
. In particular, any two choices of
\gamma_{R}^{\mathrm{H}}
differ by an element of
F^{0}\omega(\pi_{R})
, and we are done.

6.8.8.
Proof of Lemma
6.8.6

We already know the action
F^{0}\omega(P)\times_{S_{k^{\prime}}}F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})\to F^{0}\omega(P)
has trivial stabilizers, as the same is true of
\omega(P)\times_{S_{k^{\prime}}}\omega(\pi_{S})\to\omega(P)
. Therefore, it suffices to show the action is transitive. We devote
6.8.9
-
6.8.14
to showing this transitivity.

6.8.9.

Let
Q
be a (pseudo-)torsor under a group scheme
H
over a scheme
X
with action map
a_{Q}
. We can define the
difference map

d_{Q}\colon Q\times_{X}Q\to H
as follows:

For an
X
-scheme
T
and
a,b\in P(T)
, there is a unique
g\in\pi(T)
such that
ag=b
. We let the image of
(a,b)\in(P\times_{X}P)(T)
be
g\in\pi(T)
.

The difference map is the unique map for which the composition

Q\times_{X}Q\xrightarrow{\Delta_{Q}\times\id_{Q}}(Q\times_{X}Q)\times_{X}Q=Q\times_{X}(Q\times_{X}Q)\xrightarrow{\id_{Q}\times d_{Q}}Q\times_{X}H\xrightarrow{a_{Q}}Q

is projection onto the second factor.

6.8.10.

We mention two functoriality properties of the difference map. First, if
X^{\prime}
is an
X
-scheme, and
Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q_{X^{\prime}}
is the base-changed torsor under
H^{\prime}\coloneqq H_{X^{\prime}}
over
X^{\prime}
, then
d_{Q^{\prime}}
is the base-change of
d_{Q}
to
X^{\prime}
. This follows simply from the description by taking
T
to be an
X^{\prime}
-scheme instead of an
X
-scheme. Second, if
Q_{i}
is a torsor under
H_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
e_{1}\colon H_{1}\to H_{2}
is an isomorphism of group schemes over
X
and
e_{2}\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{2}
is an isomorphism that intertwines with
e_{1}
and the actions, then the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{Q_{1}\times_{X}Q_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{2}\times e_{2}}
\scriptstyle{d_{Q_{1}}}
\textstyle{H_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{2}\times_{X}Q_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{Q_{2}}}
\textstyle{H_{2}}

6.8.11.

Let
P
be as in
6.8.5
. Then
6.8.9
for
H=\omega(\pi_{R})
,
Q=\omega(P)
, and
X=S_{k^{\prime}}
provides a map of
S_{k^{\prime}}
-schemes
d_{\omega(P)}\colon\omega(P)\times_{S_{k^{\prime}}}\omega(P)\to\omega(\pi_{S})
. We wish to show in
6.8.13
that the difference map can be upgraded to a map
P\times P\to\pi_{S}
of
\mathcal{T}_{S}
-schemes.

6.8.12.

As described in
[
48
, 5.11]
(in the case
S
is a field), to upgrade this to a map in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
, it suffices to define such a map for every
\omega^{\prime}\in\Fib(\mathcal{T}_{S})(S^{\prime})
for an
S
-scheme
S^{\prime}
, compatible with base-change and isomorphisms between fiber functors. In fact, the same is true for general
S
by the description of affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
as stacks affine over
\Fib(\mathcal{T}_{S})
in
B.5.11
.

6.8.13.

Given
\omega^{\prime}
, we have a torsor action in
S^{\prime}
-schemes
\omega^{\prime}(P)\times_{S^{\prime}}\omega^{\prime}(\pi_{S})\to\omega^{\prime}(P)
. We define
(d_{P})_{\omega^{\prime}}
as the inverse torsor map associated to
H=\omega^{\prime}(\pi_{R})
,
Q=\omega^{\prime}(P)
, and
X=S^{\prime}
.

If
\omega^{\prime}\in\Fib(\mathcal{T}_{S})(S^{\prime})
and
S^{\prime\prime}
is an
S^{\prime}
-scheme, let
\omega^{\prime\prime}
be the image of
\omega^{\prime}
in
\Fib(\mathcal{T}_{S})(S^{\prime\prime})
. Then the torsor action
\omega^{\prime\prime}(P)\times_{S^{\prime\prime}}\omega^{\prime\prime}(\pi_{S})\to\omega^{\prime\prime}(P)
is just the base-change along
S^{\prime\prime}\to S^{\prime}
of
\omega^{\prime}(P)\times_{S^{\prime}}\omega^{\prime}(\pi_{S})\to\omega^{\prime}(P)
. It follows by
6.8.10
that
(d_{P})_{\omega^{\prime\prime}}
is the base-change along
S^{\prime\prime}\to S^{\prime}
of
(d_{P})_{\omega^{\prime}}
.

If
\omega^{\prime}_{1},\omega^{\prime}_{2}\in\Fib(\mathcal{T}_{S})(S^{\prime})
and
g\colon\omega^{\prime}_{1}\to\omega^{\prime}_{2}
is an isomorphism, we get isomorphisms
g(\pi_{S})\colon\omega^{\prime}_{1}(\pi_{S})\to\omega^{\prime}_{2}(\pi_{S})
and
g(P)\colon\omega^{\prime}_{1}(P)\to\omega^{\prime}_{2}(P)
that intertwine with the torsor action, hence a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{1}(P)\times_{S^{\prime}}\omega^{\prime}_{1}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g(P)\times g(P)}
\scriptstyle{(d_{P})_{\omega^{\prime}_{1}}}
\textstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{1}(\pi_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g(\pi_{S})}
\textstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{2}(P)\times_{S^{\prime}}\omega^{\prime}_{2}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d_{P})_{\omega^{\prime}_{2}}}
\textstyle{\omega^{\prime}_{2}(\pi_{S})}

We have thus shown the compatibilities required by
6.8.12
, so we have an inverse torsor map
d_{P}\colon P\times P\to\pi_{S}
in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
.

6.8.14.

Assuming now
S
is affine, we may apply
(\omega_{H})_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}
to
d_{P}\colon P\times P\to\pi_{S}
to get a filtered map

(d_{P})_{\omega}\colon\omega(P)\times\omega(P)\to\omega(\pi_{S})=\omega_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}(\pi_{S})

and thus by Lemma
6.8.3
, a map of
S_{k^{\prime}}
-schemes

F^{0}(d_{P})_{\omega}\colon F^{0}\omega(P)\times F^{0}\omega(P)\to F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})

for which the composition

(13)

F^{0}\omega(P)\times F^{0}\omega(P)\xrightarrow{\Delta\times\id}F^{0}\omega(P)\times F^{0}\omega(P)\times F^{0}\omega(P)\xrightarrow{\id\times F^{0}(d_{P})_{\omega}}F^{0}\omega(P)\times F^{0}\omega(\pi_{S})\xrightarrow{(a_{P})_{\omega}}F^{0}\omega(P)

is projection onto the second factor.

Transitivity now follows: let
S^{\prime}
be an
S_{k^{\prime}}
-scheme,
\omega^{\prime}_{H}
the base-change of
(\omega_{H})_{S_{k^{\prime}}/S}
to
S^{\prime}
, and
p_{1},p_{2}\in F^{0}\omega^{\prime}(P)
. Then base-changing (
13
) shows that
F^{0}(d_{P})_{\omega}(p_{1},p_{2})
is an element of
F^{0}\omega^{\prime}(\pi_{S})
sending
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
, so the action is indeed transitive, and we are done.

6.9.
Abstract Chabauty–Kim diagram: objects

Let
\pi
be an effective prounipotent
\mathcal{T}
-group. We construct morphisms
\beta^{R}
,
\delta
,
\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}
,
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{R}}/F^{0}
of
pointed set
-valued functors
29
29

29

Under the conditions of Theorem
6.4.3
, they are all representable by affine
k^{\prime}
-schemes, but we will not need that for the rest of §
6
.
on
k^{\prime}
-algebras as in the following diagram

\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)_{k^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\omega(\pi)/F^{0}}
\textstyle{Z^{1}\big(U^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)^{\mathbb{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}}
\textstyle{Z^{1}\big(U;\omega(\pi)\big)^{\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{cr}}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{R}}/F^{0}}

We define
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)
as in
B.5.12
and
\omega(\pi)/F^{0}
is as in
6.2.5
. For a
k^{\prime}
-algebra

, we define

Z^{1}\big(U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}});\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)(R)=Z^{1}\big(U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}})_{R};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}\big)

and

Z^{1}\big(U(\omega);\omega(\pi)\big)(R)=Z^{1}\big(U(\omega)_{R};\omega(\pi)_{R}\big).

The
\mathbb{G}
-fixed subsheaf
Z^{1}\big(U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}});\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)^{\mathbb{G}}
is defined in the usual way: an
R
-point
c
of
Z^{1}\big(U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}});\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)
is said to be fixed by
\mathbb{G}
if it’s fixed by the action of
\mathbb{G}(R^{\prime})
on
Z^{1}\big(U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}});\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)(R^{\prime})
for every
R
-algebra
R^{\prime}
. We let
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{cr}}
denote the image of
{\mathbb{G}}\subseteq G^{\operatorname{gr}}
under the transport-of-structure isomorphism
\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}\colon G^{\operatorname{gr}}\xrightarrow{\sim}G
induced by
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\colon\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\omega
.

6.10.
Abstract Chabauty–Kim diagram: maps

Having defined the vertices of
6.9
(*) we now define the arrows. Let
R
be a
k^{\prime}
-algebra and let

P\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)_{k^{\prime}}(R).

We define

\delta P\colon U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}})_{R}\to\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)_{R}

on
u\in U(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}})(R^{\prime})
(
R^{\prime}
an arbitrary
R
-algebra) by

(\delta P)(u)=(\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R^{\prime}})^{-1}\cdot u(\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R^{\prime}});

note that
\delta
is an isomorphism by
[
34
, Theorem A.4]
.
We choose
\gamma\in F^{0}\omega(P)
arbitrary and define

\beta^{R}(P)\coloneqq(\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R})^{-1}\cdot\gamma\in\big(\omega(\pi)/F^{0}\big)(R).

The map
\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}
is transport of structure induced by
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}
. The map
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{R}}/F^{0}
is given by evaluation at
\mathfrak{u}^{R}
followed by projection
\omega(\pi)\twoheadrightarrow\omega(\pi)/F^{0}
.

Theorem 6.11
.

In the situation and the notation of
6.6
, Diagram
6.9
(*) commutes.

Proof.

Fix
R
an arbitrary
k^{\prime}
-algebra and
P\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{R}}/F^{0}(\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}(\delta(P)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}\delta P)(\mathfrak{u}^{R})

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big(\delta P(\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}(\mathfrak{u}^{R}))\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big(\delta P((\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{u}^{R}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big(\delta P((\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\circ{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big(\delta P((\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big((\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R})^{-1}\cdot(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R}))\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\big((\gamma^{\mathrm{gr}}_{R})^{-1}\cdot(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}})^{-1}(\gamma^{\mathrm{H}}_{R})\big)

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R})^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{H}}_{R}

\displaystyle\pmod{F^{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{R}(P),

the last step because
\gamma=\gamma^{\mathrm{H}}_{R}\in F^{0}\omega(P)
by Proposition
6.8.1
.
∎

6.11.1.
Remark

The theorem implies that
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{R}}\circ\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}\pmod{F^{0}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{u}^{R}\in U(k^{\prime})
.

6.12.
Remark on splittings

One downside of this approach is that the splitting given by
\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}
does not agree with the splitting used in the setting of mixed Tate motives (e.g., in
[
38
]
,
[
32
]
), in which the weight filtration is canonically split by the Hodge filtration.

Alternatively, we may replace
\tau^{\mathrm{cr}}
by
\tau^{\mathrm{H}}
; in fact, we can then circumnavigate
\tau^{\mathrm{H}}
altogether. Our goal in §
6.13
-
6.14
is to record this variant.

6.13.
Left-handed variant

With notation as in §
6.10
-
6.9
, we define the
(left) localization-realization map

\mathrm{LocReal}=\beta^{L}\colon H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)_{k^{\prime}}\to F^{0}\backslash\omega(\pi)

by choosing
\gamma\in F^{0}\omega(P)
and setting

\beta^{L}(P)=(\gamma)^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R}.

Letting
\tau^{\mathrm{H}}
denote transport-of-structure along
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
,
{\mathbb{G}}_{H}
the image of
{\mathbb{G}}\subseteq G^{\operatorname{gr}}
under
\tau^{\mathrm{H}}\colon G^{\operatorname{gr}}\xrightarrow{\sim}G
, and
\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\operatorname{gr}}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}
, we get a diagram

\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)_{k^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{L}}
\textstyle{Z^{1}\big(U^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\big)^{\mathbb{G}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\mathrm{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{gr}}^{L}}}
\textstyle{Z^{1}\big(U;\omega(\pi)\big)^{\mathbb{G}_{H}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{L}}}
\textstyle{F^{0}\backslash\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F^{0}\backslash\omega(\pi).}

Theorem 6.14
.

Diagram
6.13
(*) commutes.

Proof.

This is similar to
6.11
. If
c^{\mathrm{gr}}\in Z^{1}\big(U^{\mathrm{gr}};\pi^{\mathrm{gr}}\big)^{\mathbb{G}}(R)
, then
c\coloneqq\tau^{\mathrm{H}}(c^{\mathrm{gr}})
is given by the composition indicated in the diagram below left.

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}(\cdot)\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}}
\textstyle{\omega(\pi)}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{u}^{L}=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}\circ(\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\gamma^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R}}
\textstyle{U^{\mathrm{gr}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c^{\mathrm{gr}}}
\textstyle{\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathrm{gr}}^{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
