Suppose now that
c=\delta(P)
comes from a
\pi_{R}
-torsor
P\in H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
. Then, as depicted above right, we have

\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{L}}\big(\tau^{\mathrm{H}}(\delta P)\big)=c(\mathfrak{u}^{L})=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}(c^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\operatorname{gr}}))=(\gamma^{\mathrm{H}}_{R})^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}_{R}\equiv\beta^{L}(P)\pmod{F^{0}}.

∎

6.14.1.
Remark

As before, the theorem implies that
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{L}}\circ\tau^{\mathrm{H}}=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\circ\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\operatorname{gr}}}\pmod{F^{0}}
is independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{u}^{L}\in U(k^{\prime})
.

6.14.2.
Remark

One advantage of the approach of §
6.13
-
6.14
is that
\beta^{L}
agrees with the usual Bloch–Kato logarithm in the case that
\pi
is abelian. This has been the more recent trend in Chabauty–Kim theory (e.g.,
[
21
, §4.2.1]
), although the original papers of Kim (e.g.,
[
83
, Proposition 1]
) used
\beta^{R}
.

Remark 6.15
.

If
M\in\mathcal{T}^{ss}
is semisimple, and
\omega^{W}
is a weight-filtered fiber functor, then
\omega^{W}(M)
is canonically split. Moreover, any splitting of
\omega^{W}
induces the canonical splitting on
\omega^{W}(M)
. In particular, in the setting of paragraph
6.13
, if
\pi
is semisimple, then the isomorphism between the graded and ungraded versions of the
p
-adic Albanese variety induced by
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
(arrow labeled
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
in Diagram
6.13
(*)) is just the canonical splitting of the weight filtration and thus independent of choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
.

7.
p
-Adic Periods for Motivic Structures and Galois Representations

In this section, we apply the abstract considerations of §
4
-
6
to the categories of arithmetic interest mentioned in §
3
. We discuss in particular our
p
-adic period conjecture and the relation to syntomic regulators. We leave out all discussion of non-abelian cohomology and the Chabauty–Kim method, as those are the subject of §
8
.

7.1.
Categories of motivic structures

Definition 7.2
.

Let
K
be a number field. We define a
category of motivic structures over
K
(resp.
category of weight-semisimple strongly geometric
p
-adic Galois representations
) to be a full thick Tannakian subcategory
\mathcal{T}
of the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
).

Let
S\subseteq S_{f}(K)
. We define a
category of lisse motivic structures over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
(resp.
category of lisse
p
-adic Galois representations over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
) to be a full thick Tannakian subcategory
\mathcal{T}
of the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
(Definition
3.6.5
) (resp.
\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}
).

The thickness property ensures that if
M\in\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
where

\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}),\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}}),\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sg}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}},\Rep_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}^{\mathrm{sf,S}}(G_{K})^{\mathrm{wss}}\},

then

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),M)=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),M).

Remark 7.2.1
.

A category of systems is evidently weight-filtered by
2.5.11
.

Example 7.2.2
.

For
A
an abelian variety over
K
with good reduction outside
S
, let
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}({\mathbb{Q}},A)
(
3.8.2
). Then
\mathcal{T}
is a category of lisse motivic structures over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
.

7.2.3.
Notation

For a category
\mathcal{T}
of (lisse) motivic structures (resp. Galois representations) and

\Xi\in\{G,U,G^{\operatorname{gr}},U^{\operatorname{gr}},L^{W}={\mathbb{G}},U^{W}_{H}=F^{0}U\},

we set

\Xi(\mathcal{T})\coloneqq\Xi(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}),

which is a pro-algebraic group over
K
(resp.
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
). For

\mathcal{T}=\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}),\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}}),\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}},A),\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},A),

we refer to
\Xi(\mathcal{T})
, respectively, by

\Xi(K),\Xi(\mathcal{O}_{K,S}),\Xi(A),\Xi(\mathcal{O}_{K,S},A).

In the latter cases, note that
{\mathbb{G}}(A)={\mathbb{G}}(A,\mathcal{O}_{K,S})
is independent of
S
.

7.3.
Period loops for motivic structures and Galois representations

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a category of lisse motivic structures (resp. lisse
p
-adic Galois representations) over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
,
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)\setminus S
, and
U=U(\mathcal{T})
. Using the Hodge-filtered Frobenius equivariant fiber functor of
3.5.3
(resp.
3.2.2
) and a choice of arithmetic Hodge path, Definition
5.6
gives us the right-hand and left-hand
unipotent
\mathfrak{p}
-adic period loop of
\mathcal{T}

\mathfrak{u}^{R}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathfrak{u}^{R}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathcal{T})\in U(K_{\mathfrak{p}})

\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathcal{T})\in U(K_{\mathfrak{p}})

Dually, we get
\mathfrak{p}
-adic period homomorphisms

\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{R}=\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{R}(\mathcal{T})\colon\mathcal{O}(U)\to K_{\mathfrak{p}}

\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}=\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(\mathcal{T})\colon\mathcal{O}(U)\to K_{\mathfrak{p}},

whose restrictions to
\mathcal{O}(F^{0}\backslash U)
(resp.
\mathcal{O}(U/F^{0})
) are independent of the choice of arithmetic Hodge path.

7.3.1.
Remark

By Remark
3.5.4
, the versions for motivic structures and Galois representations, respectively, correspond under
\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}
.

We now formulate a
p
-adic version of the Kontsevich–Zagier Period Conjecture, generalizing that of
[
115
]
:

Conjecture 7.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a category of lisse motivic structures over
\mathcal{O}_{K,S}
and
\mathfrak{p}\notin S
. Then
\mathfrak{u}^{R}_{\mathfrak{p}}
(resp.
\mathfrak{u}^{L}_{\mathfrak{p}}
) is dense in
F^{0}\backslash U
(resp.
U/F^{0}
). Equivalently,
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{R}
(resp.
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
) is injective on
\mathcal{O}(F^{0}\backslash U)
(resp.
\mathcal{O}(U/F^{0})
).

7.3.3.
Remark

This should be compared with the closely-related conjectures of Ancona–Frăţilă
[
2
, Conjectures 8.1,8.3]
. Theirs would be more likely candidates for the “correct”
p
-adic period conjecture in the pure case. However, if one wants an actual conjecture and not a meta-conjecture whose formulation depends on other conjectures (c.f.
[
2
, §9]
), then the conjecture of Ancona-Frăţilă is largely limited to the setting of pure motives. Indeed, in order to formulate a similar conjecture for our categories of mixed motivic structures, these categories would need good special fibers over finite fields. For a fuller discussion of these issues and for a comparison in the mixed Tate setting, see
[
40
]
.

7.4.
p
-adic periods and
p
-adic regulators

7.4.1.
Introduction

In this subsection, we establish a relationship between our
p
-adic period point
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and syntomic regulators. In the case of mixed Tate motives, i.e.
\mathcal{T}=\mathbf{MT}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S},{\mathbb{Q}})
, we have an embedding

\bigoplus_{n}K_{2n-1}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})^{(n)}\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}(-n)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}=\bigoplus_{n}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}({\mathbb{Q}}(0),{\mathbb{Q}}(n))\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}(-n)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(U(\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}})).

Restricting the period map

\mathcal{O}(U)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{per}_{p}}{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}

to

K_{2n-1}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})^{(n)}\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}(-n)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}

for a fixed
n
, we equivalently get a map

K_{2n-1}(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})^{(n)}\to{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)_{{\mathrm{dR}}}.

For
n\leq 0
, the left-hand side is zero, so this is not interesting. But for
n\geq 1
, the right-hand side is precisely the syntomic cohomology

H^{1}_{\syn}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}},n),

and the map is identified with the syntomic regulator.

7.4.2.
Bloch–Kato Exponential Map

With notation as in §
7.3
,
30
30

30

Our notation reflects the case of motivic structures, but Proposition
7.4.5
holds in the case of
p
-adic Galois representations as well.
let
M
be an object of
\mathcal{T}
with
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{0}M=0
. Then
1-\phi
induces an isomorphism on
M_{\mathrm{cr}}
, so we have by
[
22
, Corollary 3.8.4]
an isomorphism

\operatorname{D}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(M_{p})/F^{0}\xrightarrow{\exp_{\mathrm{BK}}}H^{1}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};M_{p}),

whose inverse is denoted
\log_{\mathrm{BK}}
. Note that
\iota_{\mathfrak{p}}
(
3.3.2
) induces an isomorphism
M_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}\coloneqq M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}K_{\mathfrak{p}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{D}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(M_{p})
. We consider the composition

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}}

\displaystyle H^{1}_{f,S}(G_{K};M_{p})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{loc}_{\mathfrak{p}}}H^{1}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};M_{p})\xrightarrow[\sim]{\log_{\mathrm{BK}}}M_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}/F^{0}.

Dually, this gives an element

\displaystyle LM_{\mathrm{BK}}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\homo(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M),{\mathbb{Q}})\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}M_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}/F^{0}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\homo(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}/F^{0})^{\vee},K_{\mathfrak{p}}).

We would like to relate
LM_{\mathrm{BK}}
to
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}\colon\mathcal{O}(U/F^{0})\to K_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

7.4.3.
Extensions and Graded Galois Groups

Let
\omega=\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
. For
M
as in
7.4.2
, we first define a canonical map

a_{M}^{\operatorname{gr}}\colon\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{\vee}\to\mathcal{O}(U^{\operatorname{gr}}).

Given
c\otimes f\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{\vee}
, we choose an extension

0\to M\to E\to\mathbb{Q}(0)\to 0,

representing
c
and then apply
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}
to get an extension

0\to\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)\to\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(E)\to{\mathbb{Q}}\to 0

of
G^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{T})
-representations. Since
{\mathbb{G}}
is reductive, the sequence

0\to\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{{\mathbb{G}}}\to\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(E)^{{\mathbb{G}}}\to{\mathbb{Q}}^{{\mathbb{G}}}\to 0

is again exact. But
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{{\mathbb{G}}}=0
since
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{0}M=0
, so
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(E)^{{\mathbb{G}}}={\mathbb{Q}}^{{\mathbb{G}}}={\mathbb{Q}}
. Therefore, there is a unique
1_{E}\in\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(E)^{{\mathbb{G}}}
mapping to
1\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, which is the
\gamma^{\operatorname{gr}}
of Proposition
6.3.2
for
M
effective. Since
U^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{T})
acts trivially on
{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have for every
u\in U^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{T})
that

-1_{E}+u(1_{E})\in\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M).

We define
a_{M}^{\operatorname{gr}}(c\otimes f)
to be the function

u\in U^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathcal{T})\mapsto f(-1_{E}+u(1_{E})).

For
M
effective, there is another description of
a_{M}^{\operatorname{gr}}
via the map
\delta
of §
6.10
. We note that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)_{K}\overset{}{\simeq}\,H^{1}(\mathcal{T};M)_{K}=H^{1}(G^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M))\xrightarrow[\delta]{\sim}Z^{1}(U^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M))^{\mathbb{G}}.

Given

c\otimes f\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{\vee}\simeq Z^{1}(U^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M))^{\mathbb{G}}\otimes_{K}\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)^{\vee},

the functional
f\colon\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M)\to K
is an element of
\mathcal{O}(\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(M))
. We have
a_{M}^{\operatorname{gr}}(c\otimes f)=c^{\#}(f)
.

7.4.4.
Extensions and Ungraded Galois Groups

Let us now fix a choice of arithmetic Hodge path
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}\colon\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\omega
. This gives us an isomorphism
\tau^{\mathrm{H}}\colon G^{\operatorname{gr}}\to G
restricting to an isomorphism
U^{\operatorname{gr}}\to U
and
1_{H}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}(1_{E})\in\omega(E)
. Note that
1_{H}\in F^{0}\omega(E)
.

We define

a_{M}\colon\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\omega(M)^{\vee}\to\mathcal{O}(U)

depending on
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
(but independent if
M
is semisimple), as follows.

The simplest way is to transport everything via the isomorphisms
\omega^{\mathrm{gr}}(V)\to\omega(V)
and
U^{\operatorname{gr}}\to U
induced by
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
. But we may give a description parallel to the one above by letting
a_{M}(c\otimes f)
be the function sending
u\in U
to
f(-1_{H}+u(1_{H}))
.

Proposition 7.4.5
.

For any choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
, the composition

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(\omega(M)/F^{0})^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\omega(M)^{\vee}\xrightarrow{a_{M}}\mathcal{O}(U)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}

is

LM_{\mathrm{BK}}\in\homo(\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}/F^{0})^{\vee},K_{\mathfrak{p}}).

Proof.

By definition of
a_{M}
, we have
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(a_{M}(c\otimes f))=f(-1_{H}+u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(1_{H}))
. But
u_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(1_{H})=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}({\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}}^{-1}(1_{H}))=\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{cr}}(1_{E})
is by Lemma
6.7.1
the unique
\phi
-invariant element of
\omega(E)
mapping to
1\in{\mathbb{Q}}
, which we denote by
1_{\mathrm{cr}}
. Thus

\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(a_{M}(c\otimes f))=f(-1_{H}+1_{\mathrm{cr}}).

But
-1_{H}+1_{\mathrm{cr}}\in M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}/F^{0}
is precisely
\log_{\mathrm{BK}}(c)
, so indeed

\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(a_{M}(c\otimes f))=\log_{\mathrm{BK}}(c\otimes f),

and we are done.
∎

7.4.6.
Syntomic Cohomology and Regulators

We recall the theory of syntomic regulators. A common theme in results about Iwasawa theory and
p
-adic L-functions is the computation of syntomic regulators of specific elements in algebraic
K
-theory or motivic cohomology. See for example
[
13
,
41
,
86
,
87
]
.

While ultimately the connection with
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is just a reformulation of Proposition
7.4.5
, we thought it fruitful to elucidate the connection given the importance of syntomic regulators. In Example
7.4.10
, we describe an application of this to computing
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}
for use in the Chabauty–Kim method.

Let
\mathcal{X}\to V\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}}
smooth and projective,
X=\mathcal{X}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}
, and let
\kappa
be the residue field. For
k,n\geq 0
, we have a quasi-isomorphism (
[
15
, Proposition 9.9]
,
[
50
, A.2]
):

(14)

\RGamma^{\mathrm{rig}}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)\simeq\RGamma_{f}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{K}(n))\coloneqq[\RGamma_{\mathrm{rig}}(\mathcal{X}_{\kappa}/\kappa)^{\varphi=p^{n}}\to\RGamma_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)/F^{n}]

where the first is defined in
[
15
, §6]
and the second in
[
90
, §2.1]
and
[
91
, §3]
. We denote the cohomology groups by
H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)
.

There are syntomic Fontaine–Messing “period morphisms”

\alpha_{k,n}\colon H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)\to H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)),

known as ‘
l
’ in
[
91
, §4-5]
, and syntomic Chern classes (
[
15
, §7]
or
[
90
, Theorem 2.1]
)

c_{n,m}^{\syn}\colon K_{m}(\mathcal{X})\to H^{2n-m}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)

that agree with the usual étale Chern classes (
[
90
, Proposition 3.4]
). Via the Chern character (see e.g.
[
106
, §7.1]
), one gets maps

(15)

K_{2n-k}(\mathcal{X})^{(n)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{reg}_{\syn}}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n).

compatible with cup products, which we call
syntomic regulators
. The composition (first isomorphism because
\mathcal{X}
is regular)

\CH^{n}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\simeq K_{2n-k}(\mathcal{X})^{(n)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{reg}_{\syn}}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)\xrightarrow{\rho_{\syn}}H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))

is thus the same as

\CH^{n}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to\CH^{n}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\cyc_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}}H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)).

Niziol (
[
90
, §5]
) shows that (
14
) is computed by a spectral sequence with
E^{i,j}_{2}=H^{i}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};H^{j}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))
, and this spectral sequence maps to the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence
E^{i,j}_{2}=H^{i}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};H^{j}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))
computing
H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))
via the inclusion
H^{i}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};-)\hookrightarrow H^{i}(G_{{\mathfrak{p}}};-)
on
E_{2}
-terms and
\alpha_{k,n}
on the limit.

The description (
14
) induces a map

H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)/F^{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n).

By
[
15
, Proposition 9.11]
, the composition

H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)/F^{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{k,n}}H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(X;{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))\to H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))

is
0
. Letting
H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)_{0}\coloneqq\operatorname{Ker}(H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)\to H^{k}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))
, the induced map

H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)/F^{n}\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X},n)_{0}\to H^{1}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};H^{k-1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))

is
\exp_{\mathrm{BK}}
.

7.4.7.
Syntomic regulators and the
p
-adic period map

Now suppose that
X
is a smooth projective variety over
K
with smooth projective model
\mathcal{X}
over the localization
\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}
whose base-change to
V
we denote
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Recall from §
3.7
that we have a map

\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}\colon\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}}))}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n)).

By
[
90
, Theorems 3.1, 3.2]
, the image of

\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}}H^{1}(G_{K};H_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}^{k-1}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))

lands in
H^{1}_{f}(G_{K};H_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}^{k-1}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))
, which implies that the image of

\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(X,2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))

lands in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))
.

We may define

\displaystyle\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\otimes H_{k-1}^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)(n)\xrightarrow{\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}\otimes\id}\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))\otimes H_{k-1}^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)(n)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathbf{SM}_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathbb{Q}})}}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))\otimes\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n))^{\vee}\xhookrightarrow{a_{h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{k-1}(X)(n)}}\mathcal{O}(U)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}

Thus
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
defines an associated map

\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X)\otimes_{K}K_{\mathfrak{p}}=H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}}),

and we take the composition

(16)

\begin{split}\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})(n)\twoheadrightarrow\\
H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})(n)/F^{0}\simeq H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})/F^{n}\xrightarrow{\delta}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}},n)\end{split}

Proposition 7.4.8
.

The map of (
16
) defined using our
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}
is the same the syntomic cycle class map

(17)

\begin{split}\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\hookrightarrow\CH^{n}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\\
\simeq K_{2n-k}(\mathcal{X})^{(n)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to K_{2n-k}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}})^{(n)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{reg}_{\syn}}H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}},n).\end{split}

Proof.

Note that the images of both lie in
H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}},n)_{0}
. We have an injection

\beta\colon H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}},n)_{0}\simeq H^{1}_{f}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};H^{k-1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n)))\hookrightarrow H^{1}(G_{\mathfrak{p}};H^{k-1}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}(\overline{X};{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(n))),

where the first isomorphism is from the spectral sequence for
H^{k}_{\syn}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{p}},n)
, noting that
H^{i}_{f}
vanishines for
i>1
. We compose both (
16
) and (
17
) with
\beta
and prove the results are equal.

By compatibility between syntomic and étale Chern classes, (
17
) just becomes
\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}
.

By Proposition
7.4.5
, the map

\CH^{n}_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{X},2n-k)_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})(n)/F^{0}

in (
16
) is

\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}\circ\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}=\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}.

But
\beta\circ\delta
is
\exp_{\mathrm{BK}}
, so in total we get
\exp_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}
, which is
\AJ_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}}}
as desired.

∎

7.4.9.
Remark

Proposition
7.4.8
implies that the composition (
16
) is independent of choice of
\mathfrak{p}^{\mathrm{H}}
. Ultimately, this is a result of taking the projection
H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})(n)\twoheadrightarrow H^{k-1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(X_{\mathfrak{p}})(n)/F^{0}
.

Example 7.4.10
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
K
with good model
\mathcal{E}
over
Z\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K}}
containing
\mathfrak{p}
. Let
\sigma\in K_{2}(\mathcal{E})
, defined explicitly so that its image in
K_{2}(K(E))\simeq K_{2}^{M}(K(E))
is
f\otimes g
, for
f,g\in K(E)^{\times}
. Let
\omega_{0},\omega_{1}
be a basis of differential forms of the second kind on
E
. Let
M=h_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(E)(1)=h^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}}(E)(2)
, and let
k=n=m=2
. We have
\omega_{0}\otimes(\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{1}),\omega_{1}\otimes(\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{1})\in M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}^{\vee}=H^{1}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(E)(-1)
.

Via
\AJ_{{\mathrm{mot}}}
, we get a class
\sigma_{\mathbf{SM}}\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{Z}({\mathbb{Q}}))}({\mathbb{Q}},h_{\mathbf{SM}}^{1}(E)(2))
. Fixing a choice of arithmetic Hodge path, we send
\sigma_{\mathbf{SM}}\otimes\omega_{0}\otimes(\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{1}),\sigma_{\mathbf{SM}}\otimes\omega_{1}\otimes(\omega_{0}\wedge\omega_{1})
via
7.4.4
to elements
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{O}(U(Z))
.

By Proposition
7.4.8
, the numbers
\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(\sigma_{0}),\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(\sigma_{1})
may be computed in terms of the syntomic regulator
K_{2}(\mathcal{E})\to H^{2}_{\syn}(\mathcal{E},2)_{0}\simeq M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}/F^{0}=M_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
.

The ratio
\frac{\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(\sigma_{0})}{\operatorname{per}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}(\sigma_{1})}
(which is independent of
\sigma
) is a ratio of coefficients of a Chabauty–Kim function, specifically
-\frac{2c_{2}}{c_{1}}
in the notation of
[
33
, Theorem 1.4]
. The first author and M. Lüdtke have already computed such a function for a particular elliptic curve by testing the general form of the theorem of loc.cit. on known
{\mathbb{Z}}[\frac{1}{2}]
-points; this thus produces a computation of the syntomic regulator
via the Chabauty–Kim method
.

This syntomic regulator may alternatively be computed using
p
-adic integration via the method of
[
16
,
18
]
, and comparing them is the subject of work-in-progress by the first author and M. Lüdtke.

8.
Applications to Chabauty–Kim

Our goal in this section is to explain how our main result (Theorem
6.14
) fits into the Chabauty–Kim method, specifically the computation of the localization map from the global Selmer variety to the local Selmer variety. We focus on categories of motivic structures rather than Galois representations; the latter is dealt with in the parallel work
[
34
]
.

8.1.
Fundamental group and path torsors

We explain how to use the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
to study the Chabauty–Kim method via motivic structures. In particular, we construct a motivic version
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)
of the unipotent fundamental group of
X
in the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
and define a unipotent Kummer map

X(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})\to H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)).

The key is to define path torsors between any two
a,b\in X(\mathcal{O}_{K,S})
. The Betti, de Rham, and
\ell
-adic realizations of these path torsors appear already in
[
48
, §10]
, which, modulo the lack of a
p
-adic comparison in loc.cit., allows one to define a version
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,b)
of the unipotent fundamental group of
X
in the category
\mathbf{SPM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
. The key step is thus to show that this lifts to the level of Voevodsky’s geometric motives.

We first describe the explicit approach of
[
65
, §4.2]
(c.f. also
[
43
, §3.12]
), originally due to Beilinson, to constructing an inductive object of
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
that realizes
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,b)
. We then make remarks about how more modern technology allows one to get this construction in a cleaner and more abstract way.

8.1.1.

Let
X
be an arbitrary smooth connected variety over a field
K
of characteristic
0
. Goncharov (
[
65
, §4.2]
) defines explicit complexes of varieties
P^{n}(X;a,b)
associated to
X,a,b
, and we let
\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b)\in\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
denote the object associated to
P^{n}(X;a,b)[n]
, so that

H^{0}_{B}(\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b))=H^{n}_{B}(P^{n}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b))=\mathcal{P}^{n}(X({\mathbb{C}});a,b)\coloneqq\mathcal{P}(X({\mathbb{C}});a,b)/\mathcal{I}_{a,b}^{n+1}

in the notation of
[
65
, §4.1]
, and so that we get a map

p_{n}\colon\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b)\to\mathcal{P}^{n-1}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b)

for each
n
coming from the boundary map
P^{n}(X;a,b)_{\bullet}\to P^{n-1}(X;a,b)_{\bullet}[-1]
on p.45 of lot.cit.

For
K
a number field, we note that

\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)\coloneqq H^{0}(\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SM}}(\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{M}}(X;a,b)))\in\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})

by definition of the latter. The inductive limit

\varinjlim_{n}\mathcal{P}^{n}_{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)^{\vee}

along the duals of
p_{n}
is a Hopf algebra in
\operatorname{Ind}{\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})}
. We denote its spectrum by
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)
, which is an affine scheme in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. By Theorems 4.1, 4.3, and 4.4 of
[
65
]
, its Betti,
\ell
-adic, and de Rham components give the usual Betti,
\ell
-adic étale, and de Rham unipotent path torsors of
X
. For
a=b
, we denote it by
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,a)
.

8.1.2.
Remark

There are multiple recent constructions of a path-space torsor between any two points in a model-theoretic or infinity-categorical setup: see
{{}_{\eta}B_{\xi}}
of
[
36
, 2.2]
,
P_{X}(x,y)
of
[
78
, 3.12]
, and a simple modification (c.f. Remark
8.2.3
(
1
))
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{un}}(X,x_{0},x_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{geo}}(X,x_{0},x_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
of
[
6
, Construction 4.5.1]
. In all cases, these give objects in the associated homotopy category, which in all cases is equivalent to
DM(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
(also denoted
DA(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
), the triangulated category of motivic complexes over
K
with rational coefficients, of which
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
is the full subcategory of compact objects.

8.2.
Tangential basepoints

We may also include the case of tangential basepoints if
X
is a curve with a
K
-point, as follows. Letting
X^{\prime}
denote the smooth compactification of
X
and
Y=X^{\prime}\setminus X
, a tangential basepoint is a pair
b=(\overline{b},v)
with
\overline{b}\in Y(K)
and
v\in T_{\overline{b}}(X^{\prime})\setminus\{0\}
. Work of Deligne (
[
48
, §15]
) produces a pro-algebraic group
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X;a,b)
in
\mathbf{SPM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
when
a
or
b
(or both) is a tangential basepoint. We prove:

Theorem 8.2.1
.

If
X(K)\neq\emptyset
,
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X;a,b)
is in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
(and we denote it by
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)
).

8.2.2.
Proof of Theorem
8.2.1

We modify the proof of
[
43
, Théorème 4.4]
. First, note that as in the proof of
[
43
, 4.11]
, it suffices to treat the case where only one of
a
or
b
is tangential (this uses the fact that
X(K)
is nonempty). Let us assume
b
is tangential. The same argument also shows it suffices to prove it for a single
a\in X(K)
for each
b
. Let us suppose that only
b=(\overline{b},v)
is tangential.

Let
g
be the genus of
X^{\prime}
and
c
an auxiliary closed point of
X^{\prime}
not equal to
a
or
\overline{b}
. We choose
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
n\coloneqq\deg(m[c])>2g
. Then the divisors
-[\overline{b}]+m[c]
,
-[a]-[\overline{b}]+m[c]
, and
-2[\overline{b}]+m[c]
are all of degree greater than
2g-2
, so by Riemann–Roch, we have

\displaystyle\dim{H^{0}(X^{\prime};-[\overline{b}]+m[c])}=\deg(-[\overline{b}]+m[c])=n-1

\displaystyle\dim{H^{0}(X^{\prime};-[a]-[\overline{b}]+m[c])}=\deg(-[a]-[\overline{b}]+m[c])=n-2

\displaystyle\dim{H^{0}(X^{\prime};-2[\overline{b}]+m[c])}=\deg(-2[\overline{b}]+m[c])=n-2.

Since
K
is of characteristic
0
and therefore infinite, we may find
u\in H^{0}(X^{\prime};-[\overline{b}]+m[c])\setminus(H^{0}(X^{\prime};-[a]-[\overline{b}]+m[c])\cup H^{0}(X^{\prime};-2[\overline{b}]+m[c]))\subseteq K(X)=K(X^{\prime})
. In other words,
u
is a uniformizer at
\overline{b}
and is defined and non-vanishing at
a
.

Viewing
u
as a map from
X^{\prime}
to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, let
U
denote preimage of
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
in
X
. Note that
a\in U
.

Then
a\in U(K)
and
b
is a tangential basepoint of
U
, so we may consider
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)
. The map
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X;a,b)
induced by the inclusion
U\hookrightarrow X
is surjective, so it suffices to prove that
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)
is in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

The scheme
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)
is isomorphic to its Lie algebra via the exponential map and therefore may be viewed as a pro-vectorial scheme in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. We consider the twist

\operatorname{Lie}\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1)=\underline{Hom}({\mathbb{Q}}(1),\operatorname{Lie}\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a))

which is a pro-vectorial scheme in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
but without a Lie bracket. The
\underline{Hom}
refers to internal Hom of pro-vectorial schemes in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. We similarly refer to
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1)
as the same scheme as
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)
but with twisted
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
-structure, isomorphic to
\operatorname{Lie}\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1)
via the same exponential map.

There is a canonical map
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U,b)
given by local monodromy, and we compose with the change of basepoint map
(p,\gamma)\mapsto p\gamma p^{-1}
to get a map

\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\times{\mathbb{Q}}(1)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\times\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U,b)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(X,a).

This produces a map

\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\to\underline{Hom}({\mathbb{Q}}(1),\operatorname{Lie}\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a))\simeq\operatorname{Lie}\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1)\simeq\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1).

This map is invariant under the right action of the local monodromy
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)\subseteq\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U,b)
. The existence of a tangential basepoint
b
implies that
U
is affine, so its geometric fundamental group is free, so that
[
43
, Lemma 4.7]
applies and thus
[
43
, Lemma 4.5]
shows that

\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,\overline{b})\coloneqq\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)/{\mathbb{Q}}(1)

embeds into
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(U,a)(-1)
. This implies that
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,\overline{b})
is in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

The map
u\colon U\to{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
induces a map
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U,b)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}},(0,u(v)))
sending the local monodromy
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)
at
b
isomorphically onto
({\mathbb{G}_{m}},(0,u(v)))\simeq{\mathbb{Q}}(1)
because
u
is a uniformizer. It also induces a map
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};u(a),(0,u(v)))
that is equivariant for the
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)
-actions on both, the latter of which makes
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};u(a),(0,u(v)))
into a
{\mathbb{Q}}(1)
-torsor.

The product map

\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)\to\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};u(a),(0,u(v)))\times\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,\overline{b})

is an isomorphism. Finally, the torsor
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};u(a),(0,u(v)))
is the same as
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};u(a),du(v))\simeq\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}({\mathbb{G}_{m}};1,\frac{du(v)}{u(a)})
by
[
48
, 15.51]
. Since the latter is between elements of
{\mathbb{G}_{m}}(K)
, it is in
\mathbf{SM}_{K}({\mathbb{Q}})
. This implies that
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SPM}}(U;a,b)
is as well. ∎

Remark 8.2.3
.

One expects a less ad hoc proof of Theorem
8.2.1
to not require the assumption
X(K)\neq\emptyset
. In fact, there are two approaches:

(1)

Modify
[
6
, Construction 4.5.6]
by replacing
\rm{LS}_{\rm{geo}}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})^{\heartsuit,\otimes}
with the subcategory of unipotent objects (the thick subcategory generated by the unit object), which uses the theory of motivic nearby cycles developed in
[
4
, §3]
.
32
32

32

This construction may be phrased in the language of
[
5
]
by replacing the triangulated category
\mathbf{SmDA}
in 2.4.1 by its thick subcategory
\mathbf{SmDA}^{\mathrm{un}}
generated by
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{SmDA}}
to get a functor

\phi^{\mathrm{un}}_{\Delta*}\colon\mathbf{SmDA}^{\mathrm{un}}(X,{\mathbb{Q}})\to\mathbf{SmDA}^{\mathrm{un}}(X^{2},{\mathbb{Q}}),

taking
\phi_{\Delta*}^{\mathrm{un}}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbf{SmDA}})
to get a Hopf algebroid over
X
, then taking its fiber over
(a,b)
using Corollaire 2.40 or
[
6
, Definition 3.2.18]
in the tangential case.

(2)

Put an appropriate log structure on the complex
P^{n}(X;a,b)
and then use logarithmic motives as in
[
110
]

Note also
[
95
]
, which provides a bridge between the two approaches. Using either of these, all of §
8.3
applies even if
X(K)=\emptyset
.

8.2.4.

Let
w\in S_{f}(K)
. Let
X
be a smooth geometrically connected variety over
K
with smooth compactification
X^{\prime}
. Suppose
X
has strong good reduction at
w
, i.e., it has a smooth model
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{O}_{w}
in which the closure
\mathcal{X}
of
X
has complement
\mathcal{Y}
étale over
\mathcal{O}_{w}
.

Let
b=(\overline{b},v)
be a tangential basepoint and
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
the ideal sheaf of functions vanishing at
\overline{b}
(equivalently, at its closure in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
). We say that
b
is
integral at
w
if
v
sends the image of
H^{0}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime};\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2})\to(\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2})_{\overline{b}}=T_{\overline{b}}(X^{\prime})^{\vee}
to a subset of
\mathcal{O}_{w}\subseteq K_{w}
not contained in
{\mathfrak{m}}_{w}
.

Let each of
a,b
be basepoints integral at
w
. Then the
\ell
-adic component of of
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)
is unramified (resp. crystalline) at
w\notin S_{\ell}(K)
(resp.
w\in S_{\ell}(K)
) by
[
114
, proof of Theorem 2.8]
33
33

33

For the case of tangential basepoints, one can argue as in the proof of Theorem
8.2.1
(with the caveat that
\mathcal{X}(Z)
must be nonempty if
a
and
b
are both tangential).
(resp.
[
93
, Theorem 1.11]
).

In particular if
X
has strong good reduction outside
S\subseteq S_{f}(K)
, and
a,b
are integral outside
S
, then
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X;a,b)
is an affine scheme in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

8.3.
The Chabauty–Kim diagram

We describe the general setup for Chabauty–Kim. Let
K
be a number field,
Z=\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K,S}}
an open subscheme of
\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}_{K}}
, and
\mathcal{X}\to Z
a smooth morphism whose generic fiber
X
is a hyperbolic curve along with smooth proper compactification
\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to Z
such that the complement
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{X}
is étale over
Z
. We denote by
Y,X^{\prime},X
their generic fibers, respectively.

8.3.1.

We fix a
Z
-integral basepoint
b
and consider the unipotent fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)

of §
8.1.1
, which is a pro-algebraic group in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Then
\operatorname{Lie}{\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)}
is a Lie algebra in
\operatorname{Pro}{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})}
whose associated graded for the descending central series filtration is a quotient of the free pronilpotent Lie algebra on
h_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X)\in\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
. Letting
J
be the (generalized) Jacobian of
X
such that
h_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X)=h_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(J)
, we find that

\operatorname{Lie}{\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)}\in\operatorname{Pro}{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)}

and thus that
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)
is a prounipotent group in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
.

Given
z\in\mathcal{X}(Z)
, the construction of
8.2.4
gives a torsor
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X;z,b)
under
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)
in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
. This defines a map

\kappa\colon\mathcal{X}(Z)\to H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)).

8.3.2.

Let
\Pi
be a finite-type quotient of
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)
in
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
,
34
34

34

such as the
n
th quotient
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)_{n}
for the descending central series
and set
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\coloneqq\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\pi)
,
\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}\coloneqq\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}(\pi)=\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}K_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and
\Pi_{p}\coloneqq\mathrm{real}_{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},p}(\Pi)
. Then we have an object

H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J);\Pi)=H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi).

A priori, it is just a functor from
{\mathbb{Q}}
-algebras to pointed sets. Conjecture
3.6.9
implies that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)}(\mathbbm{1},M)
is finite-dimensional for any
M\in\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
. Since
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}},J)
is neutralized by the Betti realization, Theorem
6.4.3
would imply that it is an affine scheme (of finite type by Remark
6.4.4
).
35
35

35

By Remark
6.4.5
, the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-structure doesn’t depend on our use of the Betti realization; in the case
K={\mathbb{Q}}
, we get the exact same
{\mathbb{Q}}
-structure using the de Rham realization to prove Theorem
6.4.3
.

8.3.3.

The projection
\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)\twoheadrightarrow\Pi
defines a map

H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b))\to H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi),

which we can compose with
\kappa
to get
\kappa_{\Pi}
.

We choose a prime
p
and a place
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)\setminus S
. For
z\in\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, we have an étale unipotent torsor
\pi_{1}^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\mathrm{un}}(X;z,b)
with
G_{\mathfrak{p}}
-action, defining a map
\kappa_{\mathfrak{p}}\colon\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\to H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\pi_{1}^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\mathrm{un}}(X,b))
and thus to
H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\Pi_{p})
. This produces the following diagram, whose étale incarnation first appeared (at least for
\Pi=\pi_{1}^{\mathbf{SM}}(X,b)_{n}
) in
[
83
, p.120]
:
36
36

36

One may also consider a version that takes the product over all
\mathfrak{p}\in S_{p}(K)
of the bottom-right two objects as in
[
49
, §3.1 (5)]
.

(18)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\Pi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\mathfrak{p},\Pi}}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\Pi_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\log_{\mathrm{BK}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}F^{0}\backslash\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}.}

Note that
\kappa_{\Pi}
maps into the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-points of
H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)
,
\kappa_{\mathfrak{p},\Pi}
maps into the
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-points of
H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\Pi_{p})
, and
\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi}
is really a map from the base-change of
H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)
from
{\mathbb{Q}}
to
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

8.3.4.

The map
\log_{\mathrm{BK}}
is defined as follows. Let
R
be a
{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
-algebra and
P\in H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};\pi_{p})(R)=H^{1}_{f}(K_{\mathfrak{p}};(\pi_{p})_{R})
. We note
P_{\mathrm{cr}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}(P_{p})
and
P_{{\mathrm{dR}}}\coloneqq\operatorname{D}_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(P_{p})
over
R_{\mathfrak{p},0}\coloneqq R\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}K_{\mathfrak{p},0}
and
R_{\mathfrak{p}}\coloneqq R\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}
, respectively. By
[
83
, Lemma 1]
, there is a unique Frobenius-invariant point
\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}\in P_{\mathrm{cr}}(R_{\mathfrak{p},0})
. Choosing
\gamma^{\mathrm{H}}
in
F^{0}T_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}})
, we set

\log_{\mathrm{BK}}([P_{p}])\coloneqq(\gamma^{\mathrm{H}})^{-1}\gamma^{\mathrm{cr}}\in F^{0}\backslash\pi_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}}(R_{\mathfrak{p}})=\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(F^{0}\backslash\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}},\mathfrak{p}})(R).

By Lemma
6.7.1
, the map
\beta^{L}
of
6.13
is the same as
\log_{\mathrm{BK}}\circ\mathrm{loc}_{\Pi}
.

8.4.
Conclusion

Now that we have formulated our Chabauty–Kim diagram in terms of the category
\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}})
, let us apply the theory of §
6
.

Recall

H^{1}(\mathbf{SM}_{K,S}({\mathbb{Q}});\Pi)_{K}\simeq H^{1}(G;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})\simeq Z^{1}(U^{\operatorname{gr}};\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}},\operatorname{gr}}(\Pi))^{{\mathbb{G}}(J)}\overset{\tau^{\mathrm{H}}}{\simeq}Z^{1}(U;\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\Pi))^{{\mathbb{G}}_{H}(J)}.

Under this isomorphism, we can rewrite (
18
) as

(19)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\Pi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\int_{\mathfrak{p}}}
\textstyle{Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{{\mathbb{G}}_{H}(J)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{0}\backslash\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}}}
\textstyle{F^{0}\backslash\operatorname{Res}_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}}(\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})_{K_{\mathfrak{p}}},}

which is the Theorem of §
1.4
.

This provides a foundation for future work in non-abelian Chabauty beyond Quadratic Chabauty. In particular, it puts us in a generalization of the setup of
[
32
, §2.4.2]
. As in that case, one may write
\mathfrak{ev}_{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}}
as the pullback along
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{L}\colon\operatorname{Spec}{K_{\mathfrak{p}}}\to U
itself of the universal evaluation map

Z^{1}(U;\Pi_{{\mathrm{dR}}})^{{\mathbb{G}}_{H}(J)}\times_{K}U\to F^{0}\backslash\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}(\pi)\times_{K}U.

The computation of this image is thus a purely geometric problem over the function field
K(U)
.

Appendix A
Mixed Motives

A.1.

Abelian categories of mixed motives are the ultimate candidate and main motivation behind the notion of weight-filtered Tannakian category. As they are mostly conjectural, we have relegated them to this short appendix.

A.2.

As originally conjectured in
[
11
, 5.10]
and described in more detail in
[
55
, §4.4.2, §5.3, Conjecture 33]
, there should be a category
\mathcal{MM}_{K}
of
mixed motives over
K
for any field
K
. The rationalization
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})=\mathcal{MM}_{K}\otimes{\mathbb{Q}}
should be a Tannakian category, and for any embedding
\iota\colon K\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
, there should be a corresponding rational singular cohomology realization functor
\omega^{\iota}
that is a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-rational fiber functor. One similarly expects
K
-rational (resp.
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-rational) fiber functors
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
(resp.
\omega^{\ell}=\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}
) from de Rham (resp.
\ell
-adic) cohomology, as long as
K
has characteristic
0
(resp. different from
\ell
).

A.3.

The derived category of bounded complexes of
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
is expected to be naturally isomorphic to
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
, as implied for example in
[
31
, §A.1-A.2]
. This natural isomorphism should further intertwine
\omega^{\iota}
,
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, and
\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}
of
A.2
with
\omega^{B,v}
,
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
, and
\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}
of
3.3.3
via this isomorphism and
\mathrm{real}_{\mathbf{SPM}}
of Theorem
3.6.2
.

A.4.

The properties listed in
[
55
, §4.4.2]
imply that the weight filtration makes
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
into a weight-filtered Tannakian category, as follows.

First, we verify that the filtration is strict. For this, we verify the hypotheses of Lemma
2.5.7
. For
M,N\in\Ob(\mathcal{T})
and
m\neq n
, recall that
\operatorname{gr}_{m}M
and
\operatorname{gr}_{n}N
are in the
m
th and
n
th graded parts, respectively, of the graded category
\mathrm{Mot}_{\mathrm{num}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
, which is the socle (full subcategory of semisimple objects) of
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
. Any subquotient of either is semisimple and thus a subquotient in
\mathrm{Mot}_{\mathrm{num}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
, hence of weights
m
and
n
, respectively. In particular, no two nonzero such subquotients can be isomorphic.

Finally, every
\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M\in\mathrm{Mot}_{\mathrm{num}}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
and is therefore semisimple, so the filtration indeed satisfies Definition
2.5.9
.

A.4.1.

By
2.5.11
, any Tannakian subcategory of
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
should also be a weight-filtered Tannakian category. (In particular, we are interested in subcategories of the form
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{P}}
as described in
[
69
, §3]
.) While this observation may seem trivial, it is important because certain subcategories of the conjectural
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
have been constructed either entirely non-conjecturally or at least more explicitly.

A.5.

In particular, the category
\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}{K},\mathbb{Q})
of
[
43
, 1.1]
is a weight-filtered Tannakian category that is expected to be a Tannakian subcategory of
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
. The former follows from the weight filtration described in loc.cit., including the exactness of the associated graded and the fact that the associated graded is a direct sum of objects of the form
{\mathbb{Q}}(n)
, which are all simple. This category (and its unramified subcategories) has already found extensive applications to the Chabauty–Kim method, as in
[
38
,
39
,
32
]
.

A.6.

Candidates have been constructed for categories of mixed elliptic motives (
[
96
]
,
[
28
]
). Such a category is expected to be the thick Tannakian subcategory of
\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
generated by
h_{1}(E)
for a fixed elliptic curve
E/K
.

A.7.

As mentioned in
A.2
, if
\chara{K}=0
, then algebraic de Rham cohomology provides a fiber functor
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
over
K
.
This functor comes equipped with a Hodge filtration that makes
\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}
into a Hodge-filtered fiber functor as in
2.6.1
.

A.8.

The existence of a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor
is a bit more subtle. For this, we must restrict to motives over a number field
K
that are unramified at some finite place
\mathfrak{p}
of
K
.
The unramifiedness corresponds to crystallinity when considering
p
-adic étale cohomology for
p
the characteristic of
\mathfrak{p}
.

A.8.1.

Under the given notation, it is most natural to refer to such a subcategory as
\mathrm{MMot}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},{\mathbb{Q}})
, where
\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}}
denotes the localization of the ring
\mathcal{O}_{K}
at the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
. Such a category should sit naturally into a square

\textstyle{\mathrm{MMot}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{MMot}(k,{\mathbb{Q}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},\mathbb{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(\operatorname{Spec}{K},\mathbb{Q}),}

where the right vertical arrow is given by the inclusion functor
\mathrm{MMot}(k,{\mathbb{Q}})\hookrightarrow D^{b}(\mathrm{MMot}(k,{\mathbb{Q}}))
sending an object
M
to the complex
M
concentrated in degree
0
followed by the expected equivalence of
A.3
, and
\operatorname{DM}_{\mathrm{gm}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},\mathbb{Q})
is as in
[
31
, Definition 11.1.10]
.

In the special case of
\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}{K},\mathbb{Q})
, we may define
\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},\mathbb{Q})
as the union over
Z\ni\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathrm{MT}(Z,\mathbb{Q})
(
[
43
, 1.6]
), and we expect that
\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},\mathbb{Q})=\mathrm{MMot}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},{\mathbb{Q}})\cap\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}{K},\mathbb{Q})
.

A.8.2.

Whether working with the conjectural
\mathrm{MMot}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},{\mathbb{Q}})
or the non-conjectural
\mathrm{MT}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},\mathbb{Q})
, we expect a
K_{\mathfrak{p},0}
-linear crystalline realization fiber functor
\omega^{\mathrm{cris}}
that intertwines via
A.3
with
R_{\mathrm{rig}}
(
[
31
, 17.2.23]
). This functor should come equipped with natural isomorphisms
\omega^{\mathrm{cris}}\otimes_{K_{\mathfrak{p},0}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}\simeq\omega^{{\mathrm{dR}}}\otimes_{K}K_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\omega^{\mathrm{cris}}\simeq\operatorname{D}_{\mathrm{cris}}\circ\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},p}
. The latter property in fact can serve as a definition of
\omega^{\mathrm{cris}}
, for which one need only assert that
\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},p}(M)
is crystalline at
\mathfrak{p}
for all
M\in\mathrm{MMot}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathfrak{p}},{\mathbb{Q}})
.

A.8.3.

It is widely expected that
\{\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}(M)\}_{\ell}
form a strongly compatible system of Galois representations and that
\omega^{{\textrm{\'{e}t}},\ell}(\operatorname{gr}^{W}_{n}M)
is pure of weight
n
for any
M\in\mathrm{MMot}(K,{\mathbb{Q}})
(
[
108
, p.82]
). If this is the case, then
\omega^{\mathrm{cris}}
is a Frobenius-equivariant fiber functor over
(K_{\mathfrak{p},0},\Frob_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

Appendix B
Background and Technicalities on Tannakian Categories

Here, we review the theory of Tannakian categories along with some technicalities on stacks and groupoids that we need mainly in §
5
-
6
.

B.1.
Basics on Tannakian categories

Let
k
be a field. This material can all be found in
[
97
,
45
,
44
]
.

B.1.1.
Preliminaries

An
additive category
is a category in which all finite biproducts (simultaneous coproducts and products) exist; such a category is automatically enriched over the monoidal category of abelian groups with tensor product. A
k
-linear category
is an additive category further enriched over
k
-vector spaces and
\otimes_{k}
. We assume notions of
symmetric monoidal category
(what is called
ACU
\otimes
-category
in
[
97
, I.2.4.3]
and
tensor category
in
[
45
, II.1.1]
),
dualizable object
(
[
44
, 2.1.2]
), and
abelian category
. A symmetric monoidal category is
rigid
(also called
autonomous
) if all objects are dualizable.

The term
tensor category
seems to have many different definitions in the literature, which range from simply meaning symmetric monoidal category (above) to
[
44
, 2.1]
, which requires abelian, rigid, and the condition that
k\xhookrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{End}\,{(}\mathbbm{1})
. Therefore, we use the term only in the context ‘X tensor category’ for
\emptyset\neq X\subseteq\{\mathrm{additive},\mathrm{abelian},k-\mathrm{linear}\}
; this means a symmetric monoidal category with structure
X
, such that both are compatible (e.g., the tensor product respects biproducts and/or is
k
-linear).

Thus a
rigid abelian
k
-linear tensor category
is a rigid symmetric monoidal abelian
k
-linear category in which the tensor product is biadditive (respects biproducts) and
k
-bilinear.

Any abelian category is in particular an
exact category
; that is, there is a notion of short exact sequence satisfying the conditions listed in
[
27
, Definition 2.1]
.

B.1.2.
Fiber functors

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a symmetric monoidal
k
-linear exact category.
A
fiber functor
on
\mathcal{T}
with values in a
k
-scheme
S
is an exact tensor functor
\mathcal{T}\to\QCoh(S)
.
Note that if
\mathcal{T}
is rigid, then any such functor lands in
\LF(S)
(§
4.3
).
37
37

37

By
[
107
,
Tag 0FNU
(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)]
and
[
107
,
Tag 05P2
(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(4)]
,
\LF(S)=\QCoh(S)_{\mathrm{rig}}
, and by
[
44
, 2.7]
, a symmetric monoidal functor sends dualizable objects to dualizable objects.
.
We denote the groupoid of exact
k
-linear symmetric monoidal functors and symmetric monoidal natural transformations by
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}
, so that the groupoid of
S
-valued fiber functors is

\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T},\QCoh(S))

or, when
\mathcal{T}
is rigid,

\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T},\LF(S)).

If
\omega
is a fiber functor with values in
S
, and
S^{\prime}
is an
S
-scheme, we denote by
\omega_{S^{\prime}}
or
\omega_{S^{\prime}/k}
the composition of
\omega
with the pullback functor
\QCoh(S)\to\QCoh(S^{\prime})
. Note that
\omega_{S/k}=\omega
.

B.1.3.
Definition

A
Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
over
k
is a rigid abelian
k
-linear tensor category possessing a fiber functor over a nonempty
k
-scheme such that the natural map
k\to\mathrm{End}\,{(}\mathbbm{1})
given by
\alpha\mapsto\alpha\id_{\mathbbm{1}}
is an isomorphism.

For the rest of §
B.1
, we let
\mathcal{T}
be a Tannakian category over
k
.

B.1.4.
Tannakian fundamental groups

If
\omega
is a fiber functor on
\mathcal{T}
with values in a
k
-scheme
S
, then
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)\coloneqq\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega)
, the group tensor automorphisms of
\omega
, is naturally a flat affine group schemes over
S
, known as the
Galois group of
\mathcal{T}
at
\omega
. Its functor of points is defined, for an
S
-scheme
S^{\prime}
, by

\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)(S^{\prime})=\underline{\mathrm{Aut}\,}^{\otimes}(\omega_{S^{\prime}}).

B.1.5.
Tannakian subcategories

A
Tannakian subcategory
is a full abelian subcategory containing a unit object and closed under tensor products, duals, and subquotients.

In particular, if
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian subcategory and
\omega
is a fiber functor on
\mathcal{T}
, then
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)\to\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{\prime},\omega)
is faithfully flat (
[
48
, 6.6]
references a proof in the neutral case and states that it is true in general; c.f. also
[
12
, §2]
).

B.1.6.
Algebraic Tannakian Categories

Let
\omega
be a fiber functor over
k^{\prime}
. Following
[
45
, Remark 3.10]
or
[
97
, III.3.3.1]
,
\mathcal{T}
is
algebraic
if any of the following equivalent conditions hold:

•

\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)
is finite-type as an
k^{\prime}
-scheme (i.e., it is an
algebraic
rather than
pro-algebraic
group)

•

\mathcal{T}
is tensor-generated by a single object (“has a tensor generator”)

•

\mathcal{T}
is tensor-generated by a finite collection of objects

From the first condition, it is easy to see that a Tannakian subcategory of an algebraic Tannakian category is algebraic.

B.1.7.
Neutral Tannakian categories

We say that
\mathcal{T}
is
neutral
if it possesses a fiber functor
\omega
with values in
k
. In such a case, Tannakian reconstruction states that the natural functor

\mathcal{T}\to\Rep(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega))

is an equivalence of Tannakian categories. We discuss a generalization to the non-neutral case in
B.3.2
.

B.2.
Algebraic geometry in a Tannakian category

B.2.1.

We work with objects of
\Pro{\mathcal{T}}
and
\Ind\mathcal{T}
. Each is an abelian
k
-linear tensor category.

B.2.2.

If
\omega\colon\mathcal{T}\to\LF(S)
is a fiber functor, we can extend
\omega
to exact tensor functors
\Pro\mathcal{T}\to\QCoh(S)
(resp.
\Ind\mathcal{T}\to\QCoh(S)
) using the fact that
\QCoh(S)
is complete (resp. cocomplete).

B.2.3.

We recall that an
affine scheme in
\mathcal{T}
is a spectrum in the sense of
[
48
, 5.4]
of a commutative ring in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
. A
group scheme

\pi
in
\mathcal{T}
is a group object in affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}
; equivalently, it is the spectrum of a commutative Hopf algebra object
\mathcal{O}(\pi)
in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
. If
\omega
is a fiber functor over a
k
-scheme
S
, then
\omega(\pi)\coloneqq\operatorname{Spec}{\omega(\mathcal{O}(\pi))}
is a group scheme over
S
. C.f.
B.5.11
for another definition of (affine) schemes in
\mathcal{T}
.

B.2.4.

Since
(\Pro{\mathcal{T}})^{\mathrm{op}}
is naturally equivalent to
\Ind\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and duality is an equivalence
\mathcal{T}\simeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{op}}
, duality gives an equivalence

\Ind{\mathcal{T}}\simeq(\Pro{\mathcal{T}})^{\mathrm{op}}.

B.2.5.

If
\pi
is a group scheme in
\mathcal{T}
, we say that
\pi
is
prounipotent
if
\omega(\mathcal{O}(\pi))
is the Hopf algebra of a prounipotent group for some (equivalently any) fiber functor
\omega
over a field. The dual of
\mathcal{O}(\pi)
under
B.2.4
is a Hopf algebra in
\Pro{\mathcal{T}}
denoted
\mathcal{U}\pi
, as it is naturally the (completed) universal envelopping algebra of
\pi
under any fiber functor. Then the kernel of
\mathcal{U}\pi\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}\pi
is a Lie algebra
\operatorname{Lie}\pi
in
\Pro{\mathcal{T}}
, and
\mathcal{U}\pi
is a quotient of the tensor algebra
T(\operatorname{Lie}\pi)
.

B.2.6.

If
\pi
is a group scheme in
\mathcal{T}
, a
torsor in
\mathcal{T}
under
\pi
is a
\mathcal{T}
-scheme
P
with a group action
\pi\times P\to P
in
\mathcal{T}
-schemes for which
\mathcal{O}(P)
is not the zero object of
\mathcal{T}
, and
\pi\times P\to P\times P
is an isomorphism (equivalently, if some fiber functor
\omega
over some field extension of
k
makes
\omega(P)
into a torsor under
\omega(\pi)
). We let
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(k)
denote the pointed set of isomorphism classes of torsors in
\mathcal{T}
under
\pi
, with
\pi
as a torsor under itself forming the distinguished point of this set.

B.3.
Tannakian reconstruction in the non-neutral case

Given a Tannakian category
\mathcal{T}
over
k
and a fiber functor
\omega
over a
k
-scheme
U
, we get a
k
-groupoid acting on
U
as follows (
[
44
, 1.11]
): We let
\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}
denote the projections from
U\otimes_{k}U
to
U
and set

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(\omega)\coloneqq\underline{\operatorname{Isom}}^{\otimes}(\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega,\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega).

Then
\mathcal{G}
is a scheme over
U\times_{k}U
with projections to
U
denoted
b
and
s
. For a
k
-scheme
T
, an element
g\in\mathcal{G}(T)
thus gives a pair
b(g),s(g)\in U(T)
of
T
-points of
U
, and
g\in\operatorname{Isom}^{\otimes}(s(g)^{*}\omega,b(g)^{*}\omega)
. There is thus a composition map

\circ\colon\mathcal{G}\times_{{{}^{s}}U^{b}}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G},

where, if
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}
, with
s(g_{1})=t_{1}
,
b(g_{1})=s(g_{2})=t_{2}
, and
b(g_{2})=t_{3}
, then

g_{2}\circ g_{1}\colon t_{1}^{*}\omega\xrightarrow{g_{1}}t_{2}^{*}\omega\xrightarrow{g_{2}}t_{3}^{*}\omega.

Then
(\mathcal{G},\circ,s,b)
forms a transitive (meaning
\mathcal{G}
is fpqc over
U\times_{k}U
)
k
-groupoid acting on
U
in the sense of
[
44
, 1.6]
. In the notation of
[
107
,
Tag 0231
]
,
(U,\mathcal{G},s,b,\circ)
is a groupoid scheme over
\operatorname{Spec}{k}
.

B.3.1.

A
representation
of
\mathcal{G}
is the data of a quasi-coherent sheaf
V
on
U
and maps
\rho(g)\colon V_{s(g)}\to V_{b(g)}
for every
g\in\mathcal{G}(T)
and
k
-scheme
T
, compatible with
\circ
and change of
T
. We note
\Rep(U\colon\mathcal{G})
(
[
44
, 1.7]
) the category of representations for which
V
is a vector bundle on
S
(assuming
S
is non-empty).

B.3.2.

By
[
44
, Theorem 1.12(ii)]
, the functor
\omega
induces an equivalence between
\mathcal{T}
and
\Rep(U\colon\mathcal{G})
.

Similarly, we get an equivalence between
\Ind\mathcal{T}
and
\Ind\Rep(U\colon\mathcal{G})
. In particular, if
Y
is a (group) scheme over
U
, to show that
Y
is
\omega
of a corresponding (group) scheme in
\mathcal{T}
(c.f.
[
48
, 5.4]
or
B.2
), it suffices to define an action
\rho
of
\mathcal{G}
on
Y
. Such an action means giving, for each
g\in\mathcal{G}(T)
for a
k
-scheme
T
, a (homo)morphism
\rho(g)\colon Y_{s(g)}\to Y_{b(g)}
, compatible with
\circ
and change of
T
.

B.4.
Background on stacks

We need some background on stacks (in particular, the gerbes associated to Tannakian categories) for the purposes of §
B.5
, where we discuss the base-change
\mathcal{T}_{S}
for a
k
-scheme
S
.

B.4.1.
Stacks

A
stack
refers to a stack for the fpqc topology, i.e., a
2
-sheaf of groupoids on the category of affine schemes with the fpqc topology. Following e.g.
[
113
, Notation]
, a
prestack
is just a
2
-presheaf of groupoids; i.e., a
(2,1)
-functor from the category of affine schemes to the
(2,1)
-category of groupoids. A scheme, via its functor of points, is a stack valued in discrete groupoids (i.e., those with no non-identity morphisms).

B.4.2.
Sheaves on stacks

A
quasi-coherent sheaf
on a prestack
\mathcal{X}
consists of a collection of functors
\mathcal{X}(S)\to\QCoh(S)
for each scheme
S
that are compatible (in a
2
-categorical rather than strict sense) with morphisms
S_{1}\to S_{2}
. A
vector bundle
on
\mathcal{X}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf for which each functor
\mathcal{X}(S)\to\QCoh(S)
lands in
\LF(S)
.

The category
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
(resp.
\LF(\mathcal{X})
) is the category of quasi-coherent sheaves (resp. vector bundles) on
\mathcal{X}
with an obvious notion of morphism; equivalently, it is the
2
-limit of the categories
\QCoh(S)
(resp.
\LF(S)
) over the opposite of the fibered category associated to
\mathcal{X}
.

The category
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
is an abelian tensor category. The subcategory
\LF(\mathcal{X})
is the full subcategory of dualizable objects; it is an additive tensor category but not usually abelian; but it has an exact structure inherited from that of
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
.

B.4.3.
Algebraic stacks

The term
algebraic stack
(
[
107
,
Tag 026O
]
) used throughout the Stacks Project and by most algebraic geometers is what we call an
fppf-algebraic
stack: it is a stack with representable, separated, and quasi-compact diagonal that has an fppf (equivalently, smooth) cover by a scheme.

We need the notion of
fpqc-algebraic
, which originally appeared in
[
63
, Example 1.8]
and appears with this name in
[
64
, Definition 2.29]
. It is a stack whose diagonal is representable, separated, and quasi-compact and which has an
fpqc
cover by a scheme.

B.4.4.
Presentations and groupoids

If
\mathcal{X}
is fpqc-algebraic and
U\to\mathcal{X}
is an fpqc covering by a scheme over a scheme
S
, then there is a groupoid in algebraic spaces over
S
(in the sense of §
B.3
or
[
107
,
Tag 0231
]
)
(R\coloneqq U\times_{\mathcal{X}}U,\circ,s,b)
acting on
U
with
s,b
fpqc such that
\mathcal{X}=[U/R]
(in the sense of
[
107
,
Tag 044Q
]
). Conversely, if
(R,\circ,s,b)
is a groupoid scheme over
S
acting on
U
for which
s
(equivalently
b
) is fpqc, then the quotient
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq[U/R]
is an fpqc-algebraic stack over
S
with fpqc covering by
U
. Such an
(R,\circ,s,b)
is known as a(n
fpqc) presentation
of
\mathcal{X}
.

Then
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
is the same as the category of representations of
(R,\circ,s,b)
as in
B.3.1
, and
\LF(\mathcal{X})\simeq\Rep(X\colon R)
.

B.4.5.
Gerbes and Tannakian categories

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a Tannakian category over
k
. Consider the functor
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
from
k
-schemes to groupoids sending
S
to the groupoid

\Fib{\mathcal{T}}(S)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T},\LF(S))

of fiber functors on
\mathcal{T}
with values in
S
. Then
\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}}
is an fpqc-algebraic stack (while it is fppf-algebraic iff
\mathcal{T}
is algebraic) over
k
.

More precisely, as stated in
[
97
]
and proved in
[
44
]
, it is an affine gerbe over
k
.

Given an object
M
of
\mathcal{T}
, we get a functor
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}(S)=\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T},\LF(S))\to\LF(S)
given by
\omega\mapsto\omega(M)
. These form an object of
\LF(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})
as in
B.4.2
, and, by varying
M\in\mathcal{T}
, this pieces together to a functor

\mathcal{T}\to\LF(\Fib{\mathcal{T}}).

Then the main theorem of Tannakian reconstruction says that this functor is an equivalence of
k
-linear tensor categories.

More formally, the associations
\mathcal{T}\mapsto\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathcal{G}\mapsto\LF(\mathcal{G})
give an equivalence between the
2
-category of Tannakian categories over
k
and exact tensor functors and the
2
-category of affine gerbes over
k
.

B.4.6.
Gerbes and groupoid schemes

If
\mathcal{T}
is neutral with fiber functor
\omega
, then
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}=BG
, where
G=\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega)
. Here,
BG\coloneqq[\operatorname{Spec}{k}/G]
(also denoted
TORS(G)
in
[
45
, II]
) is the classifying stack of the group scheme
G
, and
BG(S)
is the groupoid of
G
-torsors on
S
.

If
\omega
is a fiber functor over an affine
k
-scheme
U
, then we get a transitive
k
-groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\circ,s,b)
acting on
U
as in §
B.3
. The object
\omega\in\Fib{\mathcal{T}}(U)
is an fpqc cover of
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
by
U
, and
(\mathcal{G},\circ,s,b)
is the
k
-groupoid (for
S=\operatorname{Spec}{k}
) as in
B.4.4
.

Conversely, if we start with a transitive
k
-groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\circ,s,b)
acting on
U
, then
[U/\mathcal{G}]
is a gerbe equivalent to
\Fib{\Rep(U\colon\mathcal{G})}
.

B.5.
Base-change of Tannakian categories and fiber functors

Since we work with cohomology (Selmer)
schemes
in §
6.9
-
6.10
, we must allow ourselves to work in the base-change
\mathcal{T}_{S}
of
\mathcal{T}
to an arbitrary
k
-scheme
S
.

B.5.1.

As
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
is a gerbe over
\operatorname{Spec}{k}
, we can base-change it to a gerbe
(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}
over
S
. Then we define

\mathcal{T}_{S}\coloneqq\LF((\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}).

Pullback along the map
(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}\to\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}}
gives an exact tensor functor from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}_{S}
; for
M\in\mathcal{T}
, we write
M_{S}
for its image in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
.

Then
\mathcal{T}_{S}
is an
\mathcal{O}(S)
-linear category, but it is not generally abelian. More specifically, it is a rigid
\mathcal{O}(S)
-linear tensor category, and it has an exact structure inherited from
\QCoh(\Fib{\mathcal{T}}_{S})
for which
\otimes
is exact. C.f.
[
117
, §2.4]
and
[
100
, §10.2]
.

B.5.2.
Fiber functors

Given an
S
-scheme
S^{\prime}
and a map
f\colon S^{\prime}\to(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}
of stacks over
S
, we get a pullback functor
F_{\LF}(f)=f^{*}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}=\LF((\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})\to\LF(S^{\prime})
. This functor is an exact
\mathcal{O}(S)
-linear tensor functor. We refer to such a functor as an
S^{\prime}
-valued fiber functor on
\mathcal{T}_{S}
and denote the groupoid of such functors by
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T}_{S},\LF(S^{\prime}))
.

B.5.3.
Base change of fiber functors

If
\omega=\omega_{X/k}
is a fiber functor over a
k
-scheme
X
, it corresponds to a map
X\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
of stacks over
k
.

We can base-change to get a map
X\times_{k}S\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}\times_{k}S=(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{S}
of stacks over
S
, hence an
\mathcal{O}(S)
-linear pullback functor

\omega_{S_{X}/S}\colon\mathcal{T}_{S}\to\LF(S_{X}),

where
S_{X}\coloneqq S\times_{k}X
.

If
X
starts out as an
S
-scheme, we can pull back along
X\to S_{X}
to get an
X
-valued fiber functor
\omega_{X/S}
on
\mathcal{T}_{S}
. Alternatively, we can use the base-change adjunction
\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(X,\Fib{\mathcal{T}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(X,(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{S})
, and
\omega_{X/S}
is the object on the right corresponding to
\omega
on the left.

Similarly, if
S
starts out as a
X
-scheme over
k
, we can compose
S\to X\to\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
to get an
S
-valued fiber functor
\omega_{S}=\omega_{S/k}
on
\mathcal{T}
, then apply the previous sentence to get an
S
-valued fiber functor
\omega_{S/S}
on
\mathcal{T}_{S}
. Alternatively, the map
S\to X
over
k
induces
S\to S_{X}
over
S
, and pulling back
\omega_{S_{X}/S}
along this gives
\omega_{S/S}
.

B.5.4.

If
S^{\prime}\to(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{S}
is fpqc, then by
B.4.4
, we get a groupoid in
S
-schemes
(S^{\prime},R,s,t,c)
and an identification
\mathcal{T}_{S}\equiv\operatorname{Rep}(S^{\prime},R,s,t,c)
. The pullback
\mathcal{T}_{S}=\LF((\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{S})\to\LF(S^{\prime})
is a fiber functor on
\mathcal{T}_{S}
corresponding to forgetting the groupoid action.

B.5.5.

In §
6.5
, we discuss base-change of graded and filtered fiber functors.

B.5.6.
Tannakian reconstruction

By definition of
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
, for any
k
-scheme
X
, we have

\Fib{\mathcal{T}}(X)=\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T},\LF(X)).

The content in the theory of Tannaka duality is that this is always an affine gerbe when
\mathcal{T}
is a Tannakian category, and that if
\mathcal{T}
is the category of representations of an affine group (resp. groupoid) scheme, then
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}
is representable by the corresponding classifying (resp. quotient) stack.

Since the above equality is of groupoids and not just sets, this says in particular that the automorphism group of a given fiber functor is representable (as
X
varies) by a group scheme, and furthermore this group scheme is
G
if
\mathcal{T}=\Rep(G)
.

Let us replace
\mathcal{T}
by
\mathcal{T}_{S}
for a
k
-scheme
S
and
X
by an arbitrary prestack
\mathcal{X}
of groupoids on
S
schemes. We have a priori different groupoids

(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(\mathcal{X},(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{X},\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})\eqqcolon\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{X})

and

(\Fib{\mathcal{T}_{S}})(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T}_{S},\LF(\mathcal{X}))

as well as a natural functor

(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{F_{\LF}}(\Fib{\mathcal{T}_{S}})(\mathcal{X}).

B.5.7.
Remark on terminology

In
[
113
, A.21,A.23]
, for
G
a group scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{T}=\Rep(G)
(i.e., if
\mathcal{T}
is neutral), an object of
(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}(\mathcal{X})
is known as a
G
-bundle over
\mathcal{X}
, while an object of
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T}_{S},\LF(\mathcal{X}))
is known as a
Tannakian
G
-bundle over
\mathcal{X}
.

Theorem B.5.8
.

If
S
is affine, then the functor
F_{\LF}
is an equivalence of groupoids.

B.5.9.
Proof

An affine groupoid scheme
(R,\circ,s,b)
over
S
acting on
U
is the same as a
Hopf algebroid

(A,\Gamma)
, where
A=\mathcal{O}(U)
and
\Gamma=\mathcal{O}(R)
, and a representation of
(R,\circ,s,b)
is the same as a comodule over
(A,\Gamma)
.

We recall (
[
62
, Definition 3.1]
,
[
75
, Definition 1.4.3]
) that a Hopf algebroid
(A,\Gamma)
is an
Adams Hopf algebroid
if
\Gamma
is flat over
A
and if
\Gamma
, viewed as a comodule over
(A,\Gamma)
, is a filtered system
\{\Gamma_{\alpha}\}
of comodules for which
\Gamma_{\alpha}
is finitely generated and projective over
A
. An fpqc-algebraic stack is an
Adams stack
(
[
64
, Definition 6.5(2)]
,
[
99
, §1.3]
) if the associated Hopf algebroid for some fpqc presentation (as in
B.4.4
) is an Adams Hopf algebroid.

Choose a fiber functor
\omega
of
\mathcal{T}
over an extension
k^{\prime}/k
. As in
B.4.6
, we get a transitive
k
-groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\circ,s,b)
acting on
U=\operatorname{Spec}{k^{\prime}}
, so in the associated Hopf algebroid,
A=k^{\prime}
(while
\Gamma=\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{G})
). By
[
44
, Corollaire 3.9]
, every representation of
\mathcal{G}
is a filtered union of subrepresentations that are locally free of finite rank over
A
. In particular, this is true of
\Gamma
, so
(A,\Gamma)
is an Adams Hopf algebroid, and
\Fib{\mathcal{T}}=[U/\mathcal{G}]
is an Adams stack.

An affine scheme
S=\operatorname{Spec}{R}
is obviously an Adams stack (it is presented by a Hopf algebroid with
A=\Gamma=R
, so
\Gamma
is free of finite rank already). It follows by
[
101
, Theorem 1.8]
that
(\Fib{\mathcal{T}})_{S}=\Fib{\mathcal{T}}\times_{k}S
is also an Adams stack.

The result then follows as in the proof of
[
113
, Proposition A.30]
.
38
38

38

That is, if
\mathcal{X}
is an Adams stack, then
(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}(\mathcal{X})\xrightarrow{F_{\QCoh}}\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}(\QCoh((\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}),\QCoh(\mathcal{X}))
induces an equivalence of
(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}(\mathcal{X})
with
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{tame}}(\QCoh((\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}),\QCoh(\mathcal{X}))
by
[
99
, 1.2.1]
, which is the same as
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{cocont}}(\QCoh((\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S}),\QCoh(\mathcal{X}))
by
[
99
, 1.3.2]
.
Applying
[
102
, Theorem 3.4.2]
to
\mathscr{A}=\mathcal{T}_{S}\coloneqq\LF(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})\subseteq\QCoh(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})
and
\mathscr{D}=\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
, this is equivalent to
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{r-ex}}(\mathcal{T}_{S},\QCoh(\mathcal{X}))
via restriction along
\LF(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})\subseteq\QCoh(\operatorname{Fib}{\mathcal{T}})_{S})
by
[
102
, 3.4.3]
. Since tensor functors respect duals (
[
44
, 2.7]
), this is precisely
\operatorname{Hom}^{\otimes}_{\mathrm{ex}}(\mathcal{T}_{S},\LF(\mathcal{X}))=\Fib{\mathcal{T}_{S}}(\mathcal{X})
, so we are done in this case. The case of general
\mathcal{X}
follows by the same colimit argument.

B.5.10.

We can mimic
B.2
to define affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
. The category
\Ind{\mathcal{T}_{S}}
inherits a symmetric monoidal structure, and a commutative algebra in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}_{S}}
is just a commutative monoid object. The category
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
of affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
is defined to be the opposite of the category of commutative algebras in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
; the corresponding object in
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
is called the
spectrum
of the given algebra in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}}
.

A group scheme in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
is just a group object in
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
; equivalently, it is the spectrum of a Hopf algebra object in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}_{S}}
.

Following the notation of
B.5.1
, if
X
is an affine scheme in
\mathcal{T}
, we write
X_{S}
for the affine scheme in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
given by
\operatorname{Spec}{\mathcal{O}(X)_{S}}
. If
\pi
is a group scheme in
\mathcal{T}
, we similarly write
\pi_{S}
for the corresponding group scheme in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
.

B.5.11.

Let
Z\in\operatorname{Aff}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
and
\mathcal{A}
its coordinate algebra in
\Ind{\mathcal{T}_{S}}
. By taking its inductive limit,
\mathcal{A}
gives rise to an algebra in
\QCoh(\Fib(\mathcal{T})_{S})
. We can take relative Spec (
[
94
, 10.2.1]
) to get a stack
\underline{\operatorname{Spec}}_{\Fib(\mathcal{T})_{S}}(\mathcal{A})
with a morphism
\underline{\operatorname{Spec}}_{\Fib(\mathcal{T})_{S}}(\mathcal{A})\to\Fib(\mathcal{T})_{S}
that is affine by
[
94
, Theorem 10.2.4]
.

Thus
\operatorname{Aff}(\mathcal{T}_{S})
is equivalent to the full subcategory of the category of affine morphisms of stacks
\pi\colon\mathscr{Y}\to\Fib(\mathcal{T})_{S}
for which
\pi
is locally free. If
S
is the spectrum of a field, then this latter condition is always satisfied.

B.5.12.

If
\pi
is a group scheme in
\mathcal{T}
, then for a
k
-scheme
S
, we denote by
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(S)
the pointed set of isomorphism classes of right
\pi_{S}
-torsors in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
; that is, a (right)
\pi_{S}
-torsor is a nonempty affine scheme
P
in
\mathcal{T}_{S}
with a (right) group action map
P\times\pi_{S}\to P
in schemes over
\mathcal{T}_{S}
for which
P\times\pi_{S}\to P\times P
is an isomorphism.

If
\omega_{S}
is a fiber functor over
S
, then we have a bijection

H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(S)\simeq H^{1}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{S});\omega_{S}(\pi)),

where the latter is defined by algebraic cocycles modulo algebraic coboundaries.

If
M
is an object of
\mathcal{T}
and
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we denote by
H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(S)
the group
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}}(\mathbbm{1},M_{S})
. If
\omega_{S}
is a fiber functor over
S
, then we have a bijection

H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(S)\simeq H^{i}(\pi_{1}(\mathcal{T},\omega_{S});\omega_{S}(M)).

If
R
is a
k
-algebra, then
H^{1}(\mathcal{T};\pi)(R)
(resp.
H^{i}(\mathcal{T};M)(R)
) denotes the above for
S=\operatorname{Spec}{R}
.
